for the non-spiked model and
i_{0}=r+1
for the spiked model. Here,
\xi\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}[\![{i_{0},\mathsf{K}}]\!]\to\mathbb{N}
represents a fixed configuration, and we can interpret
\xi(j)
as the number of particles at site
j
. This interpretation allows us to view the EMF as an interacting particle system later.
The factor
(2\xi(k)-1)!!
corresponds precisely to the
\xi(k)
-th moment of a standard Gaussian random variable, and we adopt the convention that
(-1)!!=1
. Clearly,
f_{t}(\xi)
is a functional encoding joint moments of the generalized components of the non-outlier eigenvectors.

Before delving into the analysis of
f_{t}(\xi),
let us provide some heuristics as to why the asymptotic variance takes the form presented in (
4.6
). For simplicity, we consider the single-particle case where
\xi=\delta_{k_{0}}
for some
k_{0}\in[\![{i_{0},\mathsf{K}}]\!]
. In this scenario, we have
f_{t}(\xi)=\mathbb{E}\left(|z_{k_{0}}(t)|^{2}\big|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right).
For
z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
, using the spectral decomposition of
W(t)W(t)^{\top}
, we find that on the local scale of order
\eta,

\frac{p\im\big({\bf{v}}^{\top}\left(W(t)W(t)^{\top}-z\right)^{-1}{\bf{v}}\big)}{\im\tr\left(W(t)W(t)^{\top}-z\right)^{-1}}\approx\text{local average of }|z_{k}(t)|^{2}\text{ for }{\lambda_{k}(t)-E=\OO(\eta)}.

(4.13)

Drawing inspiration from the results for Wigner matrices
[
15
]
, we expect that the random variables
z_{k}(t)
are asymptotically independent, and that the distributions of
z_{k}(t)
and
z_{k^{\prime}}(t)
are close to each other when
|k-k^{\prime}|\ll n
. Guided by this intuition, we expect that that
f_{t}(\xi)
should closely resemble the averaged quantity in (
4.13
) due to the law of large numbers, as long as we choose
n^{-1}\ll\eta\ll 1
.

Next, we show that the left-hand side (LHS) of (
4.13
) can be effectively approximated by
\varphi_{0}
as defined in (
4.5
). First, using the local laws presented in
Lemma

B.9
and
Remark

B.10
below, the LHS of (
4.13
) has a deterministic limit, denoted as

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}({\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},z)\equiv\varphi({\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},z,D_{t}):=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{\im\big({\bf{v}}^{\top}[z(1+m(z,D_{t0})D_{t})]^{-1}{\bf{v}}\big)}{|z|^{-2}\eta(1-c_{n}^{-1})+c_{n}^{-1}\im m(z,D_{t0})},

(4.14)

for
z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta
, where
D_{t0}:=D_{00}+t
denotes the non-spiked part of
D_{t}
.
By setting
t=0
,
E=x
, and
\eta\downarrow 0
, we obtain the formula in (
4.5
) with
{\bf{w}}={\bf{v}}
. (This is also why we use the same notation
\varphi
in (
4.5
) and (
4.14
).)
Moreover, by the eigenvalue rigidity property of
W(t)W(t)^{\top}
stated in Lemma
B.9
below, we know that
\lambda_{k}(t)
is well-approximated by
\gamma_{k}(t)
with high probability. Therefore, by choosing
E=\gamma_{k_{0}}(t)
and
\eta=\oo(1)
, according to the above discussion, we expect that when
t=\oo(1)
,

\mathbb{E}|z_{k_{0}}(t)|^{2}\approx\varphi_{t}({\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},\gamma_{k_{0}}(t)+\mathrm{i}\eta)\approx\varphi_{t}({\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},\gamma_{k_{0}}(t))\approx\varphi_{0}({\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},\gamma_{k_{0}}(t)).

This gives the desired result for the single-particle case.
For general configuration, using the above argument we heuristically expect that

f_{t}(\xi)\approx\prod_{k=i_{0}}^{\mathsf{K}}\varphi_{0}({\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},\gamma_{k}(t))^{\xi(k)}.

(4.15)

In general, establishing the asymptotic distribution of
\{|z_{k}(t)|^{2}\}_{k=i_{0}}^{\mathsf{K}}
can be accomplished by demonstrating that its finite-dimensional moments asymptotically match those of a random vector composed of independent Chi-squared random variables with the desired variances.
To make the above heuristics rigorous, we will work with the following smoothed version of
\varphi_{0}(\cdot)
in the actual proof.

Definition 4.3
.

Given the deterministic unit vector
{\bf{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, let
\varphi_{0}
be defined as in (
4.5
). Let
s(x)
be a smooth, non-negative function supported in
[-1,1]
such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}s(x)dx=1
. For any constant
\varepsilon>0
, we define
s_{\varepsilon}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=n^{\varepsilon}s(n^{\varepsilon}x)
and the function
\phi(x,\varepsilon)
as the convolution

\phi(x,\varepsilon):=s_{\varepsilon}*\varphi_{0}({\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},x).

(4.16)

Then, for any fixed configuration
\xi:[\![{i_{0},p}]\!]\mapsto\mathbb{N}
, we define

g_{t}(\xi,\varepsilon)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\prod_{k=i_{0}}^{\mathsf{K}}\phi(\lambda_{k}(t),\varepsilon)^{\xi(k)}.

(4.17)

Remark 4.4
.

We remark that the function
g_{t}
is defined in terms of
\varphi_{0}
and
\lambda_{k}(t)
rather than
\varphi_{t}
and
\gamma_{k}(t)
. The usage of
\lambda_{k}(t)
instead of
\gamma_{k}(t)
is due to certain technical considerations, which will become apparent in the technical derivation (
E.40
) below.
Using the definition of
\phi
and the estimate (
B.18
) in the appendix, it can be readily observed that for any small constant
\tau>0
,

\left|\partial_{x}^{s}\phi^{ij}(x,{\varepsilon})\right|=\OO(n^{s{\varepsilon}}),\quad\forall\ \ s\in\{0,1,2\},\quad x\in[\tau,\tau^{-1}].

(4.18)

The following EMF result shows that
f_{t}(\xi)
converges to the equilibrium
g_{t}(\xi,{\varepsilon})
at a time scale of
n^{-1/3+\mathsf{c}}
as long as we choose
{\varepsilon}
sufficiently small.

Theorem 4.5
.

Under the setting of Lemma
4.1
, choose
T_{i}=C_{i}n^{-1/3+\mathsf{c}}
for some constants
0<C_{1}<C_{2}
and
\mathsf{c}\in(0,1/6)
.
Then, for any fixed
L\in\mathbb{N}
and
\nu>0
, there exist (small) positive constants
{\varepsilon}_{0},{\varepsilon}_{1}>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{T_{1}\leq t\leq T_{2}}\max_{|\xi|=L}\left|f_{t}(\xi)-g_{t}(\xi,{\varepsilon}_{1})\right|\geq n^{-{\varepsilon}_{0}}\right)\leq n^{-\nu},

where
|\xi|:=\sum_{j=i_{0}}^{\mathsf{K}}\xi(j)
denotes the total number of particles.

Lemma 4.6
.

Under the setting of Lemma
4.1
, take
n^{-1/3+{\varepsilon}}\leq t\leq n^{-1/6-{\varepsilon}}
for an arbitrary constant
{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/12)
. Then, for any fixed
m\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist (small) positive constants
{\varepsilon}_{0},{\varepsilon}_{1},\nu>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{|\xi|=m}\Big|g_{t}(\xi,{\varepsilon}_{1})-\prod_{k=i_{0}}^{\mathsf{K}}\varphi_{0}({\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},\gamma_{k}(t))^{\xi(k)}\Big|\geq n^{-{\varepsilon}_{0}}\right)\leq n^{-\nu}.

Proof.

The function
g_{t}(\xi,{\varepsilon}_{1})
represents a smoothed version of the product
\prod_{k=i_{0}}^{\mathsf{K}}\varphi_{0}({\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},\lambda_{k}(t))^{\xi(k)}
.
The proof then follows from the standard fact concerning convolutions with approximate identities and the rigidity of
\lambda_{k}(t)
, as stated in Lemma
B.9
below (specifically, refer to (
B.37
)).
∎

We are now ready to prove Lemma
4.1
by combining the results in
Theorem

4.5
and Lemma
4.6
.

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

Let
\xi
be a configuration supported on
\{i_{k}+r\}_{k=1}^{L}\subset[\![{r+1,\mathsf{K}}]\!]
. Note that the functional
f_{t}(\xi)
in (
4.12
) encodes the joint moments of the random variables
p|\langle{\bf{v}},{\bf{u}}_{i_{1}+r}(t)\rangle|^{2},\ldots,p|\langle{\bf{v}},{\bf{u}}_{i_{L}+r}(t)\rangle|^{2}
. By employing Theorem
4.5
and Lemma
4.6
, we establish that these joint moments match the corresponding finite-dimensional joint moments of
|\mathcal{N}_{1}|^{2},\ldots,|\mathcal{N}_{L}|^{2},
where
\mathcal{N}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{N}_{L}
are i.i.d. centered Gaussian random variables having the desired variances
\varphi_{0}({\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},\gamma_{i_{1}}(t)),\ldots,\varphi_{0}({\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},\gamma_{i_{L}}(t))
. This concludes the proof using the standard method of moments.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

In
Theorem

4.5
, we have chosen
t\asymp n^{-1/3+\mathsf{c}}
, which yields an almost sharp speed of convergence to local equilibrium for the edge eigenvalues and eigenvectors (see e.g.,
[
15
,
48
,
31
]
). However, this rate is suboptimal for the convergence of bulk eigenvalues and eigenvectors. Indeed, it has been demonstrated in
[
15
,
10
]
that the bulk eigenvectors of Wigner matrices converge to local equilibrium once
t\gg n^{-1}
, and we anticipate that similar results extend to sample covariance matrices as well. Since this aspect is not the primary focus of the current paper, we will refrain from delving into this direction here.

The proof of
Theorem

4.5
can be found in Appendix
E
. The main ingredient is a probabilistic description of the EMF of
f_{t}(\xi)
as a multi-particle random walk in a random environment. The random environment is described by the well-studied Dyson Brownian motion of the eigenvalues. Let
\mathsf{B}(t)=(\mathsf{B}_{ij}(t))
be a
p\times n
matrix where
\mathsf{B}_{ij}
,
i\in[\![{p}]\!]
and
j\in[\![{n}]\!]
, are independent standard Brownian motions. For any
t\geq 0
,
W(t)
in (
4.3
) can be rewritten as

W(t)=W(0)+n^{-1/2}\mathsf{B}_{t}.

(4.19)

We refer to this type of dynamics as a rectangular DBM. Under (
4.19
),
Lemma

4.9
provides the stochastic differential equations (SDEs) that describe the evolution of eigenvalues and eigenvectors of
Q(t)
.
Note that
\lambda_{k}(t)\equiv 0
when
k\in[\![{\mathsf{K}+1,p}]\!]
. To account for this, we introduce the following equivalence relation.

Definition 4.8

(Equivalence relation)
.

We define the following equivalence relation on
[\![{1,p}]\!]
:
k\sim l
if and only if
k=l
or
k,l\geqslant\mathsf{K}+1
. In particular, when
p\leqslant n
,
k\sim l
simply means
k=l
.

Lemma 4.9
.

The eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{k}(t)\}_{k=1}^{p}
and eigenvectors
\{{\bf{u}}_{k}(t)\}_{k=1}^{p}
of
Q(t)
satisfy the following SDEs:

\mathrm{d}\lambda_{k}(t)\;=\;2\sqrt{\lambda_{k}(t)}\frac{\mathrm{d}B_{kk}(t)}{\sqrt{n}}+\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{l:l\not\sim k}\frac{\lambda_{k}(t)+\lambda_{l}(t)}{\lambda_{k}(t)-\lambda_{l}(t)}\bigg)\mathrm{d}t,

(4.20)

\mathrm{d}\bm{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(t)\;=\;\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{l:l\not\sim k}\frac{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}(t)}\ \mathrm{d}B_{lk}(t)+\sqrt{\lambda_{l}(t)}\,\mathrm{d}B_{kl}(t)}{\lambda_{k}(t)-\lambda_{l}(t)}\,\bm{\mathrm{u}}_{l}(t)-\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{l:l\not\sim k}\frac{\lambda_{k}(t)+\lambda_{l}(t)}{(\lambda_{k}(t)-\lambda_{l}(t))^{2}}\,\bm{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,,

(4.21)

where
k\in[\![{p}]\!]
and
B_{ij}
,
i,j\in[\![{p}]\!]
, are independent standard Brownian motions. (Note that
B
has the same distribution as
\mathsf{B}
, although they generally correspond to different matrix Brownian motions.) We will refer to (
4.20
) and (
4.21
) as DBM and eigenvector flow, respectively. Their initial conditions at
t=0
are given by
\{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{p}
and
\{{\bf{u}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{p}
.

Proof.

See
[
17
]
for the derivations. Here, we adopt the formulation presented in
[
15
, Appendix C]
.
∎

We extend the definition (
4.12
) to

f_{t}(\xi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\mathbb{E}\Biggl({\prod_{k=1}^{p}z_{k}(t)^{2\xi(k)}\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{t}}\Biggr)\prod_{k=1}^{p}\frac{1}{(2\xi(k)-1)!!}\,

(4.22)

for the particle configuration
\xi:[\![{p}]\!]\to\mathbb{N}
. For
\xi=(\xi(1),\ldots,\xi(p))
, we introduce the notation

\xi^{k\to l}\;\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\;\xi+\bm{\mathrm{1}}(\xi(k)\geqslant 1)(\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{l}-\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k}),

(4.23)

which denotes the particle configuration obtained by moving one particle from site
k
to site
l
, given that
\xi(k)\geq 1
. The following lemma defines the main object of study—the eigenvector moment flow. Its proof will be presented in Appendix
D
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Suppose the SDEs (
4.20
) and (
4.21
) in Lemma
4.9
hold. Let
f_{t}(\xi)
be defined as in (
4.22
). Define the generator
\mathscr{B}(t)
as

\mathscr{B}(t)f(\xi)\;\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\;\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l\not\sim k}\Upsilon_{kl}(t)\,2\xi(k)(1+2\xi(l))\bigl({f(\xi^{k\to l})-f(\xi)}\bigr),

(4.24)

where the matrix
\Upsilon
is defined as

\Upsilon_{kl}(t)\;\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\;\bm{\mathrm{1}}(k\not\sim l)\frac{\lambda_{k}(t)+\lambda_{l}(t)}{2n(\lambda_{k}(t)-\lambda_{l}(t))^{2}}\,.

(4.25)

Then,
f_{t}(\xi)
satisfies the equation

\partial_{t}f_{t}(\xi)\;=\;\mathscr{B}(t)f_{t}(\xi)\,.

(4.26)

The proof of Theorem
4.5
is based on a careful analysis of the equation (
4.26
). For more details, readers can refer to Appendix
E
.

4.3
Green’s function comparison and proof of Lemma
4.2

As discussed below
Lemma

4.2
, in order to complete the proof, it suffices to prove the comparison estimate (
4.9
). Inspired by the works
[
13
,
26
,
44
]
, we first establish a functional integral representation formula for the generalized components of the non-outlier eigenvectors, expressed in terms of the resolvents of either the non-spiked or spiked model.

We first introduce some new notations. For any
E_{2}\geq E_{1}>0,
let
\mathfrak{f}_{E_{1},E_{2},\eta}(x)
denote an indicator function of the interval
[E_{1},E_{2}]
smoothed on the scale
\eta.
More precisely,
\mathfrak{f}_{E_{1},E_{2},\eta}
is a smooth function satisfying the following properties: (1)
\mathfrak{f}_{E_{1},E_{2},\eta}(x)\equiv 1
for
x\in[E_{1},E_{2}]
, (2)
\mathfrak{f}(x)\equiv 0
for
x\notin[E_{1}-\eta,E_{2}+\eta]
, and (3)

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\mathfrak{f}_{E_{1},E_{2},\eta}^{\prime}(x)|\lesssim\eta^{-1},\qquad\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\mathfrak{f}_{E_{1},E_{2},\eta}^{\prime\prime}(x)|\lesssim\eta^{-2}.

(4.27)

For any positive integer
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathfrak{q}_{k}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
be a smooth cutoff function such that

\mathfrak{q}_{k}(x)\equiv 1,\ \ \text{if}\ |x-k|\leqslant\frac{1}{3};\qquad\mathfrak{q}_{k}(x)\equiv 0,\ \ \text{if}\ |x-k|\geqslant\frac{2}{3};\qquad\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\mathfrak{q}_{k}^{\prime}(x)|\lesssim 1.

(4.28)

For any
k\in[\![{r+1,\mathsf{K}}]\!]
, if
\gamma_{k}\in[a_{2j},a_{2j-1}]
, then we define

\Delta_{k}:=[(n_{j}+1-k)\wedge(k-n_{j-1})]^{-1/3}n^{-{2}/{3}},

(4.29)

where recall that
n_{j}
is defined in (
2.11
) and the choice of
\Delta_{k}
is due to the rigidity of eigenvalues in (
B.37
) below. Now, for any constants
\varepsilon,\delta_{1},\delta_{2}>0
and
E\in\mathbb{R}
, we define

E^{-}_{k}({\varepsilon},\delta_{1}):=E-n^{-2\varepsilon+\delta_{1}}\Delta_{k},\quad E^{+}({\varepsilon})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\lambda_{+}+2n^{-2/3+\varepsilon},\quad\eta_{k}({\varepsilon}):=\Delta_{k}n^{-2\varepsilon},\quad\widetilde{\eta}_{k}({\varepsilon}):=\Delta_{k}n^{-3\varepsilon},

(4.30)

and the intervals (recall that
\gamma_{k}(t)
are defined above (
4.5
))

I_{k}\equiv I_{k}(t,\delta_{2})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=[\gamma_{k}(t)-n^{\delta_{2}}\Delta_{k},\gamma_{k}(t)+n^{\delta_{2}}\Delta_{k}].

(4.31)

Corresponding to the function
\mathfrak{f}_{E_{1},E_{2},\eta}
defined above, we abbreviate

\mathfrak{f}_{k}(x)\equiv\mathfrak{f}_{k}(x,{\varepsilon},\delta_{1}):=\mathfrak{f}_{E_{k}^{-}({\varepsilon},\delta_{1}),E^{+}({\varepsilon}),\widetilde{\eta}_{k}({\varepsilon})}(x).

(4.32)

Finally, for any
E\in\mathbb{R}
, we define

x_{k}^{1}(E,t):={\bf{v}}^{\top}(Q(t)-E-\mathrm{i}\eta_{k})^{-1}{\bf{v}},\quad x_{k}^{0}(E,t):={\bf{v}}^{\top}(\mathcal{Q}_{t}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta_{k})^{-1}{\bf{v}},

(4.33)

where recall that
Q(t)
and
\mathcal{Q}_{t}
were defined in (
4.4
) and (
4.8
), respectively.

Now, we are ready to state the key functional representation formula for the generalized components of non-outlier eigenvectors. The proof of this formula will be presented in Appendix
F.1
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Under the setting of Lemma
4.2
, for any
t\asymp n^{-1/3+\mathsf{c}}
, there exists a small constant
{\varepsilon}>0
such that the following estimate holds for sufficiently small constants
\delta_{1}\equiv\delta_{1}({\varepsilon})
,
\delta_{2}\equiv\delta_{2}({\varepsilon},\delta_{1})
, and
\nu>0
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\theta\left(p|\langle{\bf{v}},{\bf{u}}_{i_{1}+r}(t)\rangle|^{2},\ldots,p|\langle{\bf{v}},{\bf{u}}_{i_{L}+r}(t)\rangle|^{2}\right)=\mathbb{E}\theta\Big(

\displaystyle\frac{p}{\pi}\int_{I_{i_{1}}}\left[\im x^{1}_{i_{1}}(E,t)\right]\mathfrak{q}_{i_{1}}\left[\tr\mathfrak{f}_{i_{1}}(Q(t))\right]\mathrm{d}E,\ldots,

\displaystyle\frac{p}{\pi}\int_{I_{i_{L}}}\left[\im x^{1}_{i_{L}}(E,t)\right]\mathfrak{q}_{i_{L}}\left[\tr\mathfrak{f}_{i_{L}}(Q(t))\right]\mathrm{d}E\Big)+\OO(n^{-\nu}).

A similar estimate holds for
\{p|\langle{\bf{v}},{\bf{w}}_{i_{k}+r}(t)\rangle|^{2}\}_{k=1}^{L}
,
\{x^{0}_{i_{k}}(E,t)\}_{k=1}^{L}
, and
\mathcal{Q}_{t}
.

With
Lemma

4.11
, proving (
4.9
) boils down to showing that the two representation formulas involving the resolvents of
Q(t)
and
\mathcal{Q}_{t}
are asymptotically equal to each other. As discussed below (
4.9
), this is achieved through a novel comparison argument based on the interpolation (
4.10
). Correspondingly, we define the family of interpolating sample covariance matrices as

\mathsf{Q}_{t}^{s}=W_{t}^{s}(W_{t}^{s})^{\top},\quad\text{with}\quad\mathsf{Q}^{1}_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}Q(t),\quad\mathsf{Q}^{0}_{t}=\mathcal{Q}_{t}.

(4.34)

Here, “
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}
” means “equal in distribution”.
Let
x^{s}_{k}(E,t)
be defined as in (
4.33
) using
\mathsf{Q}_{t}^{s}.
The following lemma provides the Green’s function comparison result, which, together with
Lemma

4.11
, concludes the proof of (
4.9
).

Lemma 4.12
.

Under the setting of Lemma
4.2
, define
W_{t}^{s}
as in (
4.10
) for some semi-orthogonal matrices
{U}^{0},U^{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times 2p}
. Suppose there exist constants
C,\mathsf{c}^{\prime}>0
such that

\big[(U^{0}-U^{1})^{\top}(U^{0}-U^{1})\big]^{1/2}_{jj}\leq Cn^{-\mathsf{c}^{\prime}},\quad j\in[\![{2p}]\!].

(4.35)

Then, there exists a constant
\nu>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\theta\left(\mathcal{U}_{i_{1}}(\mathsf{Q}_{t}^{0}),\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{i_{L}}(\mathsf{Q}_{t}^{0})\right)=\mathbb{E}\theta\left(\mathcal{U}_{i_{1}}(\mathsf{Q}_{t}^{1}),\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{i_{L}}(\mathsf{Q}_{t}^{1})\right)+\OO(n^{-\nu}),

(4.36)

where we used the simplified notation

\mathcal{U}_{k}(\mathsf{Q}_{t}^{s}):=\frac{p}{\pi}\int_{I_{k}}\left[\im x^{s}_{k}(E,t)\right]\mathfrak{q}_{k}\left(\tr\mathfrak{f}_{k}(\mathsf{Q}_{t}^{s})\right)\mathrm{d}E,\quad k\in[\![{r+1,\mathsf{K}}]\!].

To establish (
4.36
), we will control the derivative of
\mathbb{E}\theta\left(\mathcal{U}_{i_{1}+r}(\mathsf{Q}_{t}^{s}),\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{i_{L}+r}(\mathsf{Q}_{t}^{s})\right)
with respect to
s
. More details can be found in Appendix
F.2
.
Now, we are ready to conclude the proof of Lemma
4.2
by combining the above two lemmas.

Proof of Lemma
4.2
.

When
t\asymp n^{-1/3+\mathsf{c}}
, it is straightforward to check that the condition (
4.35
) holds with
c^{\prime}=1/3-\mathsf{c}
for
U^{0}
and
U^{1}
defined below (
4.10
). Then, by applying
Lemma

4.11
and
Lemma

4.12
, we obtain (
4.9
), which further concludes the proof of
Lemma

4.2
by setting
t=\mathfrak{t}
and
D_{0}=\Lambda-\mathfrak{t}
.
∎

Appendix A
Summary of loss functions and shrinkers

According to
[
32
,
52
]
, some special loss functions have been used frequently in the literature owing to their significance in applications. For the convenience of readers, we list them in Table
1
. Hereafter, we consistently denote the loss functions by
\mathcal{L}(\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
.

Loss function

{\bf\mathcal{L}(\Sigma,\widehat{\Sigma})}

Loss function

{\bf\mathcal{L}(\Sigma,\widehat{\Sigma})}

Frobenius

\|\Sigma-\widehat{\Sigma}\|_{F}/\sqrt{p}

Inverse Quadratic

\|\widehat{\Sigma}^{-1}\Sigma-I\|_{F}/\sqrt{p}

Disutility

\tr[(\widehat{\Sigma}^{-1}-\Sigma^{-1})^{2}\Sigma]/\tr(\Sigma^{-1})

Quadratic

\|\Sigma^{-1}\widehat{\Sigma}-I\|_{F}/\sqrt{p}

Inverse Stein

\left(\tr[\widehat{\Sigma}^{-1}\Sigma-I]-\log[\det(\widehat{\Sigma}^{-1}\Sigma)]\right)/p

Fréchet

\|\widehat{\Sigma}^{1/2}-\Sigma^{1/2}\|_{F}/\sqrt{p}

Minimum Variance

p\tr[\widehat{\Sigma}^{-1}\Sigma\widehat{\Sigma}^{-1}]\Big/\left(\tr[\widehat{\Sigma}^{-1}]\right)^{2}-p/\tr(\Sigma^{-1})

Log-Euclidean

\|\log(\widehat{\Sigma})-\log(\Sigma)\|_{F}/\sqrt{p}

Stein

\left(\tr[\widehat{\Sigma}\Sigma^{-1}-I]-\log[\det(\widehat{\Sigma}\Sigma^{-1})]\right)/p

Symmetrized Stein

\tr[\widehat{\Sigma}\Sigma^{-1}+\widehat{\Sigma}^{-1}\Sigma-2I]/p

Weighted Frobenius

\tr[(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)^{2}\Sigma^{-1}]/\tr(\Sigma)

Inverse Frobenius

\|\Sigma^{-1}-\widehat{\Sigma}^{-1}\|_{F}/\sqrt{p}

Table 1
:
Summary of commonly used loss functions for the population covariance matrix
\Sigma
and its estimator
\widehat{\Sigma}
.

For the loss functions considered in
Table

1
, the analytical forms of the shrinkers
\varphi_{i}
can be computed explicitly. We summarize them in Lemma
A.1
below.

Lemma A.1
.

For the loss functions in Table
1
, the optimal shrinkers
\varphi_{i}
,
i\in[\![{\mathsf{K}}]\!]
, are given as follows.

(i)

For the Frobenius, inverse Stein, disutility, and minimum variance norms, we have that
\varphi_{i}=\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\Sigma\bm{u}_{i}.

(ii)

For Stein, weighted Frobenius, and inverse Frobenius norms, we have that
\varphi_{i}=(\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\bm{u}_{i})^{-1}.

(iii)

For the symmetrized Stein norm, we have that
\varphi_{i}=\sqrt{\frac{\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\Sigma\bm{u}_{i}}{\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\bm{u}_{i}}}.

(iv)

For the Log-Euclidean norm, we have that
\varphi_{i}=\exp(\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\log(\Sigma)\bm{u}_{i}).

(v)

For the Fréchet norm, we have that
\varphi_{i}=(\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\Sigma^{1/2}\bm{u}_{i})^{2}.

(vi)

For the quadratic norm, we have that
\varphi_{i}=\frac{\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\bm{u}_{i}}{\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\Sigma^{-2}\bm{u}_{i}}.

(vii)

For the inverse quadratic norm, we have that
\varphi_{i}=\frac{\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\Sigma^{2}\bm{u}_{i}}{\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\Sigma\bm{u}_{i}}.

When
c_{n}>1
, the above results remain valid for
i\in[\![{\mathsf{K}+1,p}]\!]
by replacing the factors
\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\ell(\Sigma)\bm{u}_{i}
(for
\ell(x)=x,\ x^{-1},\ \log x,\ \sqrt{x},\ x^{-2},\ x^{2}
) with
\tr[U_{0}^{\top}\ell(\Sigma)U_{0}]/(p-n).

Proof.

The proof follows from straightforward calculations; see
[
32
,
52
]
.
∎

For the above loss functions, we have the following decomposition of their associated risks. Recall the the optimal invariant estimator
\widetilde{\Sigma}
in (
1.2
) and define the diagonal matrix

\Phi:=\operatorname{diag}\left\{\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{p}\right\}.

Lemma A.2
.

For the loss functions in Table
1
and their optimal solutions
\{\varphi_{i}\}
given in Lemma
A.1
, we have the following identities.

(i)

For the Frobenius norm, we have that

\|\Sigma-\widetilde{\Sigma}\|_{F}^{2}=\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{F}^{2}-\left\|\Phi\right\|_{F}^{2}.

(ii)

For the inverse Frobenius norm, we have that

\|\Sigma^{-1}-\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-1}\|_{F}^{2}=\|\Sigma^{-1}\|_{F}^{2}-\|\Phi^{-1}\|_{F}^{2}.

(iii)

For the weighted Frobenius norm, we have that

\tr\big[(\widetilde{\Sigma}-\Sigma)^{2}\Sigma^{-1}\big]=\tr(\Sigma)-\tr(\Phi).

(iv)

For the disutility norm, we have that

\tr\big[(\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-1}-\Sigma^{-1})^{2}\Sigma\big]=\tr(\Sigma^{-1})-\tr(\Phi^{-1}).

(v)

For the inverse Stein norm, we have that

\tr\big[\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-1}\Sigma-I\big]-\log[\det(\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-1}\Sigma)]=\log\left(\frac{\det(\Phi)}{\det(\Sigma)}\right).

(vi)

For the Stein norm, we have that

\tr\big[\widetilde{\Sigma}\Sigma^{-1}-I\big]-\log[\det(\widetilde{\Sigma}\Sigma^{-1})]=\log\left(\frac{\det(\Sigma)}{\det(\Phi)}\right).

(vii)

For the Fréchet norm, we have that

\big\|\widetilde{\Sigma}^{1/2}-\Sigma^{1/2}\big\|_{F}^{2}=\tr(\Sigma)-\tr(\Phi).

(viii)

For the minimal variance norm, we have that

p\tr[\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-1}\Sigma\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-1}]\Big/\left(\tr[\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-1}]\right)^{2}=p/\tr(\Phi^{-1}).

(ix)

For the quadratic norm, we have that

\big\|\Sigma^{-1}\widetilde{\Sigma}-I\big\|_{F}^{2}=p-\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{K}}\frac{({\bf{u}}_{i}^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}{\bf{u}}_{i})^{2}}{{\bf{u}}_{i}^{\top}\Sigma^{-2}{\bf{u}}_{i}}-\frac{[\tr(U_{0}^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}U_{0})]^{2}}{\tr(U_{0}^{\top}\Sigma^{-2}U_{0})}.

(x)

For the inverse quadratic norm, we have that

\big\|\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-1}\Sigma-I\big\|_{F}^{2}=p-\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{K}}\frac{({\bf{u}}_{i}^{\top}\Sigma{\bf{u}}_{i})^{2}}{{\bf{u}}_{i}^{\top}\Sigma^{2}{\bf{u}}_{i}}-\frac{[\tr(U_{0}^{\top}\Sigma U_{0})]^{2}}{\tr(U_{0}^{\top}\Sigma^{2}U_{0})}.

(xi)

For the Log-Euclidean norm, we have that

\big\|\log(\widetilde{\Sigma})-\log(\Sigma)\big\|_{F}^{2}=\|\log(\Sigma)\|_{F}^{2}-\|\log(\Phi)\|_{F}^{2}.

(xii)

For the symmetrized Stein norm, we have that

\frac{1}{2}\tr\big[\widetilde{\Sigma}\Sigma^{-1}+\widetilde{\Sigma}^{-1}\Sigma-2I\big]=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{K}}\sqrt{({\bf{u}}_{i}^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}{\bf{u}}_{i})\cdot({\bf{u}}_{i}^{\top}\Sigma{\bf{u}}_{i})}+\sqrt{\tr(U_{0}^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}U_{0})\cdot\tr(U_{0}^{\top}\Sigma U_{0})}-p.

Proof.

The proof follows from straightforward calculations using Lemma
A.1
. We omit the details.
∎

Appendix B
Some preliminary results

In this section, we present some results that will be used in the technical proofs of the main results. For ease of presentation, in this paper, we consistently use the following notion of
stochastic domination
introduced in
[
34
]
.
It simplifies the presentation of the results and their proofs by systematizing statements of the form “
\xi
is bounded by
\zeta
with high probability up to a small power of
n
”.

Definition B.1

(Stochastic domination)
.

(i)
Let

\xi=\left(\xi^{(n)}(u):n\in\mathbb{N},u\in U^{(n)}\right),\quad\zeta=\left(\zeta^{(n)}(u):n\in\mathbb{N},u\in U^{(n)}\right),

be two families of non-negative random variables, where
U^{(n)}
is a possibly
n
-dependent parameter set. We say
\xi
is stochastically dominated by
\zeta
, uniformly in
u
, if for any fixed (small)
\tau>0
and (large)
D>0
,

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\bigcup_{u\in U^{(n)}}\left\{\xi^{(n)}(u)>n^{\tau}\zeta^{(n)}(u)\right\}\bigg)\leq n^{-D}

for large enough
n\geq n_{0}(\tau,D)
, and we will use the notation
\xi\prec\zeta
. If for some complex family
\xi
we have
|\xi|\prec\zeta
, then we will also write
\xi\prec\zeta
or
\xi=\OO_{\prec}(\zeta)
.

(ii)
As a convention, for two deterministic non-negative quantities
\xi
and
\zeta
, we will write
\xi\prec\zeta
if and only if
\xi\leq n^{\tau}\zeta
for any constant
\tau>0
.

(iii)
We say that an event
\Xi
holds with high probability (w.h.p.) if for any constant
D>0
,
\mathbb{P}(\Xi)\geq 1-n^{-D}
for large enough
n
. More generally, we say that an event
\Omega
holds
w.h.p.
in
\Xi
if for any constant
D>0
,

\mathbb{P}(\Xi\setminus\Omega)\leq n^{-D}
for large enough
n
.

We denote the resolvents of the sample covariance matrices in (
2.4
) and (
2.5
) by

\mathsf{G}_{a}(z):=\left(\mathcal{Q}_{a}-z\right)^{-1},\quad\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}_{a}(z):=\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{a}-z\big)^{-1},\quad a\in\{1,2\}.

(B.1)

For our proofs, it is more convenient to work with the following linearized block matrix
\mathsf{H}
, whose inverse
\mathsf{G}
is also called the resolvent (of
\mathsf{H}
):

\mathsf{H}(z):=\begin{pmatrix}-\Sigma_{0}^{-1}&X\\
X^{\top}&-zI_{n}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathsf{G}(z):=\mathsf{H}(z)^{-1}.

(B.2)

By Schur’s complement formula (see e.g., Lemma 4.4 of
[
45
]
), we have that

\mathsf{G}(z):=\begin{pmatrix}z\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}\mathsf{G}_{1}(z)\Sigma_{0}^{1/2}&\Sigma_{0}X\mathsf{G}_{2}\\
\mathsf{G}_{2}X^{\top}\Sigma_{0}&\mathsf{G}_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

(B.3)

Hence, a control of the resolvent
\mathsf{G}
yields directly a control of both
\mathsf{G}_{1}
and
\mathsf{G}_{2}
.
For notational convenience, we will consistently use the following notations of index sets

\mathcal{I}_{1}:=[\![{1,p}]\!],\quad\ \mathcal{I}_{2}:=[\![{p+1,p+n}]\!],\quad\ \mathcal{I}:=\mathcal{I}_{1}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}=[\![{1,p+n}]\!].

(B.4)

Then, we label the indices of the blocks of
\mathsf{H}
according to

X=(X_{i\mu}:i\in\mathcal{I}_{1},\mu\in\mathcal{I}_{2}).

In what follows, we often omit the dependence on
z
and simply write
\mathsf{H},\mathsf{G}
, etc. Finally, we adopt the following convention for matrix multiplication:
for matrices of the form
A:=(A_{st}:s\in\mathsf{l}(A),t\in\mathsf{r}(A))
and
B:=(B_{st}:s\in\mathsf{l}(B),t\in\mathsf{r}(B))
, whose entries are indexed by some subsets
\mathsf{l}(A),\mathsf{r}(A),\mathsf{l}(B),\mathsf{r}(B)\subset\mathbb{N},
the matrix multiplication
AB
is understood as

(AB)_{st}:=\sum_{k\in\mathsf{r}(A)\cap\mathsf{l}(B)}A_{sk}B_{kt}

(B.5)

for
s\in\mathsf{l}(A)
and
t\in\mathsf{r}(B)
.

B.1
Local laws for sample covariance matrices

In this subsection, we present a key input for our proofs, namely the local laws for the resolvent
\mathsf{G}

[
45
]
.
Recall the Stieltjes transform of the deformed Marchenco-Pastur law
m(z)
defined in (
2.8
).
We introduce the following (deterministic) matrix limit of
\mathsf{G}
defined as

\Pi_{\mathsf{G}}(z):=\begin{pmatrix}-\Sigma_{0}(1+m(z)\Sigma_{0})^{-1}&0\\
0&m(z)I_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

(B.6)

Moreover, for an arbitrarily small constant
\tau\in(0,1)
, we define the spectral parameter domain

\mathbf{D}\equiv\mathbf{D}(\tau,n):=\left\{z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbb{C}_{+}:\tau\leqslant E\leqslant\tau^{-1},\ n^{-1+\tau}\leqslant\eta\leqslant\tau^{-1}\right\},

(B.7)

a spectral domain outside the support of
\varrho
,

\mathbf{D}_{o}\equiv\mathbf{D}_{o}(\tau,n):=\left\{z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbb{C}_{+}:\tau\leq E\leq\tau^{-1},0<\eta\leq\tau^{-1},\ \operatorname{dist}(E,\operatorname{supp}(\varrho))\geqslant n^{-2/3+\tau}\right\},

and a spectral domain around the origin,

\mathbf{D}_{0}\equiv\mathbf{D}_{0}(n):=\left\{z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbb{C}:|z|\leq(\log n)^{-1}\right\},

Define
\mathsf{m}_{n}(z):=n^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\mathsf{G}_{2}(z).
The following lemma states the local laws for
\mathsf{G}(z)
and
\mathsf{m}_{n}(z)
.

Lemma B.2

(Theorems 3.6 and 3.7 of
[
45
]
)
.

Suppose (i)–(iii) of Assumption
2.3
hold. Then, the following estimates hold uniformly in
z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbf{D}
:

\scriptstyle{\blacktriangleright}

Anisotropic local law
: For any two deterministic unit vectors
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{p+n}
, we have

\left|\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathsf{G}(z)\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}^{\top}\Pi_{\mathsf{G}}(z)\mathbf{v}\right|\prec\Psi(z),

(B.8)

where
\Psi
is an error control parameter defined as

\Psi(z):=\sqrt{\frac{\im m(z)}{n\eta}}+\frac{1}{n\eta}.

\scriptstyle{\blacktriangleright}

Averaged local law
: We have

|\mathsf{m}_{n}(z)-m(z)|\prec\frac{1}{n\eta}.

(B.9)

In addition, outside the support of
\varrho
, we have a stronger
anisotropic local law
uniformly in
z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbf{D}_{o}
:

\left|\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathsf{G}(z)\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}^{\top}\Pi_{\mathsf{G}}(z)\mathbf{v}\right|\prec n^{-1/2}(\kappa+\eta)^{-1/4},

(B.10)

for any two deterministic unit vectors
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{p+n}
, where
\kappa
is defined as
\kappa:=\operatorname{dist}(E,\supp\varrho).

Similar to (
B.2
) and (
B.6
), we define the (linearized) resolvent
\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}
for the spiked model and the corresponding deterministic limit as

\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(z):=\begin{pmatrix}-\Sigma^{-1}&X\\
X^{\top}&-zI_{n}\end{pmatrix}^{-1},\quad\widetilde{\Pi}_{\mathsf{G}}(z):=\begin{pmatrix}-\Sigma(1+m(z)\Sigma)^{-1}&0\\
0&m(z)I_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

(B.11)

Lemma B.3

(Theorem C.4 of
[
70
]
)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.3
holds and
c_{n}\geq 1+\tau
. Then, the following
anisotropic local law
holds for
\mathsf{G}
and
\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}
uniformly in
z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbf{D}_{0}
:

\left|\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathsf{G}(z)\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}^{\top}\Pi_{\mathsf{G}}(z)\mathbf{v}\right|\prec n^{-1/2},\quad\big|\mathbf{u}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(z)\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}^{\top}\widetilde{\Pi}_{\mathsf{G}}(z)\mathbf{v}\big|\prec n^{-1/2}.

(B.12)

Remark B.4
.

In the proofs, it is slightly more convenient to use the following version of Lemma
B.2
. Recall the eigenmatrix
V\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
defined in (
2.1
). Let

\mathbf{V}:=\begin{pmatrix}V&0\\
0&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+p)\times(n+p)}.

We then rotate
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{G}
in (
B.2
) as

H(z):=\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathsf{H}(z)\mathbf{V},\quad G(z):=H(z)^{-1}.

(B.13)

Under this definition, with (
B.3
), we have that

H=\begin{pmatrix}-\Lambda_{0}^{-1}&V^{\top}X\\
X^{\top}V&-z\end{pmatrix},\quad G=\begin{pmatrix}z\Lambda_{0}^{1/2}\mathcal{G}_{1}\Lambda_{0}^{1/2}&\Lambda_{0}V^{\top}X\mathcal{G}_{2}\\
\mathcal{G}_{2}X^{\top}V\Lambda_{0}&\mathcal{G}_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

(B.14)

where the resolvents
\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{2}
are defined as

\mathcal{G}_{1}(z):=(\Lambda_{0}^{1/2}V^{\top}XX^{\top}V\Lambda_{0}^{1/2}-z)^{-1},\quad\mathcal{G}_{2}(z):=\mathsf{G}_{2}(z).

(B.15)

In this case, it is easy to see that Lemmas
B.2
and
B.3
hold for
G(z)
if we replace
\Pi_{\mathsf{G}}(z)
by

\Pi(z)=\begin{pmatrix}-\Lambda_{0}(1+m(z)\Lambda_{0})^{-1}&0\\
0&m(z)\end{pmatrix}.

(B.16)

The main advantage of
\Pi
over
\Pi_{\mathsf{G}}
is that
\Pi
is a diagonal matrix. Similarly, we can define
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{2}
,
\widetilde{G}
, and
\widetilde{\Pi}
by replacing
\Lambda_{0}
with
\Lambda
in the above definitions.

The following lemma outlines some basic estimates regarding
m(z)
and
\im m(z)
. Notably, it shows that the entries of
\Pi
in (
B.16
) are of order 1. These estimates will be used tacitly in the subsequent proofs.

Lemma B.5
.

Suppose items (i) and (iii) of Assumption
2.3
hold. Then, for any small constant
c>0
, the following estimates hold uniformly for
z
satisfying
c\leq|z|\leq c^{-1}
:

|m(z)|\asymp 1,\quad\im m(z)\asymp\begin{cases}\sqrt{\kappa+\eta},\ &\text{if}\ E\in\supp(\varrho)\\
\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}},\ &\text{if}\ E\notin\supp(\varrho)\end{cases},

(B.17)

\min_{j=1}^{p}|1+m(z)\sigma_{j}|\gtrsim 1.

(B.18)

For sufficiently small constant
c>0
, the following estimate holds uniformly for
z
satisfying
|z|\leq c
:

|zm(z)|\asymp\begin{cases}1,&\ \text{if}\ c_{n}\leq 1-\tau\\
|z|,&\ \text{if}\ c_{n}\geq 1+\tau\end{cases}.

(B.19)

Proof.

The estimates in (
B.17
) are proved in Lemma 4.10 and equation (A.7) of
[
45
]
. The estimate (
B.18
) is proved in Lemma A.6 of
[
28
]
. Finally, the estimate (
B.19
) follows from the fact that the deformed MP law has support within
[\lambda_{-},\lambda_{+}]
with a delta mass
(1-c_{n})\delta_{0}
when
c_{n}<1
.
∎

As an important consequence of the averaged local laws in Lemma
B.2
, we have the following rigidity estimate of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{j}\equiv\lambda_{j}(\mathcal{Q}_{2})
.

Lemma B.6

(Theorem 3.12 of
[
45
]
)
.

Under the assumptions of
Lemma

B.2
, for
\gamma_{j}\in[a_{2k},a_{2k-1}]
, we have

|\lambda_{j}-\gamma_{j}|\prec[(n_{k}+1-j)\wedge(j-n_{k-1})]^{-1/3}n^{-{2}/{3}},

(B.20)

where we recall the notations in (
2.11
) and
Definition

2.2
.

B.2
Eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the spiked covariance model

In this subsection, we present a collection of useful results regarding the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the spiked model
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
in (
2.4
). Recall that
\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}
and
\lambda_{i}
represent the eigenvalues of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
, respectively, while
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
and
\mathfrak{b}_{i}
are defined in (
2.20
). Furthermore, we emphasize that the
{\bm{u}_{i}}
mentioned in Lemmas
B.7
and
B.8
below refers to the eigenvectors of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
, rather than
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
.
First,
Lemma

B.7
gives the first-order limits of the outlier eigenvalues and eigenvectors, along with nearly optimal convergence rates.

Lemma B.7

(Theorems 3.2 and 3.3 of
[
27
]
)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.3
holds. For any
i\in[\![{r}]\!]
, we have that

\left|\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}-\mathfrak{a}_{i}\right|\prec n^{-1/2}.

(B.21)

Moreover, for the corresponding outlier eigenvectors, we have that for any
i,j\in[\![{r}]\!]
,

\left|\langle\bm{u}_{i},\bm{v}_{j}\rangle\right|^{2}-\frac{\mathfrak{b}_{i}}{\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}}\delta_{ij}\prec n^{-1/2}.

(B.22)

Next, we state some estimates for the non-outlier eigenvalues and eigenvectors.

Lemma B.8
.

Suppose Assumption
2.3
holds. For
i\in[\![{\mathsf{K}-r}]\!]
, we have the eigenvalue sticking estimate:

|\widetilde{\lambda}_{i+r}-\lambda_{i}|\prec n^{-1},

(B.23)

and the eigenvector delocalization estimate:

\left|\langle\bm{u}_{i+r},{\bf{v}}\rangle\right|^{2}\prec n^{-1}

(B.24)

for any deterministic unit vector
{\bf{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
. When
c_{n}>1
, we have that

\|U_{0}^{\top}{\bf{v}}\|_{2}^{2}\prec 1.

(B.25)

Finally, for
i\in[\![{r}]\!]
and
j\in[\![{r+1,p}]\!],
we have that

\left|\langle\bm{u}_{i},\bm{v}_{j}\rangle\right|^{2}-\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{i}}{\sigma_{j}}G_{ij}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})G_{ji}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})\prec n^{-3/2},\quad\text{with}\quad\mathfrak{c}_{i}:=\frac{d_{i}^{2}\mathfrak{b}_{i}}{\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}^{2}}.

(B.26)

Proof.

The results (
B.23
) and (
B.24
) have been proved in Theorems 3.7 and 3.14 of
[
29
]
for
i\leqslant\tau p
with
\tau\in(0,1)
being a small constant. The reason for considering a subset of indices is that only the edge regularity condition around
\lambda_{+}=a_{1}
was assumed in
[
29
]
. However, with the stronger regularity conditions in (
2.16
) and (
2.17
), we can extend (
B.23
) and (
B.24
) to all
i\in[\![{\mathsf{K}-r}]\!]
following the proof in
[
29
]
.
We omit the details. As for (
B.25
), we have the trivial bound

{\|U_{0}^{\top}{\bf{v}}\|_{2}^{2}}\leq\eta\im[{\bf{v}}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}_{1}(\mathrm{i}\eta){\bf{v}}],

which follows from the spectral decomposition of
\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}_{1}
.
The RHS can be written as

{\bf{v}}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}_{1}(\mathrm{i}\eta){\bf{v}}=\left({\bf{v}}^{\top}\Sigma^{-1/2},\mathbf{0}_{n}^{\top}\right)\frac{\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}(\mathrm{i}\eta)}{\mathrm{i}\eta}\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma^{-1/2}{\bf{v}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

Then, (
B.25
) follows immediately from the local law on
\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}
in (
B.12
) by taking
\eta=(\log n)^{-1}
.

It remains to prove (
B.26
). The proof relies on a representation of
\left|\langle\bm{u}_{i},\bm{v}_{j}\rangle\right|^{2}
in terms of contour integrals of the resolvent
G
(see e.g.,
[
13
,
18
,
27
,
29
]
).
We only sketch the proof since many arguments below are similar to those in
[
18
,
27
]
.
Let
\{{\bf{u}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{p}=\{V^{\top}\bm{u}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{p}
be the left singular eigenvectors of
\Lambda^{1/2}V^{\top}X
.
Due to the relation

\langle\bm{v}_{j},\bm{u}_{i}\rangle=(\bm{v}_{j}^{\top}V){\bf{u}}_{i}={\bf{e}}_{j}^{\top}{\bf{u}}_{i},

(B.27)

it suffices to focus on the entries
{\bf{u}}_{i}(j)
of
{\bf{u}}_{i}
in the following proof.
Let
\Gamma_{i}
denote the boundary of
B_{\rho}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})
, which represents a disk of radius
\rho
centered at
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
. Here, we choose
\rho
to be sufficiently small so that
B_{2\rho}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})
does not contain
\lambda_{+}
or any other outlier
\mathfrak{a}_{k}
,
k\in[\![{r}]\!]\setminus\{i\}
. Then, by (
B.21
) and item (iv) of Assumption
2.3
, we know that with high probability,
\Gamma_{i}
only encloses
\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}
and no other eigenvalues of
G(z)
. By applying Cauchy’s integral formula, we obtain that

|{\bf{u}}_{i}(j)|^{2}=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{i}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1})_{jj}(z)\mathrm{d}z=-\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{i}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{\delta})_{jj}(z)\mathrm{d}z,

(B.28)

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{\delta}
is defined by introducing a perturbation to the
j
-th eigenvalue of
\Lambda
:

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{\delta}(z):=\big(\Lambda_{\delta}^{1/2}V^{\top}XX^{\top}V\Lambda_{\delta}^{1/2}-z\big)^{-1},\quad\text{with}\quad\Lambda_{\delta}:=\Lambda+\delta\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}\mathbf{e}_{j}\mathbf{e}_{j}^{\top}.

To compute the right-hand side (RHS) of (
B.28
), we utilize the Woodbury matrix identity to express
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\delta}_{1}
in terms of
\mathcal{G}_{1}
. Using the fact that
\mathcal{G}_{1}
contains no pole inside
\Gamma_{i}
, we then obtain the following expression:

\langle{\bf{u}}_{i},{\bf{e}}_{j}\rangle^{2}=\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{1+\delta}{\delta^{2}}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{i}}\left[\mathbf{D}_{r,j}(\delta)^{-1}+1+z\mathbf{E}_{r,j}^{\top}\mathcal{G}_{1}(z)\mathbf{E}_{r,j}\right]_{r+1,r+1}^{-1}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}.

(B.29)

where we introduce the matrices

\mathbf{E}_{r,j}:=({\bf{e}}_{1},\cdots,{\bf{e}}_{r},{\bf{e}}_{j})\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times(r+1)},\quad\mathbf{D}_{r,j}(\delta):=\operatorname{diag}\left\{d_{1},d_{2},\cdots,d_{r},\delta\right\}\in\mathbb{R}^{(r+1)\times(r+1)}.

For a detailed derivation of (
B.29
), we refer readers to Lemma 5.7 and equations (7.5) and (7.6) of
[
27
]
.

Denote
\Lambda_{0,j}:=\operatorname{diag}\{\sigma_{1},\cdots,\sigma_{r},\sigma_{j}\}
, and

\Delta_{j}(z):=-\Upsilon_{j}(z)-z\mathbf{E}_{r,j}^{\top}\mathcal{G}_{1}(z)\mathbf{E}_{r,j},\quad\Upsilon_{j}(z):=\left(1+m(z)\Lambda_{0,j}\right)^{-1}.

(B.30)

By the local law (
B.10
) and the estimate on
\im m(z)
in (
B.17
), we have that uniformly in
z\in\Gamma_{i}
,

\left\|\Delta_{j}(z)\right\|\prec n^{-1/2}.

(B.31)

Denote
\Xi_{j}(z):=\mathbf{D}_{r,j}^{-1}+1-\Upsilon_{j}(z).
By item (iv) of
Assumption

2.3
and the choice of
\rho
, we have that
\|\Xi_{j}(z)^{-1}\|\lesssim 1
uniformly in
z\in\Gamma_{i}
. Then, applying Taylor expansion to

\left(\mathbf{D}_{r,j}^{-1}+1+z\mathbf{E}_{r,j}^{\top}\mathcal{G}_{1}(z)\mathbf{E}_{r,j}\right)^{-1}=\left(\Xi_{j}(z)-\Delta_{j}(z)\right)^{-1}

till order three, we can expand (
B.29
) as

\langle{\bf{u}}_{i},{\bf{e}}_{j}\rangle^{2}=s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3}+s_{4},

where
s_{i}
,
i=1,2,3,4,
are defined as

\displaystyle s_{1}:=\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{1+\delta}{\delta^{2}}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{i}}\frac{1}{1+\delta^{-1}-(1+m(z)\sigma_{j})^{-1}}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z},

\displaystyle s_{2}:=\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{1+\delta}{\delta^{2}}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{i}}\frac{\left(\Delta(z)\right)_{r+1,r+1}}{[1+\delta^{-1}-(1+m(z)\sigma_{j})^{-1}]^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z},

\displaystyle s_{3}:=\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{1+\delta}{\delta^{2}}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{i}}\left[\Xi_{j}^{-1}(z)\Delta(z)\Xi_{j}^{-1}(z)\Delta(z)\Xi_{j}^{-1}(z)\right]_{r+1,r+1}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z},

\displaystyle s_{4}:=\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{1+\delta}{\delta^{2}}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{i}}\left[\left(\Xi_{j}^{-1}(z)\Delta(z)\right)^{3}\left(\Xi_{j}(z)-\Delta(z)\right)^{-1}\right]_{r+1,r+1}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}.

Using (
B.31
), it is easy to see that

|s_{4}|\prec n^{-3/2}.

For
s_{1}
and
s_{2},
by following a similar argument as in
[
27
, Proposition 7.2]
or
[
18
, Lemma 4.31]
, we can show that for sufficiently small
\delta,
the functions
z^{-1}
,
[1+\delta^{-1}-(1+m(z)\sigma_{j})^{-1}]^{-1}
, and
(\Delta(z))_{r+1,r+1}
are all holomorphic on and inside
\Gamma_{i}
with high probability. Hence, applying Cauchy’s integral theorem, we get that

s_{1}=s_{2}=0\quad\text{with high probability}.

(B.32)

To estimate the leading term
s_{3}
, we employ a modified proof strategy similar to that of
[
18
, Lemma 4.32]
. By definition, we can write that

\displaystyle\left[\Xi_{j}^{-1}(z)\Delta(z)\Xi_{j}^{-1}(z)\Delta(z)\Xi_{j}^{-1}(z)\right]_{r+1,r+1}=\frac{1}{\left[1+\delta^{-1}-(1+m(z)\sigma_{j})^{-1}\right]^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{r+1}\Delta_{r+1,k}\Delta_{k,r+1}\Xi_{j}(k,k)^{-1},

where we have introduced the abbreviation
\Delta\equiv\Delta(z)
and used
\Xi_{j}(k,k)
to denote the
k
-th diagonal entry of
\Xi_{j}(z)
. We decompose the sum into a diagonal part with
k=r+1
and an off-diagonal part with
k\in[\![{r}]\!]
:

\sum_{k=1}^{r+1}\Delta_{r+1,k}\Delta_{k,r+1}\Xi_{j}(k,k)^{-1}=\mathsf{E}_{d}+\mathsf{E}_{o},

where
\mathsf{E}_{d}
and
\mathsf{E}_{o}
are defined as

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{d}:=\Delta^{2}_{r+1,r+1}\Xi_{j}(r+1,r+1),\quad\mathsf{E}_{o}:=z^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{r}\frac{({\bf{e}}_{j}^{\top}\mathcal{G}_{1}(z){\bf{e}}_{k})({\bf{e}}_{k}^{\top}\mathcal{G}_{1}(z){\bf{e}}_{j})}{1+d_{k}^{-1}-(1+m(z)\sigma_{k})^{-1}}.

By the discussion above (
B.32
), we have that for sufficiently small
\delta
,
\mathsf{E}_{d}
is holomorphic on and inside
\Gamma_{i}
with high probability. This implies that

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{i}}\frac{\mathsf{E}_{d}}{\left[1+\delta^{-1}-(1+m(z)\sigma_{j})^{-1}\right]^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}=0\quad\text{with high probability}.

For the off-diagonal part
\mathsf{E}_{o}
, using (
B.14
), we can rewrite it as

\mathsf{E}_{o}=\frac{1}{\sigma_{j}}\sum_{k=1}^{r}\frac{1}{\sigma_{k}}\frac{G_{jk}(z)G_{kj}(z)}{1+d_{k}^{-1}-(1+m(z)\sigma_{k})^{-1}}.

Under item (iv) of Assumption
2.3
, using the definition of
m(z)
in (
2.8
) and the definition of
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
in (
2.20
), we find that for
k\in[\![{r}]\!]\setminus\{i\}
,

1+d_{k}^{-1}-(1+m(\mathfrak{a}_{i})\sigma_{k})^{-1}=1+d_{k}^{-1}-\left(1-{\sigma_{k}}/\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}\right)^{-1}\gtrsim 1.

Consequently, due to the continuity of
m(z)
near
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
, we can choose
\rho
sufficiently small such that
1+d_{k}^{-1}-(1+m(z)\sigma_{k})^{-1}
has no zero on and inside
\Gamma_{i}
. Then, by Cauchy’s integral theorem, we have that for
k\in[\![{r}]\!]\setminus\{i\}
,

\oint_{\Gamma_{i}}\frac{G_{jk}(z)G_{kj}(z)}{\left[1+\delta^{-1}-(1+m(z)\sigma_{j})^{-1}\right]^{2}\left[1+d_{k}^{-1}-(1+m(z)\sigma_{k})^{-1}\right]}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}=0

with high probability. Finally, we only need to consider the
k=i
term in
\mathsf{E}_{o}
. Applying the parametrization
z=h(\zeta),
we obtain that with high probability,

\displaystyle\frac{1+\delta}{\delta^{2}}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{i}}\frac{\mathsf{E}_{o}}{\left[1+\delta^{-1}-(1+m(z)\sigma_{j})^{-1}\right]^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+\delta)d_{i}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}}\oint_{\gamma_{i}}\frac{h^{\prime}(\zeta)G_{ji}(h(\zeta))G_{ij}(h(\zeta))}{h(\zeta)\left[1+\delta-\delta(1+\zeta\sigma_{j})^{-1}\right]^{2}}\frac{1+\zeta\sigma_{i}}{\zeta+\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}^{-1}}\mathrm{d}\zeta,

\displaystyle=(1+\delta)\frac{d_{i}^{2}\mathfrak{b}_{i}}{\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}^{2}\sigma_{j}}\frac{G_{ji}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})G_{ij}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})}{\left[1+\delta-\delta(1-\sigma_{j}/\widetilde{\sigma}_{i})^{-1}\right]^{2}},

where we used the relation
m(h(\zeta))=\zeta
as in (
2.8
) in the first step, and the residual theorem at
\zeta=-\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}^{-1}
in the second step. Finally, taking
\delta\to 0
and using the dominated convergence theorem, we conclude that

s_{3}=\frac{d_{i}^{2}\mathfrak{b}_{i}}{\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}^{2}\sigma_{j}}G_{ji}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})G_{ij}(\mathfrak{a}_{i}),

which completes the proof of (
B.26
).
∎

B.3
Local laws for the rectangular matrix Dyson Brownian motion

In this subsection, we consider the rectangular matrix DBM of the
non-spiked
model:

W_{0}(t):=W_{0}+\sqrt{t}X^{G},\quad\text{with}\quad W_{0}:=\Lambda_{0}^{1/2}V^{\top}X,

where
X^{G}
is defined above (
4.3
). In this subsection, we present the local laws for its resolvent, which will be utilized in our proof. From these local laws, we can also derive the local laws for the resolvent of the (spiked) rectangular matrix DBM defined in (
4.3
) and (
4.4
).

Let us begin by introducing some notations. We define
\Lambda_{t}:=\Lambda_{0}+t
and
m_{t}(z):=m(z,\Lambda_{t})
as given by (
2.8
). In other words,
m_{t}(z)
is the unique solution to the equation

z=-\frac{1}{m_{t}}+\frac{1}{n}\tr\frac{\Lambda_{t}}{1+m_{t}\Lambda_{t}}

(B.33)

subject to the condition that
\im m_{t}(z)\geq 0
whenever
\im z\geq 0
. Subsequently, we denote the density function associated with
m_{t}
as
\varrho_{t}
, and refer to the spectral edges and quantiles of
\varrho_{t}
as
a_{k}(t)
and
\gamma_{k}(t)
, following the definitions in
Lemma

2.1
and in
Definition

2.2
.
Using the stability of the self-consistent equation (
B.33
) established in
[
45
]
, it is not hard to check that

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\varrho_{t}(x)-\varrho(x)|=\oo(1)

(B.34)

when
t=\oo(1)
.
Then, we define
\mathcal{G}_{1,t}
,
\mathcal{G}_{2,t}
,
G_{t}
, and
\Pi_{t}
as described in
Remark

B.4
, with the replacements of
W_{0}
,
\Lambda_{0}
, and
m(z)
by
W_{0}(t)
,
\Lambda_{t}
, and
m_{t}(z)
, respectively.
Let
\{\lambda_{k}(t)\}_{k=1}^{p}
and
\{{\bf{u}}_{k}(t)\}_{k=1}^{p}
denote the eigenvalues and corresponding eigenvectors of
W_{0}(t)W_{0}(t)^{\top}
.
Finally, we introduce the error control parameter

\Psi_{t}(z):=\sqrt{\frac{\im m_{t}(z)}{n\eta}}+\frac{1}{n\eta},

Now, we are ready to state the main result of this subsection.

Lemma B.9
.

Suppose (i)–(iii) of Assumption
2.3
hold. Fix a time
T\asymp n^{-1/3+\mathsf{c}}
for some constant
\mathsf{c}\in(0,1/6).
The following estimates hold uniformly in
z\in\mathbf{D}
and
t\in[0,T]
.

(i)

The support of
\varrho_{t}
has the same number of connected components as
\varrho_{t}
, and the spectral edges
a_{k}(t)
of
\varrho_{t}
are perturbations of those of
\varrho
:
\max_{k=1}^{2q}|a_{k}(t)-a_{k}|=\oo(1).
In particular, it implies that
|\lambda_{\pm,t}-\lambda_{\pm}|=\oo(1)
, where
\lambda_{+,t}:=a_{1}(t)
and
\lambda_{-,t}:=a_{2q}(t)
denote the rightmost and leftmost spectral edges of
\varrho_{t}
, respectively.
The number of quantiles within each component remains unchanged, that is,
n_{k}(t)
defined as in (
2.11
) is constant in
t
.

(ii)

For any deterministic unit vectors
{\bf{u}},{\bf{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p+n}
, we have the anisotropic local law (recall
Lemma

B.2
)

\left|{\bf{u}}^{\top}{G}_{t}(z){\bf{v}}-{\bf{u}}^{\top}\Pi_{t}(z){\bf{v}}\right|\prec\Psi_{t}(z).

(B.35)

For
\mathsf{m}_{n,t}:=n^{-1}\tr\mathcal{G}_{2,t}
, the following averaged local law holds:

|\mathsf{m}_{n,t}(z)-m_{t}(z)|\prec\frac{1}{n\eta}.

(B.36)

(iii)

For
\gamma_{j}(t)\in[a_{2k}(t),a_{2k-1}(t)]
, we have that (recall
Lemma

B.6
)

\left|\lambda_{j}(t)-\gamma_{j}(t)\right|\prec[(n_{k}(t)+1-j)\wedge(j-n_{k-1}(t))]^{-1/3}n^{-{2}/{3}}.

(B.37)

(iv)

For
k\in[\![{\mathsf{K}}]\!]
and any deterministic unit vector
{\bf{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we have that (recall
Lemma

B.8
)

\left|\langle{\bf{u}}_{k}(t),{\bf{v}}\rangle\right|^{2}\prec n^{-1}.

(B.38)

Proof.

Under the edge regularity condition (
2.16
), it is easy to see that
a_{k}(t)
and
n_{k}(t)
are continuous in
t
when
t=\oo(1)
. This establishes part (i) of the lemma, taking into account the fact that
n_{k}(t)
takes integer values. For the other parts, we notice that
W_{0}(t)
can be expressed as

W_{0}(t)=\Lambda_{t}^{1/2}\mathbf{U}\begin{pmatrix}X\\
X^{G}\end{pmatrix},

(B.39)

where
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times 2p}
is a semi-orthogonal matrix defined as

\mathbf{U}:=\begin{pmatrix}(\Lambda_{0}/\Lambda_{t})^{1/2}V^{\top},(t/\Lambda_{t})^{1/2}I_{p}\end{pmatrix}.

This is the random matrix model studied in
[
45
]
. Furthermore, (
B.34
) and part (i) together validate the regularity conditions in (
2.16
) and (
2.17
) for
\Lambda_{t}
. Then, for each fixed
t
, (ii) is proved in Theorems 3.6 and 3.7 of
[
45
]
, (iii) is proved in Theorem 3.12 of
[
45
]
, and (iv) follows easily from (ii) and (iii) as explained, for example, in
[
12
, Theorem 2.8]
.
Finally, by utilizing a standard
\varepsilon
-net argument, we can extend these results uniformly to all
t\in[0,T]
.
∎

Remark B.10
.

The estimate (
B.36
) provides an averaged local law for
\mathsf{m}_{n,t}.
In the following proof, we will also need an averaged local law for
\underline{\mathsf{m}}_{n,t}(z):=p^{-1}\tr\mathcal{G}_{1,t}
, whose classical limit is given by

m_{1,t}(z):=-\frac{1}{p}\tr\frac{1}{z(1+m_{t}(z)\Lambda_{t})}.

From (
B.33
), we can observe that

m_{1,t}(z)=\frac{c_{n}^{-1}-1}{z}+c_{n}^{-1}m_{t}(z).

(B.40)

On the other hand, since
W_{0}(t)W_{0}(t)^{\top}
share the non-zero same eigenvalues as
W_{0}(t)^{\top}W_{0}(t)
, we have a similar relation between
\underline{\mathsf{m}}_{n,t}(z)
and
\mathsf{m}_{n,t}(z)
:

\underline{\mathsf{m}}_{n,t}(z)=\frac{c_{n}^{-1}-1}{z}+c_{n}^{-1}\mathsf{m}_{n,t}(z).

(B.41)

Under these two equations, we can directly deduce from (
B.36
) that

|\underline{\mathsf{m}}_{n,t}(z)-m_{1,t}(z)|\prec(n\eta)^{-1}.

(B.42)

Next, we consider the rectangular matrix DBM of the
spiked
model:

W(t):=W+\sqrt{t}X^{G},\quad\text{with}\quad W:=\Lambda^{1/2}V^{\top}X,

where
\Lambda
contains a non-spiked component
\Lambda_{0}
and
r
spikes as in (
2.3
). Let
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t}:=\Lambda+t
, and we again denote
\Lambda_{t}=\Lambda_{0}+t
and
m_{t}(z)=m(z,\Lambda_{t})
.
Then, we define
\widetilde{G}_{t}
and
\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}
as described in
Remark

B.4
, with the replacements of
W_{0}
,
\Lambda_{0}
, and
m(z)
by
W(t)
,
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t}
, and
m_{t}(z)
, respectively.
Let
\{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}(t)\}_{k=1}^{p}
denote the eigenvalues of
W(t)W(t)^{\top}
. We define a smaller spectral parameter domain than (
B.7
) for arbitrarily small constants
c,\tau\in(0,1)
:

\mathbf{D}^{\prime}\equiv\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(c,\tau,t,n):=\left\{z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbb{C}_{+}:\lambda_{-}-c\leqslant E\leqslant\lambda_{+}+c,n^{-1+\tau}\leqslant\eta\leqslant\tau^{-1}\right\},

(B.43)

We show that a similar local law as in (
B.35
) holds for
\widetilde{G}_{t}-\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}
.

Lemma B.11
.

Suppose Assumption
2.3
holds. Fix a time
T\asymp n^{-1/3+\mathsf{c}}
for some constant
\mathsf{c}\in(0,1/6).
Then, there exists a constant
c>0
such that the following local law holds uniformly for
z\in\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
and
t\in[0,T]
, considering any deterministic unit vectors
{\bf{u}},{\bf{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p+n}
:

\big|{\bf{u}}^{\top}\widetilde{G}_{t}(z){\bf{v}}-{\bf{u}}^{\top}\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}(z){\bf{v}}\big|\prec\Psi_{t}(z).

(B.44)

Moreover, for
i\in[\![{\mathsf{K}-r}]\!]
, we have the eigenvalue sticking estimate:

|\widetilde{\lambda}_{i+r}(t)-\lambda_{i}(t)|\prec n^{-1}.

(B.45)

Proof.

For the proof of (
B.44
), the only concern arises for
z
around the
r
outlier locations. However, by confining ourselves to a narrower spectral domain
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
around
\supp(\varrho_{t})
, we ensure that
z
stays at a distance of order
\Omega(1)
from the outliers. As a result, (
B.44
) is essentially a consequence of the local law (
B.35
). The proof of (
B.45
) is the same as that of (
B.23
). We omit the details.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of the main results

C.1
Proof of the results in Section
2.2

In this subsection, we provide the proofs of Theorem
2.4
,
Corollary

2.5
, and
Corollary

2.6
using the local laws presented in Appendix
B
and
Theorem

2.12
.

Proof of Theorem
2.4
.

Applying
Theorem

2.12
with
\omega_{j}=\ell(\sigma_{j})
and noticing
p^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{r}\ell(\sigma_{j})\phi(\bm{v}_{j},\bm{v}_{j},\gamma_{i-r})=\OO(p^{-1}),
we can deduce that

\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\ell(\Sigma)\bm{u}_{i}-\vartheta(\ell,\gamma_{i-r})\right|>n^{-\mathfrak{d}/4}\right)\leq n^{-\mathfrak{d}/2},\quad i\in[\![{r+1,\mathsf{K}}]\!],

for a constant
\mathfrak{d}>0
. By combining this estimate with the trivial bound
\left|\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\ell(\Sigma)\bm{u}_{i}-\vartheta(\ell,\gamma_{i-r})\right|\lesssim 1
, we conclude (
2.30
).
To show (
2.31
), let
\Gamma_{0}\equiv\Gamma_{0}(n)
be the contour centered at
0
with radius
\rho_{n}=(\log n)^{-1}/2
. By the rigidity estimate (
B.20
), the eigenvalue sticking (
B.23
), and the lower bound (
2.19
), we know that with high probability,
\Gamma_{0}
only encloses the
p-n
zero eigenvalues of
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
.
Then, with the spectral decomposition of
\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}_{1}
(recall (
B.1
)), we obtain that with high probability,

\displaystyle\tr\left[U_{0}^{\top}\ell(\Sigma)U_{0}\right]=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{0}}\tr\big[\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}_{1}(z)\ell(\Sigma)\big]\mathrm{d}z.

Applying the anisotropic law in
Lemma

B.3
and using Cauchy’s integral theorem at
z=0
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\tr\left[U_{0}^{\top}\ell(\Sigma)U_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{0}}\tr\left[(1+m(z)\Sigma)^{-1}\ell(\Sigma)\right]\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{\ell(\widetilde{\sigma}_{j})}{1+m(0)\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}}+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/2}).

This yields (
2.31
) by noticing that

\displaystyle\vartheta(\ell,0)=\frac{1}{p-n}\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\frac{\ell(\widetilde{\sigma}_{j})}{1+m(0)\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}}=\frac{1}{p-n}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{\ell(\widetilde{\sigma}_{j})}{1+m(0)\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}}+\OO(n^{-1}).

Now, we focus on the proof of (
2.29
).
Recall the decomposition (
4.2
):

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\ell(\Sigma)\bm{u}_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\ell(\widetilde{\sigma}_{j})|\langle\bm{u}_{i},\bm{v}_{j}\rangle|^{2}+\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\ell(\widetilde{\sigma}_{j})|\langle\bm{u}_{i},\bm{v}_{j}\rangle|^{2}:=\mathsf{w}_{1i}+\mathsf{w}_{2i},\quad i\in[\![{r}]\!].

(C.1)

Using (
B.22
), we obtain that

\mathsf{w}_{1i}=\frac{\ell(\widetilde{\sigma}_{i})}{\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}}\mathfrak{b}_{i}+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/2}).

(C.2)

The remaining part of the proof is devoted to estimating
\mathsf{w}_{2i}.
Since
\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}=\sigma_{j}
for
j\geqslant r
, by (
B.26
), we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}_{2i}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{c}_{i}\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\frac{\ell(\sigma_{j})}{\sigma_{j}}G_{ij}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})G_{ji}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{c}_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{\ell(\sigma_{j})}{\sigma_{j}}G_{ij}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})G_{ji}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})-\mathfrak{c}_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\ell(\sigma_{j})}{\sigma_{j}}G_{ij}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})G_{ji}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/2}).

Applying (
B.10
) to estimate the second sum on the RHS and recalling the notations in
Remark

B.4
, we can rewrite the above equation as

\mathsf{w}_{2i}=\mathfrak{c}_{i}\mathfrak{a}_{i}^{2}\sigma_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\ell(\sigma_{j})(\mathcal{G}_{1})_{ij}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})(\mathcal{G}_{1})_{ji}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})-\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{i}\sigma_{i}\ell(\sigma_{i})}{[1+m(\mathfrak{a}_{i})\sigma_{i}]^{2}}+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/2}).

(C.3)

It remains to estimate the quantity

\mathcal{L}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\ell(\sigma_{j})(\mathcal{G}_{1})_{ij}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})(\mathcal{G}_{1})_{ji}(\mathfrak{a}_{i}).

(C.4)

For a small
\mathsf{t}>0,
we define a new resolvent:

\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{t},z):=\left(\Lambda_{0}^{1/2}V^{\top}XX^{\top}V\Lambda_{0}^{1/2}-z-\mathsf{t}L\right)^{-1},

(C.5)

where
L
is a diagonal matrix defined as
L=\operatorname{diag}\{\ell(\sigma_{1}),\cdots,\ell(\sigma_{p})\}.

Under this definition, we notice that

\mathcal{L}_{i}=\left.\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}_{ii}(\mathsf{t},\mathfrak{a}_{i})}{\partial\mathsf{t}}\right|_{\mathsf{t}=0}.

(C.6)

To estimate (
C.6
), it suffices to establish a local law on
\mathcal{G}_{ii}(\mathsf{t},\mathfrak{a}_{i})
for any small
\mathsf{t}>0
. We rewrite
\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{t},\mathfrak{a}_{i})
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{t},\mathfrak{a}_{i})

\displaystyle=A(\mathsf{t})^{-1/2}\mathfrak{G}(\mathsf{t},\mathfrak{a}_{i})A(\mathsf{t})^{-1/2},

where
A(\mathsf{t}):=I+\mathsf{t}L/\mathfrak{a}_{i}
and

\mathfrak{G}(\mathsf{t},z):=\left(\Lambda(\mathsf{t})^{1/2}V^{\top}XX^{\top}V\Lambda(\mathsf{t})^{1/2}-z\right)^{-1}\quad\text{with}\quad\Lambda(\mathsf{t}):=A(\mathsf{t})^{-1}\Lambda_{0}.

Now, define
\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{t}}(z,\mathfrak{a}_{i})\equiv m(z,\Lambda(\mathsf{t}))
as in (
2.22
) with
x=\mathfrak{a}_{i}
.
As
\mathsf{t}\to 0
, we can utilize the local law (
B.10
) to
\mathfrak{G}(\mathsf{t},z)
at
z=\mathfrak{a}_{i}
, and substitute
\Lambda_{0}
and
m(z)
with
\Lambda(\sf{t})
and
\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{t}}(z,\mathfrak{a}_{i})
, respectively. This leads to that

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{ii}(\mathsf{t},\mathfrak{a}_{i})

\displaystyle=-\frac{A_{ii}(\mathsf{t})^{-1}}{\mathfrak{a}_{i}[1+\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{t}}(\mathfrak{a}_{i},\mathfrak{a}_{i})\Lambda_{ii}(\mathsf{t})]}+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/2})

(C.7)

for all
\mathsf{t}=\oo(1)
.
We now take
\mathsf{t}=n^{-1/4}
. Using the eigenvalue rigidity (
B.20
) for
\lambda_{1}
and the fact that
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
is an outlier of
\varrho
, we get from the definition (
C.5
) that
\|\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{t},\mathfrak{a}_{i})\|\lesssim 1
with high probability. With this bound, we readily derive that with high probability,

\mathcal{L}_{i}-\frac{\mathcal{G}_{ii}(\mathsf{t},\mathfrak{a}_{i})-\mathcal{G}_{ii}(0,\mathfrak{a}_{i})}{\mathsf{t}}=\OO(\mathsf{t}).

(C.8)

Combining (
C.6
), (
C.7
) and (
C.8
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{i}

\displaystyle=\left.-\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathsf{t}}\frac{A_{ii}(\mathsf{t})^{-1}}{\mathfrak{a}_{i}[1+\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{t}}(\mathfrak{a}_{i},\mathfrak{a}_{i})\Lambda_{ii}(\mathsf{t})]}\right|_{\mathsf{t}=0}+\OO_{\prec}(\mathsf{t}+n^{-1/2}/\mathsf{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{[1+m(\mathfrak{a}_{i})\sigma_{i}]^{2}}\left(\frac{\dot{m}_{0}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})\sigma_{i}}{\mathfrak{a}_{i}}+\frac{\ell(\sigma_{i})}{\mathfrak{a}_{i}^{2}}\right)+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/4}).

Plugging it back into (
C.3
) gives an estimate for
\mathsf{w}_{2i}
. Combining this estimate with (
C.2
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{i}^{\top}\ell(\Sigma)\bm{u}_{i}=\mathfrak{b}_{i}\left(\frac{\ell(\widetilde{\sigma}_{i})}{\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}}+\mathfrak{a}_{i}\dot{m}_{0}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})\right)+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/4}),

where we used that
\mathfrak{c}_{i}={d_{i}^{2}\mathfrak{b}_{i}}/{\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}^{2}}
and
m(\mathfrak{a}_{i})=-\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}^{-1}
. This concludes (
2.29
).
∎

Proof of Corollary
2.5
.

By
Theorem

2.4
, it suffices to simplify the formulas of
\vartheta(x)\equiv\vartheta(\ell,x)
and
\psi_{i}\equiv\psi_{i}(\ell)
when
\ell(x)=x
. When
x=0
, we notice that

\vartheta(0)=\frac{1}{p(1-c_{n}^{-1})}\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\frac{\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}}{1+m(0)\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}}=\frac{1}{c_{n}-1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{\sigma_{j}}{1+m(0)\sigma_{j}}+\OO(n^{-1}).

By (
2.8
), we have the identity

\frac{1}{m(0)}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{\sigma_{j}}{1+m(0)\sigma_{j}}.

This proves (
2.34
).
Next, when
i\in[\![{r+1,\mathsf{K}}]\!]
, we have
\gamma_{i-r}>0
and

\vartheta(\gamma_{i-r})=\frac{c_{n}}{p\gamma_{i-r}}\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\frac{\widetilde{\sigma}^{2}_{j}}{|1+m(\gamma_{i-r})\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}|^{2}}=\frac{1}{n\gamma_{i-r}}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{\sigma^{2}_{j}}{|1+m(\gamma_{i-r})\sigma_{j}|^{2}}+\OO(n^{-1}).

(C.9)

By taking the imaginary parts of both sides of (
2.8
), we obtain that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{\sigma^{2}_{j}}{|1+m(\gamma_{i-r})\sigma_{j}|^{2}}=\frac{1}{|m(\gamma_{i-r})|^{2}}.

Plugging it into (
C.9
) concludes the proof of (
2.33
).
It remains to prove (
2.32
).
We write
\psi_{i}
as

\psi_{i}=\mathfrak{b}_{i}\big(1+\mathfrak{a}_{i}\dot{m}_{0}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})\big).

(C.10)

By setting
\ell(\sigma_{i})=\sigma_{i}
and
z=x=\mathfrak{a}_{i}
, we can simplify (
2.25
) using the first equation in (
2.24
), which yields that

\dot{m}_{0}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{a}_{i}}\left(\frac{m^{\prime}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})}{|m(\mathfrak{a}_{i})|^{2}}-1\right).

Substituting this expression into (
C.10
) concludes (
2.32
).
∎

Proof of Corollary
2.6
.

This corollary follows directly from Lemma
A.1
,
Lemma

A.2
, and Theorem
2.4
.
∎

C.2
Proof of the results in Section
2.3

First, we provide the proof of
Theorem

2.8
by using
Lemma

4.1
and
Lemma

4.2
.

Proof of Theorem
2.8
.

Recall that for
D_{0}=\Lambda-\mathfrak{t}
and
D_{t}=D_{0}+t
, we choose their non-spiked parts as

D_{00}=\Lambda_{0}-\mathfrak{t}
and
D_{t0}=D_{00}+t
, respectively. Then, we define
m_{t}=m(z,D_{t0})
as in (
B.33
) and denote the density function associated with
m_{t}
as
\varrho_{t}
. The quantiles
\gamma_{k}(t)
of
\varrho_{t}
are defined as in
Definition

2.2
. Given that
\mathfrak{t}=\oo(1)
, it is straightforward to show that the eigenvalues of
D_{00}
and
D_{0}
satisfy (iii) and (iv) in
Assumption

2.3
. (In particular, the regularity conditions in (
2.16
) and (
2.17
) can be verified using (
B.34
) and part (i) of
Lemma

B.9
.) Then, combining
Lemma

4.1
and
Lemma

4.2
, we obtain that

\sqrt{p}\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}\langle\bm{v},\bm{u}_{i_{1}+r}\rangle\\
\vdots\\
\xi_{L}\langle\bm{v},\bm{u}_{i_{L}+r}\rangle\end{pmatrix}\simeq\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\Xi_{L}(\mathfrak{t})),

(C.11)

where
\Xi_{L}(\mathfrak{t})
is defined in (
4.6
) with
{\bf{v}}=V^{\top}\bm{v}
(recall that
{\bf{u}}_{i}=V^{\top}\bm{u}_{i}
). Since
D_{\mathfrak{t}}=\Lambda
, by comparing equations (
B.33
) and (
2.8
), we see that
\varrho_{\mathfrak{t}}=\varrho
. Hence, we have
\gamma_{i_{k}}(\mathfrak{t})=\gamma_{i_{k}}
for
k\in[\![{L}]\!]
. Moreover, using the stability of the self-consistent equation (
2.8
) (see Appendix A of
[
45
]
), it is easy to check that

m(x,D_{00})=m(x)+\OO(\sqrt{\mathfrak{t}}).

(C.12)

Then, using the definition (
4.5
), we conclude that

\|\Xi_{L}(\mathfrak{t})-\Xi_{L}\|\lesssim\sqrt{\mathfrak{t}}.

This establishes (
2.37
) together with (
C.11
).
∎

Recall that the proof of
Lemma

4.1
is based on
Theorem

4.5
and
Lemma

4.6
. Using these two results, we can show that the LHS of (
4.7
) converge to the RHS also in the sense of convergence of moments. Then, by applying
Lemma

4.2
and following the above proof of Theorem
2.8
, we can deduce that (
2.37
) holds in terms of matching moments, that is, for any fixed
\mathbf{k}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{L})\in\mathbb{N}^{L}
, there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\prod_{j=1}^{L}\left(p|\langle\bm{v},\bm{u}_{i_{j}+r}\rangle|^{2}\right)^{k_{j}}=\prod_{j=1}^{L}\mathbb{E}\left(|\mathcal{N}_{j}|^{2k_{j}}\right)+\OO(n^{-c}).

(C.13)

Now, we proceed to establish
Theorem

2.12
by utilizing this estimate.
Our approach follows a similar argument employed for Wigner matrices in
[
10
,
15
]
.

Proof of
Theorem

2.12
.

Due to (
B.27
), it suffices to prove the following estimate for
{\bf{u}}_{i}
:

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\Big|\sum_{j=1}^{p}w_{j}\left|{\bf{u}}_{i}(j)\right|^{2}-p^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{p}w_{j}\phi_{j}(\gamma_{i-r})\Big|>{\varepsilon}\bigg)\leqslant n^{-\mathfrak{d}}/{\varepsilon}^{2},

(C.14)

where the function
\phi_{j}(x)
is defined as

\phi_{j}(x):=\phi(\bm{v}_{j},\bm{v}_{j},x)=\frac{c_{n}\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}}{x|1+m(x)\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}|^{2}},\quad x>0.

Applying Markov’s inequality, we can derive (
C.14
) from the following estimate:

p^{-2}\mathbb{E}\Big|\sum_{j=1}^{p}w_{j}\left(p\left|{\bf{u}}_{i}(j)\right|^{2}-\phi_{j}(\gamma_{i-r})\right)\Big|^{2}\leq n^{-\mathfrak{d}}.

(C.15)

We observe that the LHS of (
C.15
) can be bounded by

\max_{j\neq k\in[\![{p}]\!]}\mathbb{E}\left(p\left|{\bf{u}}_{i}(j)\right|^{2}-\phi_{j}(\gamma_{i-r})\right)\left(p\left|{\bf{u}}_{i}(k)\right|^{2}-\phi_{k}(\gamma_{i-r})\right)+p^{-1}\max_{j=1}^{p}\mathbb{E}\left(p\left|{\bf{u}}_{i}(j)\right|^{2}-\phi_{j}(\gamma_{i-r})\right)^{2}.

(C.16)

Using (
C.13
) with
\bm{v}=\bm{v}_{j}
and
k_{j}\in\{1,2\}
, we can conclude that

p\mathbb{E}\left|{\bf{u}}_{i}(j)\right|^{2}=\phi_{j}(\gamma_{i-r})+\OO(n^{-c}),\quad p^{2}\mathbb{E}\left|{\bf{u}}_{i}(j)\right|^{4}=3\phi_{j}(\gamma_{i-r})^{2}+\OO(n^{-c}).

(C.17)

With these two estimates, we can bound the second term in (
C.16
) as

p^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left(p\left|{\bf{u}}_{i}(j)\right|^{2}-\phi_{j}(\gamma_{i-r})\right)^{2}=\OO(p^{-1}),\quad j\in[\![{p}]\!].

(C.18)

It remains to control the first term in (
C.16
).
We now apply (
C.13
) with
L=1
,
i_{1}=i-r
,
k_{1}=2
, and
\bm{v}=(\xi_{1}\bm{v}_{j}+\xi_{2}\bm{v}_{k})/\sqrt{2}
, where
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are two uniformly random signs independent of
X
. This gives that

\displaystyle p^{2}\mathbb{E}\left|{\bf{u}}_{i}(j)\right|^{4}+p^{2}\mathbb{E}\left|{\bf{u}}_{i}(k)\right|^{4}+6p^{2}\mathbb{E}\left(\left|{\bf{u}}_{i}(j)\right|^{2}\left|{\bf{u}}_{i}(k)\right|^{2}\right)=3\left(\phi_{j}(\gamma_{i-r})^{2}+\phi_{k}(\gamma_{i-r})^{2}+2\phi_{j}(\gamma_{i-r})\phi_{k}(\gamma_{i-r})\right).

Together with (
C.17
), this equation implies that

p^{2}\mathbb{E}\left(\left|{\bf{u}}_{i}(j)\right|^{2}\left|{\bf{u}}_{i}(k)\right|^{2}\right)=\phi_{j}(\gamma_{i-r})\phi_{k}(\gamma_{i-r})+\OO(n^{-c}).

(C.19)

Combining (
C.17
) and (
C.19
), we conclude that

\mathbb{E}\left(p\left|{\bf{u}}_{i}(j)\right|^{2}-\phi_{j}(\gamma_{i-r})\right)\left(p\left|{\bf{u}}_{i}(k)\right|^{2}-\phi_{k}(\gamma_{i-r})\right)\lesssim n^{-c}.

(C.20)

Plugging (
C.18
) and (
C.20
) into (
C.16
), we obtain (
C.15
), which further concludes (
C.14
).
∎

C.3
Proof of the results in Section
3

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

The estimate (
3.5
) can be derived from
[
42
, Section 5]
or
[
46
, Section 3]
, along with the eigenvalue sticking estimate (
B.23
).
The first estimate in (
3.6
) follows directly from (
B.21
).

To prove the second estimate in (
3.6
), we apply the inverse function theorem to (
2.8
) and use that
m(\mathfrak{a}_{i})=-\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}^{-1}
, which yields the relation

h^{\prime}(-\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}^{-1})=\left[m^{\prime}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})\right]^{-1}.

(C.21)

Next, considering the contour
\Gamma_{i}
defined below (
B.27
), we apply Cauchy’s integral formula to obtain that

m^{\prime}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{i}}\frac{m(z)}{(z-\mathfrak{a}_{i})^{2}}\mathrm{d}z=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{i}}\frac{\mathsf{m}_{n}(z)}{(z-\mathfrak{a}_{i})^{2}}\mathrm{d}z+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/2})=\mathsf{m}_{n}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/2}).

(C.22)

In the second step, we utilized the local law (
B.10
), and in the third step, we used the fact that, with high probability,
\mathsf{m}_{n}
has no pole inside
\Gamma_{i}
due to the rigidity estimate (
B.20
).
Since
r
is fixed, using (
B.21
) and (
B.23
), we get that

\mathsf{m}_{n}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(\lambda_{j}-{\mathfrak{a}}_{i})^{2}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=r+1}^{n}\frac{1}{(\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}-{\mathfrak{a}}_{i})^{2}}+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1})=\widehat{m}_{i}^{\prime}+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/2}).

(C.23)

Plugging (
C.23
) and (
C.22
) into (
C.21
) concludes the second estimate in (
3.6
).
With a similar argument, employing (
B.10
) and (
B.21
), we can deduce that

-\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}^{-1}=m(\mathfrak{a}_{i})=\mathsf{m}_{n}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/2})=\widehat{m}_{i}+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/2}).

(C.24)

This verifies the third estimate in (
3.6
).

For the last estimate in (
3.6
), we first notice that by (
2.15
) and (
3.5
),

K_{\varepsilon}^{+}=p-\oo(p)

(C.25)

for small enough
{\varepsilon}>0
. Since
m
is a real increasing function outside
\supp(\varrho)
, we have

b_{1}=m(\lambda_{+})<m(\mathfrak{a}_{i})<0.
Combining this with condition (
2.18
), we obtain
\min_{j=r+1}^{p}|1+m(\mathfrak{a}_{i})\sigma_{j}|\gtrsim 1,

which, together with (
C.24
), implies

\min_{j=r+1}^{p}|1+\widehat{m}_{i}\sigma_{j}|\gtrsim 1
with high probability.
Then, from (
3.5
), we see that

K_{{\varepsilon}}^{-}=\oo(p).

(C.26)

On the other hand, by employing the weak convergence in (
3.5
), along with (
B.18
) and (
C.24
)–(
C.26
), we obtain that for small
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{\ell(\sigma_{j})\sigma_{j}}{[1+m(\mathfrak{a}_{i})\sigma_{j}]^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=r+1}^{\lceil(1-\delta)p\rceil}\frac{\ell(\sigma_{j})\sigma_{j}}{(1+m(\mathfrak{a}_{i})\sigma_{j})^{2}+\delta}+\OO(\delta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=r+1}^{\lceil(1-\delta)p\rceil}\frac{\ell(\widehat{\sigma}_{j})\widehat{\sigma}_{j}}{(1+\widehat{m}_{i}\widehat{\sigma}_{j})^{2}+\delta}+\oo_{\mathbb{P}}(1)+\OO(\delta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=(r+1)\vee K_{\varepsilon}^{-}}^{p\wedge K_{\varepsilon}^{+}}\frac{\ell(\widehat{\sigma}_{j})\widehat{\sigma}_{j}}{(1+\widehat{m}_{i}\widehat{\sigma}_{j})^{2}+\delta}+\oo_{\mathbb{P}}(1)+\OO(\delta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=(r+1)\vee K_{\varepsilon}^{-}}^{p\wedge K_{\varepsilon}^{+}}\frac{\ell(\widehat{\sigma}_{j})\widehat{\sigma}_{j}}{(1+\widehat{m}_{i}\widehat{\sigma}_{j})^{2}}+\oo_{\mathbb{P}}(1)+\OO(\delta).

As
\delta
is arbitrary, we conclude from this estimate that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{\ell(\sigma_{j})\sigma_{j}}{[1+m(\mathfrak{a}_{i})\sigma_{j}]^{2}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=(r+1)\vee K_{\varepsilon}^{-}}^{p\wedge K_{\varepsilon}^{+}}\frac{\ell(\widehat{\sigma}_{j})\widehat{\sigma}_{j}}{(1+\widehat{m}_{i}\widehat{\sigma}_{j})^{2}}+\oo_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

(C.27)

Now, plugging (
B.21
), (
C.22
)–(
C.24
), and (
C.27
) into (
2.25
), we obtain that

\dot{m}_{0}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})=\frac{m^{\prime}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})}{n\mathfrak{a}_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{\ell(\sigma_{j})\sigma_{j}}{[1+m(\mathfrak{a}_{i})\sigma_{j}]^{2}}=\frac{\widehat{m}_{i}^{\prime}}{n\widehat{\mathfrak{a}}_{i}}\sum_{j=(r+1)\vee K_{\varepsilon}^{-}}^{p\wedge K_{\varepsilon}^{+}}\frac{\ell(\widehat{\sigma}_{j})\widehat{\sigma}_{j}}{(1+\widehat{m}_{i}\widehat{\sigma}_{j})^{2}}+\oo_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

(C.28)

This concludes the last estimate in (
3.6
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

The estimate (
3.11
) follows immediately from Lemma
3.1
. For the estimate (
3.12
), utilizing the eigenvalue rigidity estimate (
B.20
) and the eigenvalue sticking estimate (
B.23
), we obtain that

|\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}-\gamma_{i-r}|\prec n^{-2/3},\quad i\in[\![{r+1,\mathsf{K}}]\!].

(C.29)

Using (
B.23
) again, we can derive the following expression for
\widehat{m}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i})
when
\eta=n^{-1/2}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{m}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=r+1:|j-i|\geq n^{1/4}}^{n}\frac{1}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}-\mathrm{i}\eta}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=r+1:|j-i|<n^{1/4}}^{n}\frac{1}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}-\mathrm{i}\eta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1:|j+r-i|\geq n^{1/4}}^{n-r}\frac{1}{{\lambda}_{j}-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}-\mathrm{i}\eta}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1:|j+r-i|<n^{1/4}}^{n-r}\frac{1}{{\lambda}_{j}-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}-\mathrm{i}\eta}+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/4})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n-r}\frac{1}{{\lambda}_{j}-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}-\mathrm{i}\eta}+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/4})=\mathsf{m}_{n}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}+\mathrm{i}\eta)+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/4}).

(C.30)

In the second step, we utilize the trivial bound
|{\lambda}_{j}-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}-\mathrm{i}\eta|^{-1}\leq\eta^{-1}
when
|j+r-i|<n^{1/4}
, and when
|j+r-i|\geq n^{1/4}
, we have
|{\lambda}_{j}-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}|\gtrsim n^{-1/4}
with high probability due to the eigenvalue rigidity (
B.20
).
When combined with the averaged local law (
B.9
), the two estimates (
C.29
) and (
C.30
) imply that

\widehat{m}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i})=m(\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}+\mathrm{i}\eta)+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/4})=m(\gamma_{i-r})+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/4}),

(C.31)

where in the second step we used the following bound for any constant
C>0
:

|m(z_{1})-m(z_{2})|\lesssim|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{1/2}\quad\forall\ \ z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{C}_{+},\ C^{-1}\leq|z_{1}|,|z_{2}|\leq C.

(C.32)

(This estimate can be proven by employing the Stieltjes transform form of
m(z)
and the square root behaviors of
\varrho
around the spectral edges.) Similarly, using
Lemma

B.3
, we can derive that

\widehat{m}_{0}=\re m(\mathrm{i}\eta)+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/2})=m(0)+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1/2}).

(C.33)

With the estimates (
C.29
), (
C.31
), and (
C.33
) at hand, the proof of (
3.12
) is then similar to that of the last estimate in (
3.6
), and we omit the details.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.3
.

The estimate (
3.13
) follows from (
3.6
) and (
C.22
)–(
C.24
). The estimate (
3.14
) can be derived from (
C.29
), (
C.31
), and (
C.33
).
∎

Appendix D
Eigenvector moment flow and proof of Lemma
4.10

In this section, we study the dynamics of the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the rectangular DBM and establish Lemma
4.10
. We first introduce some new notations. We denote the processes of eigenvalues and eigenvectors as
\bm{\lambda}(\cdot)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=(\bm{\lambda}(t))_{t\geqslant 0}
and
U(\cdot)=(U(t))_{t\geqslant 0}
, respectively, where
\bm{\lambda}(t)=(\lambda_{1}(t),\ldots,\lambda_{p}(t))
and
U(t)=({\bf{u}}_{1}(t),\ldots,{\bf{u}}_{p}(t))
. We further denote by
\mathbb{E}^{U(0)}_{\bm{\lambda}(\cdot)}(\cdot)
the expectation with respect to the process
U(\cdot)
conditioned on the eigenvalue process
\bm{\lambda}(\cdot)
and the initial state
U(0)
of the eigenvector process.
Let
\mathsf{L}(t)
be the generator associated with the process
U(t)
(or equivalently the dynamics given by (
4.21
)) conditioned on
\bm{\lambda}(\cdot)
and
U(0)
. In other words, for any smooth functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{p^{2}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we have the equation

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\lambda}(\cdot)}^{U(0)}f\bigl({U(t)}\bigr)\;=\;\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\lambda}(\cdot)}^{U(0)}(\mathsf{L}(t)f)\bigl({U(t)}\bigr)\,,

(D.1)

where
(\mathsf{L}(t)f)(U(t))
represents the action of the generator on the function
f
evaluated at
U(t)
.
Finally, we define the
\bm{\lambda}(\cdot)
-measurable
p\times p
matrices
\Upsilon(t)=(\Upsilon_{kl}(t))
with entries introduced in (
4.25
).

Lemma D.1
.

The generator
\mathsf{L}(t)
associated with the eigenvector flow (
4.21
) conditioned on
\bm{\lambda}(\cdot)
and
U(0)
is

\mathsf{L}(t)=\sum_{k,l=1}^{p}\frac{1}{2}\Upsilon_{kl}(t)(\mathsf{X}_{kl})^{2}\,,

(D.2)

where
\mathsf{X}_{kl}
is defined as

\mathsf{X}_{kl}\;\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\;\sum_{j=1}^{p}\biggl({{\bf{u}}_{k}(j)\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf{u}}_{l}(j)}-{\bf{u}}_{l}(j)\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf{u}}_{k}(j)}}\biggr)\,.

(D.3)

Proof.

This result can be proved in the same way as Lemma 2.4 in
[
15
]
, employing (
4.21
) and Itô’s formula. The details are omitted.
∎

Now, we will follow the argument presented in
[
15
, Section 3.1]
to show that
\mathsf{L}(t)
acting on
f_{t}(\xi)
in (
4.22
) can be interpreted as a multi-particle random walk in random environments described by the DBM of eigenvalues. This establishes the desired
eigenvector moment flow
in
Lemma

4.10
. Now, we will derive a more general form of EMF that encompasses
Lemma

4.10
as a special case.
Let
\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{1},\dots,\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{M}
be a deterministic sequence of orthonormal vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
, where
M
is a fixed integer. For
(k,i)\in[\![{p}]\!]\times[\![{M}]\!],
we define the variables
z_{k}^{i}\;\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\;\sqrt{p}\langle{\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{i}}\mspace{2mu},{\bm{\mathrm{u}}_{k}}\rangle
and abbreviate
Z=(z_{k}^{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}(k,i)\in[\![{p}]\!]\times[\![{M}]\!])
. We further define the collection of “particle configurations”

\mathcal{S}_{n}\;\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\;\biggl\{{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{[\![{p}]\!]\times[\![{M}]\!]}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\xi_{k}^{i}\in 2\mathbb{Z}\text{\rm{ for all $k\in[\![{p}]\!]$}}}\biggr\}\,.

(D.4)

The special basis elements
e_{k}^{i}
,
(k,i)\in[\![{p}]\!]\times[\![{M}]\!],
for the configurations in
\mathcal{S}_{n}
are defined as
(e_{k}^{i})_{l}^{j}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\delta_{kl}\delta_{ij}
.
For each
\xi\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
, we assign a polynomial
P_{\xi}
of the variables in
Z
, defined as follows:

P_{\xi}\;\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\;\prod_{k=1}^{p}\prod_{i=1}^{M}(z_{k}^{i})^{\xi_{k}^{i}}

(D.5)

if
\xi_{k}^{i}\geqslant 0
for all
(k,i)\in[\![{p}]\!]\times[\![{M}]\!]
, and
P_{\xi}=0
otherwise. With the above notations, we can derive the following key result through direct calculations.

Lemma D.2
.

For
k,l\in[\![{p}]\!]
and
i\in[\![{M}]\!]
, define the operator
A_{kl}^{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\mathcal{S}_{n}\to\mathcal{S}_{n}
through

A_{kl}^{i}\xi\;\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\;\xi-e_{k}^{i}+e_{l}^{i}\,.

(D.6)

Then, we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}(t)P_{\xi}\;=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l}\Upsilon_{kl}(t)\sum_{i}\Bigl[{\xi_{k}^{i}(\xi_{k}^{i}-1)P_{A_{kl}^{i}A_{kl}^{i}\xi}-\xi_{k}^{i}(\xi_{l}^{i}+1)P_{\xi}}\Bigr]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l}\Upsilon_{kl}(t)\sum_{i\neq j}\Bigl[{\xi_{k}^{i}\xi_{k}^{j}P_{A_{kl}^{i}A_{kl}^{j}\xi}-\xi_{k}^{i}\xi_{l}^{j}P_{A_{kl}^{i}A_{lk}^{j}\xi}}\Bigr]\,.

(D.7)

Proof.

For any differentiable function
f(Z)
of
Z
, by applying the chain rule to
z_{k}^{i}=\sqrt{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{i}(j)\bm{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(j)
, we obtain that

\sum_{j=1}^{p}{\bf{u}}_{k}(j)\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf{u}}_{l}(j)}f\;=\;\sum_{i=1}^{M}z_{k}^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{l}^{i}}f\,.

(D.8)

On the other hand, using the definition in (
D.5
), we obtain that
