z_{k}^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{l}^{i}}P_{\xi}\;=\;\xi_{l}^{i}P_{\xi+e_{k}^{i}-e_{l}^{i}}\,.

(D.9)

Now, using the above two identities, we can derive the following equation for
k\not\sim l
:

\displaystyle(\mathsf{X}_{kl})^{2}P_{\xi}\;=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\Bigl[\xi_{k}^{i}(\xi_{k}^{i}-1)P_{\xi+2e_{l}^{i}-2e_{k}^{i}}+\xi_{l}^{i}(\xi_{l}^{i}-1)P_{\xi+2e_{k}^{i}-2e_{l}^{i}}-\bigl({\xi_{k}^{i}(\xi_{l}^{i}+1)+\xi_{l}^{i}(\xi_{k}^{i}+1)}\bigr)P_{\xi}\Bigr]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq j}\Bigl[\xi_{l}^{i}\xi_{l}^{j}P_{\xi+e_{k}^{i}+e_{k}^{j}-e_{l}^{i}-e_{l}^{j}}+\xi_{k}^{i}\xi_{k}^{j}P_{\xi+e_{l}^{i}+e_{l}^{j}-e_{k}^{i}-e_{k}^{j}}-\xi_{l}^{i}\xi_{k}^{j}P_{\xi+e_{k}^{i}+e_{l}^{j}-e_{l}^{i}-e_{k}^{j}}-\xi_{k}^{i}\xi_{l}^{j}P_{\xi+e_{l}^{i}+e_{k}^{j}-e_{k}^{i}-e_{l}^{j}}\Bigr]\,.

Finally, utilizing the symmetry
\Upsilon_{kl}(t)=\Upsilon_{lk}(t)
, this equation yields that

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}(t)P_{\xi}\;=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l}\Upsilon_{kl}(t)\sum_{i}\Bigl[{\xi_{k}^{i}(\xi_{k}^{i}-1)P_{\xi+2e_{l}^{i}-2e_{k}^{i}}-\xi_{k}^{i}(\xi_{l}^{i}+1)P_{\xi}}\Bigr]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l}\Upsilon_{kl}(t)\sum_{i\neq j}\Bigl[{\xi_{k}^{i}\xi_{k}^{j}P_{\xi+e_{l}^{i}+e_{l}^{j}-e_{k}^{i}-e_{k}^{j}}-\xi_{k}^{i}\xi_{l}^{j}P_{\xi+e_{l}^{i}+e_{k}^{j}-e_{k}^{i}-e_{l}^{j}}}\Bigr]\,.

We then complete the proof by substituting the definition (
D.6
) into this equation.
∎

Remark D.3
.

In light of Lemma
D.2
, the generator
\mathsf{L}(t)
acting on
P_{\xi}
can interpreted as a colored multi-particle random walk in random environments. In this process, particles move on the lattice
[\![{p}]\!]
, with each particle carrying a color from the set
[\![{M}]\!]
. The particles at each site in
[\![{p}]\!]
are unordered.
The particle configuration is encoded by
\xi\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
, where
\xi_{k}^{i}
represents the number of particles of color
i
at site
k
. The operator
A_{kl}^{i}
describes the jump of a particle with color
i
from site
k
to site
l
.
Thus, on the RHS of (
D.7
), the first term corresponds to moving two particles of the same color from site
k
to site
l
, the third term represents moving two particles of different colors from site
k
to site
l
, and the last term involves exchanging two particles of different colors between site
k
and site
l
. The second term on the right-hand side of (
D.7
) ensures that
\mathsf{L}
acts as the generator of a continuous-time Markov process. Note that under the dynamics described by (
D.7
), every site
k
always contains an even number of particles, and the total number of particles of a given color remains conserved.
By applying the techniques developed in
Appendix

E
below, we can extend our analysis to the more intricate case of a colored EMF, leading to the derivation of the more general results presented in (
2.38
). However, in the interest of simplicity, we do not delve into these details within the scope of this paper and defer their exploration to future work.

Proof of
Lemma

4.10
.

Note that
f(\xi)
can be written as

f_{t}(\xi)\;\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\;\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\lambda}(\cdot)}^{U(0)}Q_{\xi}(U(t))\,,

where
Q_{\xi}
is defined as

Q_{\xi}\;\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\;P_{\xi}\prod_{k=i_{0}}^{\mathsf{K}}\frac{1}{(2\xi(k)-1)!!},\quad\text{with}\quad P_{\xi}\;=\;\prod_{k=i_{0}}^{\mathsf{K}}z_{k}(t)^{2\xi(k)}\,.

Then,
Lemma

4.10
is a simple consequence of Lemmas
D.1
and
D.2
by taking
M=1
and
\xi_{k}^{1}=2\xi(k)
.
∎

Appendix E
Proof of Theorem
4.5

This section is dedicated to proving a key technical result of this paper—Theorem
4.5
. As demonstrated in Lemma
4.10
and
Appendix

D
, the EMF
f_{t}(\xi)
can be viewed as a multi-particle random walk in a random environment with generator (
D.2
).
Through a careful analysis of the EMF, we will show that
f_{t}(\xi)
relaxes to the “equilibrium state”
g_{t}(\xi,{\varepsilon}_{1})
on the time scale
t\gg n^{-1/3}
. Towards the end of this section, we will also briefly discuss how to extend our arguments to the scenario where the eigenvectors are projected onto multiple distinct directions.

The following proposition is an analog of Proposition 3.2 in
[
15
]
. Recall that a measure
\pi
on the configuration space is said to be
reversible
with respect to a generator
\mathsf{L}
if, for any functions
\mathrm{f}
and
\mathrm{g}
, the following equality holds:

\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\mathrm{g}(\xi)\mathsf{L}\mathrm{f}(\xi)=\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\mathrm{f}(\xi)\mathsf{L}\mathrm{g}(\xi).

We then define the Dirichlet form with respect to the reversible measure
\pi
as

\mathsf{D}^{\pi}(\mathrm{f})=-\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\mathrm{f}(\xi)\mathsf{L}\mathrm{f}(\xi).

Proposition E.1

(Proposition 3.2 of
[
15
]
)
.

Define the measure
\pi
on the configuration space by assigning the following weight to each
\xi=(\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{p})
:

\pi(\xi)=\prod_{i=1}^{p}\mathfrak{L}(\xi_{i}),\quad\mathfrak{L}(k):=\prod_{i=1}^{k}(1-(2i)^{-1}).

(E.1)

Then,
\pi
is a reversible measure for
\mathscr{B}
defined in (
4.24
), yielding that

-\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\mathrm{g}(\xi)\mathscr{B}\mathrm{f}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\sum_{l\not\sim k}\Upsilon_{kl}\xi(k)(1+2\xi(l))\bigl({\mathrm{f}(\xi^{k\to l})-\mathrm{f}(\xi)}\bigr)\bigl({\mathrm{g}(\xi^{k\to l})-\mathrm{g}(\xi)}\bigr).

In addition, the reversibility of
\pi
remains valid for the generator
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
obtained by replacing
\Upsilon
in
\mathscr{B}
with any other symmetric matrix
\Upsilon^{\prime}
, i.e.,
\Upsilon_{kl}^{\prime}=\Upsilon_{lk}^{\prime}
.

Without loss of generality, in the following proof, we will focus on the case where

i_{0}=1\quad\text{and}\quad c_{n}<1.

(E.2)

In simpler terms, we assume that our model is free from outliers or trivial eigenvalues at 0.
In fact, under
Assumption

2.3
, the outlier eigenvalues
\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}
,
i\in[\![{r}]\!]
, and the trivial eigenvalues at 0 are away from
\{\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}\}_{i=i_{0}}^{\mathsf{K}}
with a distance of order 1. Leveraging the finite speed of propagation estimate established in Lemma C.14 of
[
31
]
, we can show that the influence of the eigenvalues/eigenvectors corresponding to the outliers and the trivial zero eigenvalues on
f_{t}(\xi)
is exponentially small. Consequently, the proof of the general case without assuming (
1.6
) can be concluded by following the arguments outlined in Remarks B.15 and C.2 of
[
31
]
.

Given a small constant
\delta>0,
we introduce non-negative cutoff functions
\theta_{-,\delta}
supported on
[\lambda_{-}/3,\infty)
and
{\theta}_{+,\delta}
supported on
(-\infty,3\lambda_{+}]
, satisfying the following properties:

\theta_{-,\delta}(x)=\begin{cases}0,&x<\lambda_{-}/3\\
1,&x\geqslant 2\lambda_{-}/3\end{cases},\qquad\theta_{+,\delta}=\begin{cases}0,&x>3\lambda_{+}\\
1,&x\leqslant 2\lambda_{+}\end{cases};

(E.3)

the derivatives of
\theta_{-}
and
\theta_{+}
satisfy that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle 0\leq\theta_{-,\delta}^{\prime}(x)\lesssim n^{\delta}\theta_{-,\delta}(x)+\exp(-n^{\delta/2}),\quad|\theta_{-,\delta}^{\prime\prime}(x)|\lesssim n^{2\delta}\theta_{-,\delta}(x)+\exp(-n^{\delta/2}),

(E.4)

\displaystyle 0\leq-\theta_{+,\delta}^{\prime}(x)\lesssim n^{\delta}\theta_{+,\delta}(x)+\exp(-n^{\delta/2}),\quad|\theta_{+,\delta}^{\prime\prime}(x)|\lesssim n^{2\delta}\theta_{+,\delta}(x)+\exp(-n^{\delta/2}).

As an example, we can consider the function
\theta_{-,\delta}
defined as

\theta_{-,\delta}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(n^{\delta}(x-\lambda_{-}/2)\right)\quad\text{for}\quad\lambda_{-}/2-n^{-\delta/3}\leq x\leq\lambda_{-}/2.

Then, we properly choose the value of
\theta_{-,\delta}
on
[\lambda_{-}/3,\lambda_{-}/2-n^{-\delta/3}]
and
[\lambda_{-}/2,2\lambda_{-}/3]
such that (
E.3
) and (
E.4
) hold. We can choose the function
\theta_{+,\delta}
in a similar way. We are now prepared to prove Theorem
4.5
.

Proof of Theorem
4.5
.

Let
{\varepsilon}>0
be a sufficiently small constant. We choose
g_{t}(\xi,{\varepsilon})
as defined in (
4.17
), along with the cutoff functions
\theta_{\pm,{\varepsilon}}
. Next, we introduce the function

F_{t}\equiv F_{t}(L,\mathsf{q},{\varepsilon}):=\theta_{-,{\varepsilon}}(\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}(t))\theta_{+,{\varepsilon}}(\lambda_{1}(t))\sum_{|\xi|=L}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}_{t}(\xi,{\varepsilon})^{\mathsf{q}},

(E.5)

where
\mathsf{q}\in 2\mathbb{N}
is an even integer,
\pi
is defined in (
E.1
), and

\widetilde{f}_{t}(\xi,{\varepsilon}):=f_{t}(\xi)-g_{t}(\xi,{\varepsilon}).

To conclude the proof, it suffices to show that for any fixed
K\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathsf{p}\in 2\mathbb{N}
, there exist constants
0<{\varepsilon}<\tilde{\varepsilon}
such that the following estimate holds with high probability:

\sup_{t\in[T_{1}/2,T_{2}]}F_{t}(K,\mathsf{p},\tilde{\varepsilon})\lesssim n^{-{\varepsilon}\mathsf{p}+K}.

(E.6)

Given constants
0<{\varepsilon}^{\prime}<{\varepsilon}
,
L\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathsf{q}\in 2\mathbb{N}
, we define the function
\mathcal{A}_{t}(L,\mathsf{q},{\varepsilon},{\varepsilon}^{\prime}):=\log(F_{t}(L,\mathsf{q},{\varepsilon})+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L})
.
Then, the estimate (
E.6
) can be derived from the following technical lemma on
\mathcal{A}_{t}
, the proof of which is deferred to Section
E.1
.

Lemma E.2
.

Fix any
L\in\mathbb{N}
. Under the assumptions of Theorem
4.5
, suppose there exist constants
\tilde{\varepsilon}>0
,
{\varepsilon}\in(0,\min\{\tilde{\varepsilon},1/12\})
, and a fixed integer
\mathsf{p}_{0}\in 2\mathbb{N}
, such that (
E.6
) holds with high probability, uniformly in
t\in[T_{1}/2,T_{2}]
, for
K=L-1
and any fixed even integer
\mathsf{p}\geq\mathsf{p}_{0}
. Then, there exists a constant
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\in(0,\min\{{\varepsilon},3\mathsf{c}/8\})
and a fixed
\mathsf{q}_{0}\in 2\mathbb{N}
such that the function
\mathcal{A}_{t}(L,\mathsf{q},{\varepsilon},{\varepsilon}^{\prime})
satisfies the following SDE for any fixed
0<{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\leq{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathsf{q}\geq\mathsf{q}_{0}
:

\mathrm{d}\mathcal{A}_{t}(L,\mathsf{q},{\varepsilon},{\varepsilon}^{\prime})=S(t)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t)\mathrm{d}B(t),

(E.7)

where
B(t)
denotes a standard Brownian motion,
S(t)
can be expressed as

S(t)=-C(t)\frac{F_{t}}{F_{t}+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}-s_{+}(t)+\mathcal{E}(t),

and the following estimates hold with high probability, uniformly in
t\in[T_{1}/2,T_{2}]
:
\sigma(t)=\OO(n^{-1/2+2{\varepsilon}})
,
C(t)\gtrsim n^{1/3-\mathsf{c}/4}
,
s_{+}(t)\geq 0
, and
\mathcal{E}(t)=\OO(n^{2{\varepsilon}})
.

We now proceed to prove (
E.6
) by utilizing Lemma
E.2
. First, we note that (
E.6
) trivially holds for
K=0
since
F_{t}(0,\mathsf{p},{\varepsilon}_{0})=0
. Now, suppose (
E.6
) holds for
K=L-1
and any fixed even integer
\mathsf{p}\geq\mathsf{p}_{0}
. Then, by applying
Lemma

E.2
, we can deduce that the equation (
E.7
) holds for some constant
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}>0
and
\mathsf{q}\geq\mathsf{q}_{0}
.
By applying the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality to the diffusion term in (
E.7
), we obtain that the following event holds with high probability:

\left\{\mathcal{A}_{t}\leq{\mathcal{A}}_{s}-\alpha\int_{s}^{t}\frac{F_{t^{\prime}}}{F_{t^{\prime}}+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}+\OO\left(n^{-{1}/{3}+2{\varepsilon}+\mathsf{c}}\right)\ \forall t,s\in[T_{1}/2,T_{2}]\right\},

(E.8)

where
\alpha=cn^{1/3-\mathsf{c}/4}
for a small constant
c>0
. Note that, according to the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{t}
, we have the deterministic rough bound
\mathcal{A}_{s}\leq C\log n
for a constant
C>0
. Therefore, the above event implies that

\left\{{\mathcal{A}}_{t}\leq\min({\mathcal{A}}_{T_{1}/2},C\log n)-\alpha\int_{T_{1}/2}^{t}\frac{F_{s}}{F_{s}+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\mathrm{d}s+\oo(1)\ \forall t\in[T_{1}/2,T_{2}]\right\}

(E.9)

holds with high probability. Now, we define the following stopping time (with the convention
\inf\emptyset=T_{2}
):

\tilde{t}:=(T_{1}/2)\vee\inf\{t\in[T_{1}/2,T_{2}]:\mathcal{A}_{t}\leq\log(2n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L})\}.

Noticing that
{F_{t}}/(F_{t}+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L})\geq 1/2
for
t\in[T_{1}/2,\tilde{t}]
, on the event (
E.9
), we have

\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{t}}\leq\min(\mathcal{A}_{T_{1}/2},C\log n)-\frac{\alpha}{2}(\tilde{t}-T_{1}/2)+\oo(1)\quad\text{w.h.p.}

Since
\alpha T_{1}/2\gtrsim n^{3\mathsf{c}/4}\gg\log n
, this estimate implies that

\tilde{t}\leq T_{1}\quad\text{w.h.p.}

(E.10)

Now, we apply this fact to (
E.8
) with
s=\tilde{t}
and conclude that

\sup_{t\in[T_{1},T_{2}]}{\mathcal{A}}_{t}\leq{\mathcal{A}}_{\tilde{t}}+1\quad\text{w.h.p.}

(E.11)

Together with the definition of
F_{t}
, this implies that with high probability,

\sup_{t\in[T_{1},T_{2}]}F_{t}\leq(2e-1)n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L},

which completes the induction argument and establishes (
E.6
).

Now, given (
E.6
) with
K=L
and
0<{\varepsilon}<\tilde{\varepsilon}
, we choose
{\varepsilon}_{1}=\tilde{\varepsilon}
and
{\varepsilon}_{0}={\varepsilon}/2
and let
\mathsf{p}
be sufficiently large so that
-{\varepsilon}\mathsf{p}+L<-{\varepsilon}_{0}\mathsf{p}
.
This allows us to obtain the following inequality:

F_{t}(L,\mathsf{p},{\varepsilon}_{1})=\theta_{-,{\varepsilon}_{1}}(\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}(t))\theta_{+,{\varepsilon}_{1}}(\lambda_{1}(t))\sum_{|\xi|=L}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}_{t}(\xi,{\varepsilon}_{1})^{\mathsf{p}}\ll n^{-{\varepsilon}_{0}\mathsf{p}}\quad\text{w.h.p.}

By utilizing the rigidity estimate (
B.37
) for
\lambda_{1}(t)
and
\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}(t)
, and considering the definitions of
\theta_{\pm,{\varepsilon}_{1}}
, we can deduce that
\theta_{-,{\varepsilon}_{1}}(\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}(t))=\theta_{+,{\varepsilon}_{1}}(\lambda_{1}(t))=1
with high probability. Consequently, taking into account the fact that
\pi(\xi)
are all of order 1, the above estimate implies that

\widetilde{f}_{t}(\xi,{\varepsilon}_{1})^{\mathsf{p}}\leq n^{-{\varepsilon}_{0}\mathsf{p}}\quad\text{w.h.p.}

for any configuration
\xi
with
|\xi|=L
. This concludes the proof of
Theorem

4.5
.

∎

E.1
Proof of Lemma
E.2

In this subsection, we present the proof of Lemma
E.2
. By Itô’s formula, we have

d{\mathcal{A}}_{t}=\frac{\mathrm{d}F_{t}}{F_{t}+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}-\frac{\mathrm{d}[F]_{t}}{2(F_{t}+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L})^{2}},

where the second term on the RHS is negative. Hence, it suffices to prove that the following equation holds for
t\in[T_{1}/2,T_{2}]
:

\frac{\mathrm{d}F_{t}}{F_{t}+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}=-C(t)\frac{F_{t}}{F_{t}+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\mathrm{d}t-s_{+}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{E}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t)\mathrm{d}B(t),

(E.12)

where
s_{+}(t)\geq 0
and
C(t)
,
\mathcal{E}(t)
,
\sigma(t)
satisfy the desired estimates stated in
Lemma

E.2
.

For simplicity of notation, in the following proof, we will abbreviate
F\equiv F_{t}
,
f\equiv f_{t}
,
g\equiv g_{t}
,
\widetilde{f}\equiv\widetilde{f}_{t}
,
\lambda_{k}\equiv\lambda_{k}(t)
,
{\bf{u}}_{k}\equiv{\bf{u}}_{k}(t)
,
\phi(\lambda_{k}(t),{\varepsilon})\equiv\phi(\lambda_{k})
,
\theta_{-}\equiv\theta_{-,{\varepsilon}}(\lambda_{\mathsf{K}})
,
\theta_{+}\equiv\theta_{+,{\varepsilon}}(\lambda_{1})
, and
\mathsf{m}_{n}\equiv\mathsf{m}_{n,t}
,
\underline{\mathsf{m}}_{n}\equiv\underline{\mathsf{m}}_{n,t}
(where we adopt the notations in
Section

B.3
with
\Lambda_{0}=D_{00}
and
\Lambda_{t}=D_{00}+t
). Using (
4.20
) and Itô’s formula, we get that

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\phi(\lambda_{k})=\phi^{\prime}(\lambda_{k})\mathrm{d}\lambda_{k}+\phi^{\prime\prime}(\lambda_{k})\frac{2\lambda_{k}}{n}\mathrm{d}t,

(E.13)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}g(\xi)=\sum_{k}\Big(\partial_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)\Big)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{k}+\sum_{k}\Big(\partial^{2}_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)\Big)\frac{2\lambda_{k}}{n}\mathrm{d}t,

(E.14)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\theta_{-}=\theta_{-}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}+\theta_{-}^{\prime\prime}\frac{2\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}}{n}\mathrm{d}t,\quad\mathrm{d}\theta_{+}=\theta_{+}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}+\theta_{+}^{\prime\prime}\frac{2\lambda_{1}}{n}\mathrm{d}t.

(E.15)

Note that by the definition of
g
in (
4.17
) and the estimate (
4.18
), we have

\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\partial_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)=\OO(n^{{\varepsilon}}),\quad\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\partial^{2}_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)=\OO(n^{2{\varepsilon}}).

(E.16)

Now, applying Itô’s formula to
F
defined in (
E.5
) and using (
E.13
)–(
E.15
), we can derive that

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}F=

\displaystyle\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}}\left(\theta_{-}\theta_{+}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}+\theta_{-}\theta_{+}^{\prime\prime}\frac{2\lambda_{1}}{n}\mathrm{d}t+\theta_{-}^{\prime}\theta_{+}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}+\theta_{-}^{\prime\prime}\theta_{+}\frac{2\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}}{n}\mathrm{d}t\right)

(E.17)

\displaystyle+\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\left(\mathsf{q}\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}(\mathrm{d}f(\xi)-\mathrm{d}g(\xi))+\mathsf{q}(\mathsf{q}-1)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-2}\sum_{k}\big(\partial_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)\big)^{2}\frac{2\lambda_{k}}{n}\mathrm{d}t\right)

(E.18)

\displaystyle-\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\left(\mathsf{q}\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}\right)\left(\partial_{\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}}g(\xi)\theta_{-}^{\prime}\theta_{+}\frac{4\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}}{n}+\partial_{\lambda_{1}}g(\xi)\theta_{-}\theta_{+}^{\prime}\frac{4\lambda_{1}}{n}\right)\mathrm{d}t.

(E.19)

Here, we have used the convention that
\sum_{\xi}\equiv\sum_{\xi:|\xi|=L}.
Next, we will control these terms one by one.

First, considering the term (
E.17
), we utilize (
E.3
) and (
E.4
) with
\delta={\varepsilon}
, along with the SDE (
4.20
), to obtain that

\displaystyle\theta_{-}\theta_{+}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}+\theta_{-}\theta_{+}^{\prime\prime}\frac{2\lambda_{1}}{n}\mathrm{d}t=

\displaystyle\theta_{-}\theta_{+}^{\prime}2\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}\frac{\mathrm{d}B_{11}}{\sqrt{n}}+\theta_{-}\theta_{+}^{\prime}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k\neq 1}\frac{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{k}}\bigg)\mathrm{d}t

(E.20)

\displaystyle+\OO\left(n^{-1+2{\varepsilon}}\theta_{-}\theta_{+}+\exp(-n^{{\varepsilon}/2})\right)\mathrm{d}t.

It then follows from (
E.20
) and the bound (
E.4
) that

\sum_{\xi}\frac{\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\left(\theta_{-}\theta_{+}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}+\theta_{-}\theta_{+}^{\prime\prime}\frac{2\lambda_{1}}{n}\mathrm{d}t\right)=\OO(n^{-1+2{\varepsilon}})\mathrm{d}t+\OO(n^{-1/2+{\varepsilon}})\mathrm{d}B_{11}-s_{+}^{(1)}(t)\mathrm{d}t,

(E.21)

where
s_{+}^{(1)}(t)
represents a positive term arising from the second term on the RHS of (
E.20
). Using a similar argument, we can also show that

\displaystyle\sum_{\xi}\frac{\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\left(\theta_{-}^{\prime}\theta_{+}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}+\theta_{-}^{\prime\prime}\theta_{+}\frac{2\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}}{n}\mathrm{d}t\right)

\displaystyle=\OO(n^{-1+2{\varepsilon}})\mathrm{d}t+\OO(n^{-1/2+{\varepsilon}})\mathrm{d}B_{\mathsf{K}\mathsf{K}}+\frac{\theta_{-}^{\prime}\theta_{+}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k\neq\mathsf{K}}\frac{\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}+\lambda_{k}}{\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}-\lambda_{k}}\bigg)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\OO(n^{-1+2{\varepsilon}})\mathrm{d}t+\OO(n^{-1/2+{\varepsilon}})\mathrm{d}B_{\mathsf{K}\mathsf{K}}-s_{+}^{(2)}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\left(1+\frac{p-\mathsf{K}}{n}\right)\frac{\theta_{-}^{\prime}\theta_{+}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\mathrm{d}t,

where
s_{+}^{(2)}(t)
represents another positive term.
We can exploit the rigidity of
\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}
given by (
B.37
) and the fact that
|\lambda_{-,t}-\lambda_{-}|=\oo(1)
(as stated in (i) of
Lemma

B.9
) to conclude that
\theta_{-}^{\prime}=0
with high probability, uniformly in
t\in[T_{1}/2,T_{2}]
. Consequently, we obtain a bound similar to (
E.21
):

\sum_{\xi}\frac{\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\left(\theta_{-}^{\prime}\theta_{+}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}+\theta_{-}^{\prime\prime}\theta_{+}\frac{2\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}}{n}\mathrm{d}t\right)=\mathcal{E}^{(2)}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\OO(n^{-1/2+{\varepsilon}})\mathrm{d}B_{\mathsf{K}\mathsf{K}}-s_{+}^{(2)}(t)\mathrm{d}t,

(E.22)

where
\mathcal{E}^{(2)}(t)
is a variable satisfying
\mathcal{E}^{(2)}(t)=\OO(n^{-1+2{\varepsilon}})
with high probability, uniformly in
t\in[T_{1}/2,T_{2}]
.

Next, we control (
E.19
). For any
0<l<\mathsf{q}
, we have the trivial bound

|\widetilde{f}(\xi)|^{\mathsf{q}-l}\lesssim n^{l{\varepsilon}}\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}}+n^{-(\mathsf{q}-l){\varepsilon}}.

(E.23)

Utilizing (
E.23
) and the estimates (
E.16
) and (
E.4
), we get that

\sum_{\xi}\frac{\pi(\xi)\mathsf{q}\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\left(\partial_{\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}}g(\xi)\theta_{-}^{\prime}\theta_{+}\frac{4\lambda_{\mathsf{K}}}{n}+\partial_{\lambda_{1}}g(\xi)\theta_{+}^{\prime}\theta_{-}\frac{4\lambda_{1}}{n}\right)\mathrm{d}t=\OO(n^{-1+3\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}t.

(E.24)

Similarly, the second term of (
E.18
) can be bounded by

\mathsf{q}(\mathsf{q}-1)\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\sum_{\xi}\frac{\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-2}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\sum_{k}\left(\partial_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)\right)^{2}\frac{2\lambda_{k}}{n}\mathrm{d}t=\OO(n^{-1+4\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}t.

(E.25)

Combing (
E.21
), (
E.22
), (
E.24
), and (
E.25
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}F}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{q}\theta_{-}\theta_{+}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}(\mathrm{d}f(\xi)-\mathrm{d}g(\xi))

(E.26)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{E}_{1}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\OO(n^{-1/2+{\varepsilon}})\mathrm{d}B_{11}+\OO(n^{-1/2+{\varepsilon}})\mathrm{d}B_{\mathsf{K}\mathsf{K}}-(s_{+}^{(1)}(t)+s_{+}^{(2)}(t))\mathrm{d}t,

where
\mathcal{E}_{1}(t)
is a variable satisfying
\mathcal{E}_{1}(t)=\OO(n^{-1+4{\varepsilon}})
with high probability, uniformly in
t\in[T_{1}/2,T_{2}]
.

Now, to conclude (
E.12
), it remains to estimate the first term on the RHS of (
E.26
).
By (
4.26
), we express the term as

\displaystyle\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}\mathrm{d}f(\xi)=\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}\sum_{k\not\sim l}\Upsilon_{kl}(t)\,\xi(k)(1+2\xi(l))\bigl({f(\xi^{k\to l})-f(\xi)}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t.

(E.27)

On the other hand, using (
4.20
) and (
E.14
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}\mathrm{d}g(\xi)=\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}

(E.28)

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{k}\bigg[\partial_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)\sum_{l:l\not\sim k}\frac{\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{l}}{n(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{l})}\mathrm{d}t+\partial_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)\mathrm{d}t+\partial^{2}_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)\frac{2\lambda_{k}}{n}\mathrm{d}t+(\partial_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi))2\sqrt{\lambda}_{k}\frac{\mathrm{d}B_{kk}}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg].

The diffusion part of the above equation has the same law as

\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\bigg[\sum_{k}\bigg|\sum_{\xi}\Big(\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}\left(\partial_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)\right)\frac{2\sqrt{\lambda}_{k}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)\bigg|^{2}\bigg]^{1/2}\mathrm{d}B,

where
B
is a standard Brownian motion.
Note that the coefficient satisfies that

\displaystyle\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\bigg[\sum_{k}\bigg|\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}\left(\partial_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)\right)\frac{2\sqrt{\lambda}_{k}}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg|^{2}\bigg]^{1/2}=\OO(n^{-1/2+{\varepsilon}})\cdot\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)|\widetilde{f}(\xi)|^{\mathsf{q}-1}.

(E.29)

Here, we have used (
E.16
) and the facts that
\pi(\xi)\asymp 1
and
\partial_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)
is non-zero for at most
L
many
k
’s. Plugging (
E.27
)–(
E.29
) into (
E.26
) and using (
E.23
) and (
E.16
), we can deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}F}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}=

\displaystyle~\sum_{\xi}\frac{\mathsf{q}\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\sum_{k\not\sim l}\Bigg(\Upsilon_{kl}\,\xi(k)(1+2\xi(l))\bigl({f(\xi^{k\to l})-f(\xi)}\bigr)-\frac{(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{l})\partial_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)}{n(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{l})}\Bigg)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle~+\mathcal{E}_{2}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\OO(n^{-1/2+2{\varepsilon}})\mathrm{d}\widetilde{B}-(s_{+}^{(1)}(t)+s_{+}^{(2)}(t))\mathrm{d}t,

(E.30)

where
\widetilde{B}
is another standard Brownian motion and
\mathcal{E}_{2}(t)
is a variable satisfying
\mathcal{E}_{2}(t)=\OO(n^{2{\varepsilon}})
with high probability, uniformly in
t\in[T_{1}/2,T_{2}]
.

Taking
\eta=n^{-2/3+\mathsf{c}/2}
, we define

\mathrm{T}_{kl}\equiv\mathrm{T}_{kl}(\eta)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\mathbf{1}(k\not\sim l)\frac{(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{l})^{2}}{(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{l})^{2}+\eta^{2}},\quad\mathrm{T}_{kl}^{c}:=\mathbf{1}(k\not\sim l)-\mathrm{T}_{kl}.

Then, we decompose the first term on the RHS of (
E.30
) as

\mathsf{q}(F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L})^{-1}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}\left(A_{1}+A_{2}+A_{3}\right)\mathrm{d}t,

(E.31)

where
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
, and
A_{3}
are defined as

\displaystyle A_{1}:=\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\sum_{k\not\sim l}\Upsilon_{kl}\mathrm{T}_{kl}\xi(k)(1+2\xi(l))\bigl({f(\xi^{k\to l})-f(\xi)}\bigr),

(E.32)

\displaystyle A_{2}:=-\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\sum_{k\not\sim l}\mathrm{T}_{kl}\frac{(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{l})\partial_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)}{n(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{l})},

(E.33)

\displaystyle A_{3}:=\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\sum_{k\not\sim l}\mathrm{T}_{kl}^{c}\left[\Upsilon_{kl}\xi(k)(1+2\xi(l))\bigl({f(\xi^{k\to l})-f(\xi)}\bigr)-\frac{(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{l})\partial_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)}{n(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{l})}\right].

(E.34)

The remaining part of the proof focuses on controlling the above three terms one by one. Specifically, we will demonstrate that
A_{1}
contributes to the dominant drift term in (
E.12
).

The term
A_{2}
.
We start with the term
A_{2}
:

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\sum_{k\not\sim l}\frac{\partial_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)}{n}\frac{\lambda_{l}^{2}-\lambda_{k}^{2}}{(\lambda_{l}-\lambda_{k})^{2}+\eta^{2}}=\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\left[\sum_{k}2c_{n}\lambda_{k}\partial_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)\cdot\re\underline{\mathsf{m}}_{n}(\lambda_{k}+\mathrm{i}\eta)+\sum_{k\not\sim l}\mathrm{T}_{kl}\frac{\partial_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)}{n}\right],

where in the second step we used the spectral decomposition of
\underline{\mathsf{m}}_{n}
:

\re\underline{\mathsf{m}}_{n}(\lambda_{k}+\mathrm{i}\eta)=\re\frac{1}{p}\sum_{l}\frac{1}{\lambda_{l}-(\lambda_{k}+\mathrm{i}\eta)}=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{l:l\not\sim k}\frac{\lambda_{l}-\lambda_{k}}{(\lambda_{l}-\lambda_{k})^{2}+\eta^{2}}.

Since
\partial_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)
is non-zero for at most
L
many
k
’s, using (
E.16
), the rigidity of
\lambda_{k}
in (
B.37
), the trivial bound
\mathrm{T}_{kl}<1
and the local law (
B.35
), we obtain that
A_{2}=\OO(n^{\varepsilon})
with high probability. Together with (
E.23
), it implies that

\frac{\mathsf{q}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}A_{2}=\OO(n^{2{\varepsilon}})\quad\text{w.h.p.}

(E.35)

The term
A_{3}
. For the term
A_{3}
, we will establish that

\frac{\mathsf{q}}{F+N^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}A_{3}=\mathcal{E}_{3}(t)-s_{+}^{(3)}(t),

(E.36)

where
s_{+}^{(3)}(t)\geq 0
and
\mathcal{E}_{3}(t)
represents a variable that satisfies
\mathcal{E}_{3}(t)=\OO(n^{-2/3+4{\varepsilon}+\mathsf{c}})
with high probability, uniformly in
t\in[T_{1}/2,T_{2}]
. By utilizing
Proposition

E.1
, we can express the LHS of (
E.36
) as

\displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{q}\theta_{-}\theta_{+}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\bigg[

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\not\sim l}\mathrm{T}_{kl}^{c}\Upsilon_{kl}\xi(k)(1+2\xi(l))\bigl({f(\xi^{k\to l})-f(\xi)}\bigr)\bigl({\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}-\widetilde{f}(\xi^{k\to l})^{\mathsf{q}-1}}\bigr)

\displaystyle-\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}\sum_{k\not\sim l}\mathrm{T}_{kl}^{c}\frac{(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{l})\partial_{\lambda_{k}}g(\xi)}{n(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{l})}\bigg].

(E.37)

We proceed to control the two terms in (
E.37
). To simplify the presentation, for
k\not\sim l
and a configuration
\xi
, we define the equivalence class of configurations
[\xi]\equiv[\xi]_{k,l}
as follows:
\xi^{\prime}\in[\xi]
if and only if
|\xi^{\prime}|=|\xi|
and
\supp(\xi^{\prime}-\xi)\subset\{\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{k},\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{l}\}
.
Given
[\xi]
and
0\leq\ell\leq\xi(k)+\xi(l)
, we define
\xi^{\ell}\in[\xi]
to be the configuration with
\xi^{\ell}(k)=\ell
.
Moreover, for any
\ell\in[\![{\xi(k)+\xi(l)}]\!]
, we define the following functions:

\displaystyle h_{\xi^{\ell},k,l}

\displaystyle:=\pi(\xi^{\ell})\xi^{\ell}(k)(1+2\xi^{\ell}(l))=\pi(\xi^{\ell-1})\xi^{\ell-1}(l)(1+2\xi^{\ell-1}(k)),

\displaystyle\quad\widehat{h}_{\xi^{\ell},k,l}

\displaystyle:=\pi(\xi^{\ell})\xi^{\ell}(k)=\pi(\xi^{\hat{\ell}})\xi^{\hat{\ell}}(l),\quad\text{with}\quad\hat{\ell}=\xi(k)+\xi(l)-\ell.

Then, using the definition (
4.17
) and the anti-symmetry of the coefficient
{(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{l})}/{(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{l})}
, we can further rewrite (
E.37
) as

\displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{q}\theta_{-}\theta_{+}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\not\sim l}\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{T}^{c}_{kl}\sum_{[\xi]}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\xi(k)+\xi(l)}\bigg[\Upsilon_{kl}h_{\xi^{\ell},k,l}\big(f(\xi^{\ell-1})-f(\xi^{\ell})\big)\big(\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell})^{\mathsf{q}-1}-\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell-1})^{\mathsf{q}-1}\big)

\displaystyle-\widehat{h}_{\xi^{\ell},k,l}\frac{\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{l}}{n(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{l})}\left(g(\xi^{\ell})\frac{\phi^{\prime}(\lambda_{k},{\varepsilon})}{\phi(\lambda_{k},{\varepsilon})}\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell})^{\mathsf{q}-1}-g(\xi^{\hat{\ell}})\frac{\phi^{\prime}(\lambda_{l},{\varepsilon})}{\phi(\lambda_{l},{\varepsilon})}\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\hat{\ell}})^{\mathsf{q}-1}\right)\bigg].

(E.38)

Using the definitions of
\theta_{\pm}
and (
4.18
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\theta_{-}\theta_{+}g(\xi^{\ell})\frac{\phi^{\prime}(\lambda_{k},{\varepsilon})}{\phi(\lambda_{k},{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle=\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\frac{g(\xi^{\ell})}{\phi(\lambda_{k},{\varepsilon})}\frac{\phi(\lambda_{k},{\varepsilon})-\phi(\lambda_{l},{\varepsilon})}{\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{l}}+\OO\left(n^{2{\varepsilon}}(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{l})\right)

\displaystyle=\theta_{-}\theta_{+}g(\xi^{\hat{\ell}})\frac{\phi^{\prime}(\lambda_{l},{\varepsilon})}{\phi(\lambda_{l},{\varepsilon})}+\OO\left(n^{2{\varepsilon}}(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{l})\right).

By substituting this estimate into (
E.38
) and using (
E.23
) again, we deduce that

\displaystyle\eqref{hha4}=\frac{\mathsf{q}\theta_{-}\theta_{+}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\not\sim l}\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{T}^{c}_{kl}\Upsilon_{kl}\sum_{[\xi]}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\xi(k)+\xi(l)}\bigg[h_{\xi^{\ell},k,l}\big(f(\xi^{\ell-1})-f(\xi^{\ell})\big)\big(\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell})^{\mathsf{q}-1}-\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell-1})^{\mathsf{q}-1}\big)

\displaystyle-2\widehat{h}_{\xi^{\ell},k,l}\frac{g(\xi^{\ell})}{\phi(\lambda_{k},{\varepsilon})}\big(\phi(\lambda_{k},{\varepsilon})-\phi(\lambda_{l},{\varepsilon})\big)\big(\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell})^{\mathsf{q}-1}-\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\hat{\ell}})^{\mathsf{q}-1}\big)\bigg]+\OO(n^{-1+3{\varepsilon}}).

Note
\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell})^{\mathsf{q}-1}-\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\hat{\ell}})^{\mathsf{q}-1}
can be expressed as a telescoping sum of terms of the form
\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell^{\prime}})^{\mathsf{q}-1}-\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell^{\prime}-1})^{\mathsf{q}-1}

for some
\ell^{\prime}
between
\ell
and
\hat{\ell}
. Therefore, there exist a sequence of deterministic coefficients
c_{\xi,\ell,k,l}
of order
\OO(1)
such that we can rewrite the above equation as

\displaystyle\eqref{hha4}=\frac{\mathsf{q}\theta_{-}\theta_{+}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\not\sim l}\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{T}^{c}_{kl}\Upsilon_{kl}\sum_{[\xi]}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\xi(k)+\xi(l)}h_{\xi^{\ell},k,l}\Big[\big(f(\xi^{\ell-1})-f(\xi^{\ell})\big)+c_{\xi,\ell,k,l}\frac{g(\xi^{\ell})}{\phi(\lambda_{k},{\varepsilon})}\big(\phi(\lambda_{l},{\varepsilon})-\phi(\lambda_{k},{\varepsilon})\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\times\left(\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell})^{\mathsf{q}-1}-\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell-1})^{\mathsf{q}-1}\right)+\OO(n^{-1+3{\varepsilon}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathsf{q}\theta_{-}\theta_{+}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\not\sim l}\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{T}^{c}_{kl}\Upsilon_{kl}\sum_{[\xi]}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\xi(k)+\xi(l)}h_{\xi^{\ell},k,l}\Big[\big(f(\xi^{\ell-1})-f(\xi^{\ell})\big)+c_{\xi,\ell,k,l}\big(g(\xi^{\ell-1})-g(\xi^{\ell})\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\times\left(\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell})^{\mathsf{q}-1}-\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell-1})^{\mathsf{q}-1}\right)+\OO(n^{-1+3{\varepsilon}}).

(E.39)

By definition, we have
f(\xi^{\ell-1})-f(\xi^{\ell})=g(\xi^{\ell-1})-g(\xi^{\ell})+\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell-1})-\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell}).

Observe that if
\lvert f(\xi^{\ell-1})-f(\xi^{\ell})\rvert\geq\lvert g(\xi^{\ell-1})-g(\xi^{\ell})\rvert\log n
, then the corresponding term in (
E.39
) is negative, i.e.,

\Big[\big(f(\xi^{\ell-1})-f(\xi^{\ell})\big)+c_{\xi,\ell,k,l}\big(g(\xi^{\ell-1})-g(\xi^{\ell})\big)\Big]\left(\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell})^{\mathsf{q}-1}-\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell-1})^{\mathsf{q}-1}\right)<0.

Otherwise, based on (
E.16
), we can get that

\displaystyle\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\Upsilon_{kl}\Big[\big(f(\xi^{\ell-1})-f(\xi^{\ell})\big)+c_{\xi,\ell,k,l}\big(g(\xi^{\ell-1})-g(\xi^{\ell})\big)\Big]\left(\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell})^{\mathsf{q}-1}-\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell-1})^{\mathsf{q}-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\OO\left(n^{-1+2{\varepsilon}}(\log n)^{2}\right)\cdot\theta_{-}\theta_{+}\left(\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell})^{\mathsf{q}-2}+\widetilde{f}(\xi^{\ell-1})^{\mathsf{q}-2}\right).

(E.40)

(We remark that this is the step where it is convenient to define
g_{t}
in terms of
\lambda_{k}(t)
as in (
4.17
).)
By combining these facts, we find that

\eqref{eq_finalreducedform}=\mathcal{E}_{3}(t)-s_{+}^{(3)}(t),

where
s_{+}^{(3)}(t)\geq 0
and the variable
\mathcal{E}_{3}(t)
satisfies the following bound:

\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}_{3}(t)|

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{-1+3{\varepsilon}}+\frac{\mathsf{q}\theta_{-}\theta_{+}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-2}\frac{n^{2{\varepsilon}}(\log n)^{2}}{n}\sum_{k\not\sim l}\frac{\xi(k)\eta^{2}}{(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{l})^{2}+\eta^{2}}

\displaystyle=n^{-1+3{\varepsilon}}+\frac{\mathsf{q}\theta_{-}\theta_{+}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-2}n^{2{\varepsilon}}(\log n)^{2}\sum_{k}\xi(k)\eta\im\underline{\mathsf{m}}_{n}(\lambda_{k}+\mathrm{i}\eta)

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{-1+3{\varepsilon}}+n^{4{\varepsilon}}(\log n)^{2}\eta\leq n^{-2/3+4{\varepsilon}+\mathsf{c}}\quad\text{w.h.p.}

(E.41)

Here, in the second step, we used the spectral decomposition of
\underline{\mathsf{m}}_{n}
, and in the third step, we applied the local law (
B.35
) to
\underline{\mathsf{m}}_{n}(\lambda_{k}+\mathrm{i}\eta)
along with the rigidity of
\lambda_{k}
given by (
B.37
). This concludes (
E.36
).

The term
A_{1}
.
Finally, we prove the following inequality for sufficiently large
\mathsf{q}
:

\frac{\mathsf{q}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}A_{1}\leq-C(t)\frac{F}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}+\OO_{\prec}(1),

(E.42)

where
C(t)
is a positive variable satisfying
C(t)\gtrsim\eta^{-1/2}
with high probability. With the definition of
A_{1}
in (
E.32
), we can express the LHS of (
E.42
) as follows:

\displaystyle~\frac{\mathsf{q}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}A_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{\mathsf{q}\theta_{-}\theta_{+}}{2n(F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L})}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}\sum_{k\not\sim l}\frac{(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{l})\xi(k)(1+2\xi(l))}{(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{l})^{2}+\eta^{2}}\big(f(\xi^{k\to l})-f(\xi)\big).

(E.43)

Recalling the definitions of
f
and
\xi^{k\to l}
in (
4.22
) and (
4.23
), we can express
f(\xi^{k\to l})
as

\displaystyle f(\xi^{k\to l})=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{p|\langle{\bf{v}},{\bf{u}}_{l}\rangle|^{2}}{1+2\xi(l)}\prod_{j}\frac{(p|{\bf{v}}^{\top}{\bf{u}}_{j}|^{2})^{\xi(j)-\mathbf{1}_{j=k}}}{a_{\xi(j)-\mathbf{1}_{j=k}}}\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{t}\bigg],

(E.44)

where
a_{n}:=(2n-1)!!
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Utilizing the spectral decomposition of
\mathcal{G}_{1,t}(z)=(W(t)W(t)^{\top}-z)^{-1}
with
z=z_{k}:=\lambda_{k}+\mathrm{i}\eta
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{l:l\not\sim k}\frac{(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{l})|\langle{\bf{v}},{\bf{u}}_{l}\rangle|^{2}}{(\lambda_{l}-\lambda_{k})^{2}+\eta^{2}}=

\displaystyle~\sum_{l}\frac{2\lambda_{k}|\langle{\bf{v}},{\bf{u}}_{l}\rangle|^{2}}{(\lambda_{l}-\lambda_{k})^{2}+\eta^{2}}-\frac{2\lambda_{k}|\langle{\bf{v}},{\bf{u}}_{k}\rangle|^{2}}{\eta^{2}}+\sum_{l:l\not\sim k}\frac{(\lambda_{l}-\lambda_{k})|\langle{\bf{v}},{\bf{u}}_{l}\rangle|^{2}}{(\lambda_{l}-\lambda_{k})^{2}+\eta^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{2\lambda_{k}}{\eta}\im\left({\bf{v}}^{\top}\mathcal{G}_{1,t}(z_{k}){\bf{v}}\right)+\re\left({\bf{v}}^{\top}\mathcal{G}_{1,t}(z_{k}){\bf{v}}\right)+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1}\eta^{-2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{2\lambda_{k}}{\eta}\im\left({\bf{v}}^{\top}\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}(z_{k}){\bf{v}}\right)+\OO_{\prec}\left(\eta^{-1}\Psi_{t}(z_{k})\right).

Here, in the second step, we applied the delocalization estimate (
B.38
) to
|\langle{\bf{v}},{\bf{u}}_{k}\rangle|^{2}
, and in the third step, we applied the local law (
B.35
) to
{\bf{v}}^{\top}\mathcal{G}_{1,t}(z_{k}){\bf{v}}
with
\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}(z):=-[z(1+m_{t}(z))\Lambda_{t}]^{-1}
.
Similarly, by observing that
\xi(l)
is nonzero for at most
L
many
l
’s, and using the spectral decomposition of
\underline{\mathsf{m}}_{n}
and the local law (
B.42
), we obtain that

\frac{1}{p}\sum_{l:l\not\sim k}\frac{(\lambda_{k}+\lambda_{l})(1+2\xi(l))}{(\lambda_{l}-\lambda_{k})^{2}+\eta^{2}}=\frac{2\lambda_{k}}{\eta}\im m_{1,t}(z_{k})+\OO_{\prec}(n^{-1}\eta^{-2}).

Plugging the above three equations into (
E.43
) and applying the delocalization estimate (
B.38
) to the factors
p|\langle{\bf{v}},{\bf{u}}_{j}\rangle|^{2}
in
f(\xi)
and (
E.44
), we obtain that

\displaystyle(\ref{eq_finalparttobeestimated})=

\displaystyle~\frac{c_{n}\mathsf{q}\theta_{-}\theta_{+}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}\sum_{k}\frac{\lambda_{k}\xi(k)}{\eta}

\displaystyle\times\left[\im\left({\bf{v}}^{\top}\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}(z_{k}){\bf{v}}\right)f(\xi-{\bf e}_{k})-\im m_{1,t}(z_{k})f(\xi)+\OO_{\prec}\left(\Psi_{t}(z_{k})\right)\right].

(E.45)

By the induction hypothesis, when
K=L-1
, the estimate (
E.6
) holds with high probability for large even integer
\mathsf{p}\geq\mathsf{p}_{0}
, implying that

|\widetilde{f}(\xi-\mathbf{e}_{k})|\lesssim n^{-{\varepsilon}+(L-1)/\mathsf{p}}\quad\text{w.h.p.}\ \Rightarrow\ |\widetilde{f}(\xi-\mathbf{e}_{k})|\prec n^{-{\varepsilon}}.

(E.46)

On the other hand, by (
B.17
) and (
B.40
), we have

\im m_{1,t}(z_{k})\asymp\im m_{t}(z_{k})\gtrsim\min\left\{\sqrt{\kappa_{k}+\eta},\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\kappa_{k}+\eta}}\right\}\gtrsim\sqrt{\eta}\quad\text{w.h.p.},

(E.47)

where
\kappa_{k}
is defined as
\kappa_{k}:=\dist(\lambda_{k},\supp(\varrho_{t}))
, and we have used the fact that
\kappa_{k}\prec n^{-2/3}
according to the rigidity estimate (
B.37
). With (
E.47
), for
\eta=n^{-2/3+\mathsf{c}/2}
, we obtain that

\frac{\Psi_{t}(z_{k})}{\im m_{1,t}(z_{k})}\lesssim\left(\frac{1}{n\eta\im m_{1,t}(z_{k})}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{n\eta\im m_{1,t}(z_{k})}\lesssim\left(\frac{1}{n\eta^{3/2}}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{n\eta^{3/2}}\lesssim n^{-3\mathsf{c}/8}.

(E.48)

With the estimates (
E.46
) and (
E.48
), we can simplify (
E.45
) as follows:

\displaystyle(\ref{eq_finalparttobeestimated})=\frac{c_{n}\mathsf{q}\theta_{-}\theta_{+}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}\sum_{k}\lambda_{k}\xi(k)\frac{\im m_{1,t}(z_{k})}{\eta}

\displaystyle\times\Big(\varphi_{t}(z_{k})g(\xi-{\bf e}_{k})-f(\xi)+\OO_{\prec}\big(n^{-{\varepsilon}}+n^{-3\mathsf{c}/8}\big)\Big),

(E.49)

where
\varphi_{t}(z_{k})\equiv\varphi_{t}({\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},z_{k})={\im({\bf{v}}^{\top}\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}(z_{k}){\bf{v}})}/{\im m_{1,t}(z_{k})}
as defined in (
4.14
). By using (
B.18
), (
C.12
), (
C.32
), and the definition of
\phi(\lambda_{k},{\varepsilon})
, we can check that

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}({\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},z_{k})-\phi(\lambda_{k},{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\left[\varphi_{t}({\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},z_{k})-\varphi_{0}({\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},\lambda_{k})\right]+\left[\varphi_{0}({\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},\lambda_{k})-\phi(\lambda_{k},{\varepsilon})\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{t+\eta}+n^{-{\varepsilon}}\leq 2n^{-{\varepsilon}},

(E.50)

where in the last step, we used the fact that
\sqrt{t+\eta}\lesssim n^{-1/6+\mathsf{c}/2}+n^{-1/3+\mathsf{c}/4}\leq n^{-{\varepsilon}}
for
\mathsf{c}<1/6
and
{\varepsilon}<1/12
.
Plugging (
E.50
) into (
E.49
), and in light of the definition of
g(\xi)
in (
4.17
), we find that

\displaystyle(\ref{eq_finalparttobeestimated})=\frac{c_{n}\mathsf{q}\theta_{-}\theta_{+}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\sum_{k}\lambda_{k}\xi(k)\frac{\im m_{1,t}(z_{k})}{\eta}\left(g(\xi)-f(\xi)+\OO_{\prec}\big(n^{-{\varepsilon}}+n^{-3\mathsf{c}/8}\big)\right)\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{-c_{n}\mathsf{q}\theta_{-}\theta_{+}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\sum_{\xi}\pi(\xi)\sum_{k}\lambda_{k}\xi(k)\frac{\im m_{1,t}(z_{k})}{\eta}\left[\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}}+\OO_{\prec}\left(n^{-\tau}\big(\widetilde{f}(\xi)^{\mathsf{q}}+n^{-\mathsf{q}\delta}\big)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{-c\eta^{-1/2}F}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}+\OO_{\prec}\left(\frac{n^{-\mathsf{q}\delta-\tau+L}\eta^{-1}}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}\right)=\frac{-c\eta^{-1/2}F}{F+n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}+L}}+\OO_{\prec}\left(1\right)

for a constant
c>0
. In the second step, we used (
E.23
) with
{\varepsilon}
replaced by a constant
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}<\delta<\min\{{\varepsilon},3\mathsf{c}/8\}
, and we denote
\tau:=\min\{{\varepsilon},3\mathsf{c}/8\}-\delta
; in the third step, we used
\sqrt{\eta}\lesssim\im m_{1,t}(z_{k})\lesssim 1
by (
E.47
) and (
B.17
); in the last step, we choose
\mathsf{q}
to be sufficiently large (depending on
\delta-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
) such that
n^{-\mathsf{q}\delta-\tau}\eta^{-1}\leq n^{-{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\mathsf{q}}
. This proves (
E.42
) and completes the proof of Lemma
E.2
.

Appendix F
Proof of the results in Section
4.3

F.1
Proof of Lemma
4.11

In this subsection, we prove Lemma
4.11
using the strategies outlined in
[
13
,
44
]
. A key component of the proof relies on the level repulsion estimate. In the existing literature, the level repulsion estimate for sample covariance matrices has only been established around the edge
[
13
]
or inside the bulk via the universality of bulk eigenvalue statistics
[
61
]
. However, for our specific applications, we require the level repulsion estimate to hold over the entire spectrum.
Recall the definition of
\mathcal{W}_{t}
from (
4.8
), and denote the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{Q}_{t}=\mathcal{W}_{t}\mathcal{W}_{t}^{\top}
as
\{\bar{\lambda}_{k}(t)\}
. Given an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we define the counting function

\overline{\mathsf{N}}_{t}(I)=|\{i\in[\![{1,\mathsf{K}}]\!]:\bar{\lambda}_{i}(t)\in I\}|.

Similarly, we define the counting function
{\mathsf{N}}_{t}
for the eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{k}(t)\}
of the matrix DBM defined in (
4.4
):

{\mathsf{N}}_{t}(I)=|\{i\in[\![{1,\mathsf{K}}]\!]:\lambda_{i}(t)\in I\}|.

Recall that
\gamma_{k}(t)
represents the quantiles of
\varrho_{t}
as defined below (
B.33
), and
\Delta_{k}
is defined in (
4.29
).

Proposition F.1

(Level repulsion)
.

Under the setting of
Lemma

4.2
, consider a fixed
t\asymp n^{-1/3+\mathsf{c}}
. There exists a small constant
\delta_{0}\in(0,2/3)
such that the following statements hold. Define the disjoint union of intervals
I_{\delta_{0}}^{+}(t)
as

I_{\delta_{0}}^{+}(t):=\cup_{k=1}^{q}[a_{2k}(t)-n^{-\delta_{0}},a_{2k-1}(t)+n^{-\delta_{0}}],

and define
k_{E}:=\mathrm{arg\,min}\{|\gamma_{k}(t)-E|:k\in[\![{\mathsf{K}}]\!]\}
for
E\in I_{\delta_{0}}^{+}
.
Then, for every constant
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}],
we can find a constant
\varepsilon\equiv\varepsilon(\delta)>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\left({\mathsf{N}}_{t}\left([E-\Delta_{k_{E}}n^{-\delta},E+\Delta_{k_{E}}n^{-\delta}]\right)\geqslant 2\right)\leqslant n^{-\delta-\varepsilon},\quad\forall E\in I_{\delta_{0}}^{+}.

(F.1)

The same estimate also applies to
\overline{\mathsf{N}}_{t}
.

Proof.

An analogous level repulsion estimate inside the bulk has been established for the matrix DBM of Wigner ensembles in
[
47
, Theorem 5.1]
and
[
11
, Lemma B.1]
, with the key inputs being the local laws and the eigenvalue rigidity estimate. With the help of our Lemmas
B.9
and
B.11
, we can readily extend the arguments from
[
47
,
11
]
to our rectangular matrix DBM
Q(t)
defined in (
4.4
). Specifically, by following the reasoning presented in
[
47
, Theorem 5.1]
, we can establish that (
F.1
) holds for
E\in\cup_{k=1}^{q}[a_{2k}+\kappa,a_{2k-1}-\kappa]
, where
\kappa
is an arbitrarily small positive constant.
Furthermore, as explained in the proof of
[
11
, Lemma B.1]
, the argument in
[
47
, Theorem 5.1]
can be extended a little bit to show that there exists a sufficiently small constant
{\varepsilon}>0
so that (
F.1
) holds for
E\in\cup_{k=1}^{q}[a_{2k}+n^{-{\varepsilon}},a_{2k-1}-n^{-{\varepsilon}}]
. Since the extension from Wigner ensembles to sample covariance ensembles is standard, we omit the complete details here.

To establish (
F.1
) for
E
around the spectral edges, specifically for
E\in\cup_{k=1}^{q}([a_{2k}-n^{-\delta_{0}},a_{2k}+n^{-{\varepsilon}}]\cup[a_{2k-1}-n^{-{\varepsilon}},a_{2k-1}+n^{-\delta_{0}}])
, we can refer to
[
13
, Proposition 6.3]
. Although this result is stated for the special case with
\Lambda_{0}=I
in
[
13
]
, the same argument can be extended to our more general setting. More precisely, as explained in Lemma 6.4 of
[
13
]
, we can show that (
F.1
) holds for the Gaussian ensemble when
X
is a Gaussian random matrix.
(The proof of
[
13
, Lemma 6.4]
relies on an analysis of the joint eigenvalue probability density, employing a method developed in the proof of
[
14
, Theorem 3.2]
. While
[
14
, Theorem 3.2]
is presented for the Gaussian
\beta
ensemble, the same proof carries over almost verbatim to our setting, where the eigenvalues follow a Laguerre
\beta
ensemble.)
Next, as explained in the proof of
[
13
, Lemma 6.5]
, we can establish the level repulsion estimate (
F.1
) for
Q(t)
through a Green’s function comparison argument between the case with Gaussian
X
and that with a non-Gaussian
X
in
W(0)
. Once again, since this extension is standard and straightforward, we omit the complete details here. This concludes the proof of (
F.1
) for
E
near the spectral edges.

By (
F.1
), we know that the level repulsion estimate applies to the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{Q}^{G}_{t}:=\mathcal{W}^{G}_{t}(W^{G}_{t})^{\top}
with
W^{G}_{t}:=D_{t}^{1/2}V^{\top}X^{G}
.
To further prove this estimate for
\overline{\mathsf{N}}_{t}
, we can once again employ the Green’s function comparison argument between
\mathcal{Q}^{G}_{t}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{t}
, as explained in Lemma 6.5 of
[
13
]
. We omit the details here.
∎

With Proposition
F.1
, we now proceed to complete the proof of Lemma
4.11
by employing the method developed in
[
13
,
44
]
.

Proof of Lemma
4.11
.

We only present the proof for
Q(t)
, while the proof for
\mathcal{Q}_{t}
is the same. To simplify the presentation, we prove Lemma
4.11
in the special case where
\theta=\theta(p|\langle{\bf{v}},{\bf{u}}_{i+r}(t)\rangle|^{2})
. For brevity, we will use the abbreviations
{\bf{u}}_{i}\equiv{\bf{u}}_{i}(t)
,
Q\equiv Q(t)
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}(z)\equiv\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1,t}(z):=(Q(t)-z)^{-1}
for a fixed
t\asymp n^{-1/3+\mathsf{c}}
. Recall the definition of
\Delta_{i}
in (
4.29
) and the definition of
\eta_{i}
in (
4.30
).
We now present the first ingredient of the proof.

Lemma F.2
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
4.11
, there exists a small constant
{\varepsilon}>0
such that the following estimate holds for sufficiently small constants
\delta_{1}\equiv\delta_{1}({\varepsilon})
,
\delta_{2}\equiv\delta_{2}({\varepsilon},\delta_{1})
, and
\nu>0
:

\mathbb{E}\theta(p|\langle{\bf{v}},{\bf{u}}_{i+r}(t)\rangle|^{2})=\mathbb{E}\theta\left(\frac{p}{\pi}\int_{I_{i}}\im\big[{\bf{v}}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta_{i}){\bf{v}}\big]\chi_{i}(E)\mathrm{d}E\right)+\OO(n^{-\nu}),\quad i\in[\![{1,\mathsf{K}-r}]\!],

where
I_{i}
is defined in (
4.31
) and
\chi_{i}(E):=\mathbf{1}(\lambda_{i+1}<E_{i}^{-}\leqslant\lambda_{i})
. Here,
E_{i}^{-}=E-n^{\delta_{1}}\eta_{i}
is defined in (
4.30
), and we adopt the convention
\lambda_{\mathsf{K}+1}=0
.

Proof.

Our proof follows a similar approach to that of
[
44
, Lemma 3.1]
,
[
13
, Lemma 7.1]
, and
[
26
, Lemma 3.2]
. The only difference is that we utilize the level repulsion estimate from
Proposition

F.1
over the entire spectrum, whereas the aforementioned works rely on the level repulsion estimate either near the edges or inside the bulks. We omit the detailed exposition here.
∎

For the second ingredient of the proof, we show that the indicator function
\chi_{i}(E)
can be approximated by the smooth functions
\mathfrak{q}_{i}(x)
and
\mathfrak{f}_{i}(x)
defined in (
4.28
) and (
4.32
) up to a negligible error.

Lemma F.3
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
4.11
, there exists a small constant
{\varepsilon}>0
such that the following estimate holds for sufficiently small constants
\delta_{1}\equiv\delta_{1}({\varepsilon})
,
\delta_{2}\equiv\delta_{2}({\varepsilon},\delta_{1})
, and
\nu>0
:

\mathbb{E}\theta\left(\frac{p}{\pi}\int_{I_{i}}\im\big[{\bf{v}}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta_{i}){\bf{v}}\big]\chi_{i}(E)\mathrm{d}E\right)=\mathbb{E}\theta\left(\frac{p}{\pi}\int_{I_{i}}\im\big[{\bf{v}}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta_{i}){\bf{v}}\big]\mathfrak{q}_{i}(\tr\mathfrak{f}_{i}(Q))\mathrm{d}E\right)+\OO(n^{-\nu}),

where
i\in[\![{1,\mathsf{K}-r}]\!]
.

Proof.

Using the rigidity of eigenvalues in (
B.37
), we can obtain that with high probability,

\displaystyle p\int_{I_{i}}\im\big[{\bf{v}}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta_{i}){\bf{v}}\big]\chi_{i}(E)\mathrm{d}E

\displaystyle=p\int_{I_{i}}\im\big[{\bf{v}}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta_{i}){\bf{v}}\big]\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{N}([E^{-}_{i},E^{+}])=i)\mathrm{d}E

\displaystyle=p\int_{I_{i}}\im\big[{\bf{v}}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta_{i}){\bf{v}}\big]\mathfrak{q}_{i}[\tr(\chi_{E}(Q))]\mathrm{d}E,

where
E^{+}
is defined in (
4.30
) and we denote
\chi_{E}(x):=\mathbf{1}(x\in[E_{i}^{-},E^{+}])
. Next, by utilizing Proposition
F.1
and following the proof of (4.11) in
[
26
]
, we can show that

\mathbb{E}\theta\left(\frac{p}{\pi}\int_{I_{i}}\im\big[{\bf{v}}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta_{i}){\bf{v}}\big]\mathfrak{q}_{i}[\tr(\chi_{E}(Q))]\mathrm{d}E\right)=\mathbb{E}\theta\left(\frac{p}{\pi}\int_{I_{i}}\im\big[{\bf{v}}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta_{i}){\bf{v}}\big]\mathfrak{q}_{i}(\tr\mathfrak{f}_{i}(Q))\mathrm{d}E\right)+\OO(n^{-\nu}).

Combining the above two equations completes the proof.
∎

We observe that Lemmas
F.2
and
F.3
together establish Lemma
4.11
when
L=1
. The proof for the general case of
L>1
is analogous, as explained in Section 4 of
[
44
]
. We omit the details here.
∎

F.2
Proof of Lemma
4.12

In this subsection, we prove Lemma
4.12
using a continuous comparison argument developed in
[
45
, Section 7]
.
Recall the sequence of interpolating matrices
W^{s}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}
defined in (
4.10
). For the sake of simplicity, we denote
Y^{s}\equiv Y^{s}_{t}:=D_{t}^{-1/2}W^{s}_{t}
, i.e.,

Y^{s}_{i\mu}=\sum_{j=1}^{2p}U^{1}_{ij}\chi^{s}_{j\mu}\mathsf{X}_{j\mu}+\sum_{j=1}^{2p}U^{0}_{ij}(1-\chi^{s}_{j\mu})\mathsf{X}_{j\mu},\quad i\in\mathcal{I}_{1},\ \mu\in\mathcal{I}_{2},

where
\{\chi^{s}_{j\mu}\}_{j\in[\![{2p}]\!],\mu\in[\![{n}]\!]}
are i.i.d. Bernoulli
(s)
random variables.
Alternatively, we can express
Y^{s}
as

Y^{s}=\sum_{j=1}^{2p}\sum_{\mu=1}^{n}Z^{s}_{(j\mu)},\quad\text{with}\quad Z^{s}_{(j\mu)}:=\mathsf{X}_{j\mu}\Delta^{s}_{(j\mu)},\quad\Delta^{s}_{(j\mu)}:=\chi^{s}_{j\mu}\left(U^{1}\mathbf{e}_{j\mu}\right)+(1-\chi^{s}_{j\mu})\left(U^{0}\mathbf{e}_{j\mu}\right).

(F.2)

Here,
\mathbf{e}_{j\mu}:=\mathbf{e}_{j}\mathbf{e}_{\mu}^{\top}
represents a
2p\times n
matrix with only one non-zero entry at the
(j,\mu)
-th position.
Furthermore, given a matrix
\Gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}
, we define

Y^{s,\Gamma}_{(j\mu)}:=\sum_{(i,\nu)\neq(j,\mu)}Z_{(i\nu)}^{s}+\Gamma.

(F.3)

In particular, using this notation, we have
Y^{s}-Y^{s,\mathbf{0}}_{(j\mu)}=Z_{(j\mu)}^{s}
.
Similar to (
B.13
) and (
B.14
), we will use the following linearized resolvent:

G^{s}_{t}(z):=\begin{pmatrix}-D_{t}^{-1}&Y^{s}\\
(Y^{s})^{\top}&-z\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}zD_{t}^{1/2}{\mathcal{G}}_{1,t}^{s}(z)D_{t}^{1/2}&D_{t}Y^{s}\mathcal{G}^{s}_{2,t}\\
\mathcal{G}^{s}_{2,t}(Y^{s})^{\top}D_{t}&\mathcal{G}^{s}_{2,t}\end{pmatrix},

(F.4)

where
\mathcal{G}_{1,t}^{s}:=(D_{t}^{1/2}Y^{s}(Y^{s})^{\top}D_{t}^{1/2}-z)^{-1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{2,t}^{s}:=((Y^{s})^{\top}D_{t}Y^{s}-z)^{-1}
.
For
s\in\{0,1\}
, the local laws for the resolvent
G^{s}
have already been established in
Lemma

B.2
(when
s=0
) and Lemma
B.11
(when
s=1
). These local laws can also be extended to all
s\in(0,1)
, as summarized in the following lemma.

Lemma F.4
.

Suppose the assumptions of Lemma
4.12
hold. Define
\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}
and
\Psi_{t}
as in
Section

B.3
with
\Lambda=D_{0}
and
\Lambda_{0}=D_{00}
. Then, there exists a constant
c>0
such that the following local law holds uniformly in
z\in\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(c,\tau,t,n)
for any constant
\tau>0
and deterministic unit vectors
{\bf{u}},{\bf{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p+n}
:

\left|\mathbf{u}^{\top}G^{s}_{t}(z)\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}^{\top}\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}(z)\mathbf{v}\right|\prec\Psi_{t}(z).

(F.5)

We now prove this lemma using the continuous comparison argument presented in
[
45
,
68
]
. In the interest of brevity, we will provide a sketch of the key points, highlighting the main differences in the argument. For a more comprehensive understanding, readers can refer to Section 6 of
[
68
]
.

Proof of Lemma
F.4
.

For any
\eta\geq N^{-1+\tau}
and a sufficiently small constant
\delta\in(0,1/100)
, we define

\eta_{l}:=\eta N^{\delta l}\text{ for }\ l=0,...,K-1,\ \ \ \eta_{K}:=1,

(F.6)

where
K\equiv K(\eta):=\max\left\{l\in\mathbb{N}:\eta N^{\delta(l-1)}<1\right\}.
Since
z\mapsto G_{t}^{s}(z)-\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}(z)
is Lipschitz continuous in
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}\equiv\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(c,\tau,t,n)
with Lipschitz constant of order
\OO(\eta^{-2})=\OO(n^{2})
, it suffices to show that (
F.5
) holds for all
z
in a discrete but suitably dense subset
{\mathbf{S}}\subset\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
. Specifically, we choose
\mathbf{S}
to be an
n^{-10}
-net of
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
such that
|\mathbf{S}|\lesssim n^{20}
and

E+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbf{S}\Rightarrow E+\mathrm{i}\eta_{l}\in\mathbf{S}\text{ for }l=1,\ldots,K(\eta).

Similar to
[
68
, Section 6]
, our proof relies on an induction argument based on two scale-dependent properties, denoted as
(
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{d}
)
and
(
\bm{\mathrm{C}}_{d}
)
, formulated on the subsets

{\bm{\mathrm{S}}}_{d}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\bigl\{{z\in{\bm{\mathrm{S}}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\im z\geqslant n^{-\delta d}}\bigr\},

d\in\mathbb{N}
.

(
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{d}
)

For all
z\in{\bm{\mathrm{S}}}_{d}
, we have that for any deterministic unit vectors
{\bf{u}},{\bf{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p+n}
,

\big|\mathbf{u}^{\top}G^{s}_{t}(z)\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}^{\top}\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}(z)\mathbf{v}\big|\prec 1.

(F.7)

(
\bm{\mathrm{C}}_{d}
)

For all
z\in{\bm{\mathrm{S}}}_{d}
, we have that for any deterministic unit vectors
{\bf{u}},{\bf{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p+n}
,

\big|\mathbf{u}^{\top}G^{s}_{t}(z)\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}^{\top}\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}(z)\mathbf{v}\big|\prec n^{24\delta}\Psi(z).

(F.8)

It is clear that property (
\mathbf{C}_{d}
) implies property (
\mathbf{A}_{d}
) if
\delta
is chosen sufficiently small (depending on
\tau
) such that
n^{24\delta}\Psi(z)\ll 1
. On the other hand, if we can show that property (
\mathbf{A}_{d-1}
) implies property (
\mathbf{C}_{d}
) for all
d\leqslant\delta^{-1}
, then we can conclude that (
F.8
) holds for all
z\in\mathbf{S}
by induction on
d
. By polarization, it suffices to prove that when property (
{\bm{\mathrm{A}}}_{d-1}
) holds, we have

\big|\mathbf{v}^{\top}G^{s}_{t}(z)\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{\top}\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}(z)\mathbf{v}\big|\prec n^{24\delta}\Psi(z),

(F.9)

for all
z\in\mathbf{S}_{d}
and any deterministic unit vector
\mathbf{v}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{p+n}
. In fact, we can derive the more general bound (
F.8
) by applying (
F.9
) to the vectors
\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{u}+\mathrm{i}\mathbf{v}
, respectively.

By utilizing Markov’s inequality, showing (
F.9
) requires proving that for any fixed large
\mathsf{q}\in 2\mathbb{N}
and
d\leqslant\delta^{-1},
there exists a constant
C>0
such that

|\mathbb{E}F^{{\bf{v}},\,\mathsf{q}}(Y^{s})|\leq C(n^{24\delta}\Psi)^{\mathsf{q}}

(F.10)

uniformly for all
z\in\mathbf{S}_{d}
. Here,
F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}(Y^{s})
is defined as

F^{{\bf{v}},\,\mathsf{q}}(Y^{s})\equiv F^{{\bf{v}},\,\mathsf{q}}(Y^{s},z):=\big|{\bf{v}}^{\top}G_{t}^{s}{\bf{v}}-{\bf{v}}^{\top}\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}{\bf{v}}\big|^{\mathsf{q}}.

(F.11)

To establish (
F.10
), we follow the arguments presented in Sections 6.1 and 6.2 of
[
68
]
. First, we see that (
F.5
) and consequently (
F.10
) hold when
s=0
by
Lemma

B.11
. Second, similar to
[
45
, Lemma 7.9]
, we can employ the fundamental theorem of calculus to obtain that

\mathbb{E}F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}(Y^{1})-\mathbb{E}F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}(Y^{0})\;=\;\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s\,\sum_{j=1}^{2p}\sum_{\mu=1}^{n}\Bigl[{\mathbb{E}F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}\Bigl({Y^{s,Z^{1}_{(j\mu)}}_{(j\mu)}}\Bigr)-\mathbb{E}F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}\Bigl({Y^{s,Z^{0}_{(j\mu)}}_{(j\mu)}}\Bigr)}\Bigr]\,,

(F.12)

where we use the notation introduced in (
F.3
) with
\Gamma=Z^{0}_{(j\mu)}
or
Z^{1}_{(j\mu)}
.
Under the property (
{\bm{\mathrm{A}}}_{d-1}
), we claim the following estimate:

\sum_{j=1}^{2p}\sum_{\mu=1}^{n}\Bigl[{\mathbb{E}F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}\Bigl({Y^{s,Z^{1}_{(j\mu)}}_{(j\mu)}}\Bigr)-\mathbb{E}F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}\Bigl({Y^{s,Z^{0}_{(j\mu)}}_{(j\mu)}}\Bigr)}\Bigr]\lesssim(n^{24\delta}\Psi)^{\mathsf{q}}+|\mathbb{E}F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}(Y^{s})|.

(F.13)

By substituting this estimate into (
F.12
) and applying a standard Grönwall’s argument, we conclude (
F.10
).

The remainder of the proof focuses on establishing (
F.13
). To simplify the presentation, until the end of this proof, we slightly abuse the notation and define

G(Y)\equiv G(Y,z):=\begin{pmatrix}-D_{t}^{-1}&Y\\
Y^{\top}&-z\end{pmatrix}^{-1}

(F.14)

for given random matrix
Y
.
By the definition (
F.3
), for any given
\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}
and
M\in\mathbb{N}
, we have the following resolvent expansion:

\displaystyle G\left(Y^{s,\Gamma^{\prime}}_{(j\mu)}\right)-G\left(Y^{s,\Gamma}_{(j\mu)}\right)=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{M}G\left(Y^{s,\Gamma}_{(j\mu)}\right)\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&\Gamma-\Gamma^{\prime}\\
(\Gamma-\Gamma^{\prime})^{\top}&0\end{pmatrix}G\left(Y^{s,\Gamma}_{(j\mu)}\right)\right]^{k}

(F.15)

\displaystyle+G\left(Y^{s,\Gamma^{\prime}}_{(j\mu)}\right)\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&\Gamma-\Gamma^{\prime}\\
(\Gamma-\Gamma^{\prime})^{\top}&0\end{pmatrix}G\left(Y^{s,\Gamma}_{(j\mu)}\right)\right]^{M+1}.

By employing this expansion with
\Gamma=Z_{(j\mu)}^{s}
and choosing a sufficiently large
M
, we can utilize property
({\bf A}_{d-1})
for
G_{t}^{s}
and the rough bound

\|G(Y^{s,\Gamma^{\prime}}_{(j\mu)})\|\prec\eta^{-1}
to verify the following inequalities for any deterministic unit vectors
{\bf{u}},{\bf{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p+n}
:

\left|{\bf{u}}^{\top}G\Bigl({Y^{s,Z^{1}_{(j\mu)}}_{(j\mu)}}\Bigr){\bf{v}}\right|\prec 1,\quad\left|{\bf{u}}^{\top}G\Bigl({Y^{s,Z^{0}_{(j\mu)}}_{(j\mu)}}\Bigr){\bf{v}}\right|\prec 1,\quad\left|{\bf{u}}^{\top}G\Bigl({Y^{s,\mathbf{0}}_{(j\mu)}}\Bigr){\bf{v}}\right|\prec 1.

(F.16)

To simplify the notation, for fixed
s\in(0,1)
, we further define

f_{(j\mu)}(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}\left(Y^{s,\lambda_{0}U^{0}{\bf e}_{j\mu}+\lambda_{1}U^{1}{\bf e}_{j\mu}}_{(j\mu)}\right)\,,

(F.17)

and denote its derivatives as

f_{(j\mu)}^{(k,l)}=\partial_{\lambda_{0}}^{k}\partial_{\lambda_{1}}^{l}f_{(j\mu)}(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}),\quad k,l\in\mathbb{N}.

By applying the Taylor expansion and using the estimate (
F.16
), we derive that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}\Bigl({Y^{s,Z^{0}_{(j\mu)}}_{(j\mu)}}\Bigr)-\mathbb{E}F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}\Bigl({Y^{s,\mathbf{0}}_{(j\mu)}}\Bigr)=\sum_{\ell=2}^{4\mathsf{q}}\frac{1}{\ell!}\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}^{(\ell,0)}(0,0)\mathbb{E}(\mathsf{X}_{j\mu})^{\ell}+\OO_{\prec}(\Psi^{\mathsf{q}}),

(F.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}\Bigl({Y^{s,Z^{1}_{(j\mu)}}_{(j\mu)}}\Bigr)-\mathbb{E}F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}\Bigl({Y^{s,\mathbf{0}}_{(j\mu)}}\Bigr)\;=\;\sum_{\ell=2}^{4\mathsf{q}}\frac{1}{\ell!}\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}^{(0,\ell)}(0,0)\mathbb{E}(\mathsf{X}_{j\mu})^{\ell}+\OO_{\prec}(\Psi^{\mathsf{q}}),

(F.19)

where we have utilized the mean zero condition
\mathbb{E}\mathsf{X}_{j\mu}=0
and the independence between
Y^{s,\bm{\mathrm{0}}}_{(j\mu)}
and
\mathsf{X}_{j\mu}
. Now, to establish (
F.13
), we only need to prove that

\sum_{j,\mu}\mathbb{E}(\mathsf{X}_{j\mu})^{\ell}\left(\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}^{(\ell,0)}(0,0)-\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}^{(0,\ell)}(0,0)\right)\lesssim(n^{24\delta}\Psi)^{\mathsf{q}}+|\mathbb{E}F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}(Y^{s})|.

(F.20)

Similar to (
F.18
) and (
F.19
), we can utilize the Taylor expansion and the estimate (
F.16
) to derive that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}^{(\mathsf{k},\widetilde{\mathsf{k}})}(0,0)=\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}^{(\mathsf{k},\widetilde{\mathsf{k}})}\left(\chi^{s}_{j\mu}\mathsf{X}_{j\mu},(1-\chi^{s}_{j\mu})\mathsf{X}_{j\mu}\right)

(F.21)

\displaystyle-s\sum_{l=2}^{4\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{k}-\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}}\frac{1}{l!}\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}^{(\mathsf{k}+l,\widetilde{\mathsf{k}})}(0,0)\mathbb{E}(\mathsf{X}_{j\mu})^{l}-(1-s)\sum_{\widetilde{l}=2}^{4\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{k}-\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}}\frac{1}{\widetilde{l}!}\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}^{(\mathsf{k},\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}+\widetilde{l})}(0,0)\mathbb{E}(\mathsf{X}_{j\mu})^{\widetilde{l}}+\OO_{\prec}(\Psi^{\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{k}-\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}})

for any fixed
\mathsf{k}
and
\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}
. In the derivation, we also used the fact that
\chi^{s}_{j\mu}(1-\chi^{s}_{j\mu})=0
. By repeatedly applying (
F.21
), we can further obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}^{(\ell,0)}(0,0)=\sum_{\mathsf{k},\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}:\mathsf{k}+\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}=0}^{4\mathsf{q}-\ell}C_{(j\mu)}^{\mathsf{k},\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}}\sum_{j,\mu}\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}^{(\ell+\mathsf{k},\widetilde{\mathsf{k}})}\left(\chi^{s}_{j\mu}\mathsf{X}_{j\mu},(1-\chi^{s}_{j\mu})\mathsf{X}_{j\mu}\right)+\OO_{\prec}(\Psi^{\mathsf{q}-\ell}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}^{(0,\ell)}(0,0)=\sum_{\mathsf{k},\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}:\mathsf{k}+\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}=0}^{4\mathsf{q}-\ell}C_{(j\mu)}^{\mathsf{k},\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}}\sum_{j,\mu}\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}^{(\mathsf{k},\ell+\widetilde{\mathsf{k}})}\left(\chi^{s}_{j\mu}\mathsf{X}_{j\mu},(1-\chi^{s}_{j\mu})\mathsf{X}_{j\mu}\right)+\OO_{\prec}(\Psi^{\mathsf{q}-\ell}),

where
C_{(j\mu)}^{\mathsf{k},\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}}
represents deterministic coefficients satisfying that
C_{(j\mu)}^{0,0}=1
,

C_{(j\mu)}^{\mathsf{k},\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}}=\OO(n^{-{(\mathsf{k}+\widetilde{\mathsf{k}})}/2}),\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{k}=1\ \ \text{or}\ \ \widetilde{\mathsf{k}}=1\implies C_{(j\mu)}^{\mathsf{k},\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}}=0.

(F.22)

Now, to conclude (
F.20
), it suffices to control
\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}^{(\ell+\mathsf{k},\widetilde{\mathsf{k}})}\left(\chi^{s}_{j\mu}\mathsf{X}_{j\mu},(1-\chi^{s}_{j\mu})\mathsf{X}_{j\mu}\right)-\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}^{(\mathsf{k},\ell+\widetilde{\mathsf{k}})}\left(\chi^{s}_{j\mu}\mathsf{X}_{j\mu},(1-\chi^{s}_{j\mu})\mathsf{X}_{j\mu}\right)
.
For the terms in (
F.21
), we express them as

\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}^{(\mathsf{k},\widetilde{\mathsf{k}})}\left(\chi^{s}_{j\mu}\mathsf{X}_{j\mu},(1-\chi^{s}_{j\mu})\mathsf{X}_{j\mu}\right)=\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(P^{U^{0}}_{j\mu}\Big)^{\mathsf{k}}\Big(P^{U^{1}}_{j\mu}\Big)^{\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}}F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}\Big]\left(Y^{s}\right),

(F.23)

where we adopt the following notation given any
p\times 2p
matrix
U
:

P^{U}_{j\mu}:=(U^{\top}\nabla_{Y})_{j\mu}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}{U}_{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial Y^{s}_{i\mu}}.

(F.24)

Note that
P^{U}_{j\mu}
acting on any resolvent entry yields

P^{U}_{j\mu}G_{\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{b}}=-G_{\mathfrak{a}{\bf{u}}_{j}}G_{\mu\mathfrak{b}}-G_{\mathfrak{a}\mu}G_{{\bf{u}}_{j}\mathfrak{b}},\quad\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{I},

(F.25)

where
{\bf{u}}_{j}=U\mathbf{e}_{j}
represents the
j
-th column vector of
U
. With the above notations, we observe that to establish (
F.20
), it is sufficient to prove that for any
\ell,

\mathsf{k}
,
\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}
satisfying
\mathsf{k}\neq 1
,
\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}\neq 1
,
\ell\geq 2
, and
\ell+\mathsf{k}+\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}\leq 4\mathsf{q},

\displaystyle n^{-(\mathsf{k}+\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}+\ell)/2}\sum_{j,\mu}\mathcal{C}_{(j\mu)}^{\ell}\mathbb{E}\Big\{\Big[\Big(P^{U^{0}}_{j\mu}\Big)^{\ell+\mathsf{k}}\Big(P^{U^{1}}_{j\mu}\Big)^{\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}}F^{{\bf{v}},\,\mathsf{q}}\Big]\left(Y^{s}\right)-\Big[\Big(P^{U^{0}}_{j\mu}\Big)^{\mathsf{k}}\Big(P^{U^{1}}_{j\mu}\Big)^{\ell+\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}}F^{{\bf{v}},\,\mathsf{q}}\Big]\left(Y^{s}\right)\Big\}

(F.26)

\displaystyle\lesssim(n^{24\delta}\Psi)^{\mathsf{q}}+|\mathbb{E}F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}(Y^{s})|.

Here, we introduce the notation
\mathcal{C}_{(j\mu)}^{\ell}:=n^{\ell/2}\mathbb{E}\left(\mathsf{X}_{j\mu}\right)^{\ell}
, which is of order
\OO(1)
by (
2.14
).
The remaining part of the proof is dedicated to verifying (
F.26
). To accomplish this, we divide the proof into two cases.

Case 1:
\ell+\mathsf{k}+\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}=2.
In this case, we must have
\ell=2,\mathsf{k}=\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}=0
. Using the fact that
U^{0}(U^{0})^{\top}=U^{1}(U^{1})^{\top}=I
, we can easily check that

\sum_{j,\mu}\left(P^{U^{1}}_{j\mu}\right)^{2}F^{{\bf{v}},\,\mathsf{q}}(Y^{s})=\sum_{j,\mu}\left(P^{U^{0}}_{j\mu}\right)^{2}F^{{\bf{v}},\,\mathsf{q}}(Y^{s})=\sum_{i\mu}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial(Y^{s}_{i\mu})^{2}}F^{{\bf{v}},\,\mathsf{q}}(Y^{s}).

This equation clearly implies (
F.26
) when
\ell+\mathsf{k}+\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}=2.

Case 2:
3\leq\ell+\mathsf{k}+\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}\leq 4\mathsf{q}.

We denote
\widetilde{U}=U^{1}-U^{0}.
It is evident that in order to establish (
F.26
), it suffices to prove the following estimate for every
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
satisfying
2\leq k_{1}+k_{2}=\mathsf{k}+\widetilde{\mathsf{k}}+\ell-1\leq 4\mathsf{q}-1
:

\displaystyle n^{-(k_{1}+k_{2}+1)/2}\sum_{j,\mu}\mathcal{C}_{(j\mu)}^{\ell}\mathbb{E}\Big|\Big[\Big(P^{U^{0}}_{j\mu}\Big)^{k_{1}}\Big(P^{U^{1}}_{j\mu}\Big)^{k_{2}}P^{\widetilde{U}}_{j\mu}F^{{\bf{v}},\,\mathsf{q}}\Big]\Bigl({Y^{s}}\Bigr)\Big|\lesssim(n^{24\delta}\Psi)^{\mathsf{q}}+|\mathbb{E}F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}(Y^{s})|.

(F.27)

For the proof of (
F.27
), we need to delve into the detailed structure of the derivatives. To this end, in view of (
F.24
) and (
F.25
), we introduce the following algebraic objects, which are employed in
[
45
,
68
]
.

Definition F.5

(Words)
.

Given
j\in\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mu\in\mathcal{I}_{2}
, let
\mathcal{W}
be the set consisting of words of even length formed from the four letters
\{\mathbf{j}_{0},\mathbf{j}_{1},\widetilde{\mathbf{j}},\bm{\mu}\}
. The length of a word
w\in{\mathcal{W}}
is denoted by
2{\bm{l}}(w)\in 2\mathbb{N}
. We use bold symbols to represent the letters in words. For instance,

w=\mathbf{t}_{1}\mathbf{s}_{2}\mathbf{t}_{2}\mathbf{s}_{3}\cdots\mathbf{t}_{l}\mathbf{s}_{l+1}

(F.28)

denotes a word of length
2l
. Define
{\mathcal{W}}_{l}:=\{w\in\mathcal{W}:{\bm{l}}(w)=l\}
as the set of words of length
2l
such that the following property holds for a word as in (
F.28
):

\mathbf{t}_{i}\mathbf{s}_{i+1}\in\{\mathbf{j}_{0}\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}\mathbf{j}_{0},\mathbf{j}_{1}\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}\mathbf{j}_{1},\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}\},\quad i\in[\![{l}]\!].

Next, we assign a value
[\cdot]
to each letter as follows:
[\mathbf{j}_{0}]={\bf{u}}_{j}^{0}
,
[\mathbf{j}_{1}]={\bf{u}}_{j}^{1}
,
[\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}]=\widetilde{\bf{u}}_{j}
,
[\bm{\mu}]:=\mathbf{e}_{\mu}
, where
{\bf{u}}_{j}^{0}:=U^{0}\mathbf{e}_{j}
,
{\bf{u}}_{j}^{1}:=U^{1}\mathbf{e}_{j}
,
\widetilde{\bf{u}}_{j}:=\widetilde{U}\mathbf{e}_{j}/\|\widetilde{U}\mathbf{e}_{j}\|_{2}
. It is important to distinguish between the abstract letter and its corresponding value, which is treated as a (generalized) summation index. For the word
w
in (
F.28
) and vectors
{\bf{u}},{\bf{v}}\in\mathbb{C}^{p+n}
, we assign a random variable
A_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v},j,\mu}(w)
as follows: if
{\bm{l}}(w)=0
, we define

A_{{\bf{u}},{\bf{v}},j,\mu}(w):=\big(G(Y^{s})-\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}\big)_{{\bf{u}}{\bf{v}}};

if
{\bm{l}}(w)\geq 1
, we define

A_{{\bf{u}},{\bf{v}},j,\mu}(w):=G_{{\bf{u}}[\mathbf{t}_{1}]}(Y^{s})G_{[\mathbf{s}_{2}][\mathbf{t}_{2}]}(Y^{s})\cdots G_{[\mathbf{s}_{l}][\mathbf{t}_{l}]}(Y^{s})G_{[\mathbf{s}_{l+1}]{\bf{v}}}(Y^{s}).

(F.29)

Finally, given any
w\in\mathcal{W}_{l}
of the form (
F.28
), we denote
\mathfrak{n}_{0}(w):=\#\{i\in[\![{l}]\!]:\mathbf{t}_{i}=\mathbf{j}_{0}\ \text{or}\ \mathbf{s}_{i+1}=\mathbf{j}_{0}\}
,
\mathfrak{n}_{1}(w):=\#\{i\in[\![{l}]\!]:\mathbf{t}_{i}=\mathbf{j}_{1}\ \text{or}\ \mathbf{s}_{i+1}=\mathbf{j}_{1}\}
, and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{n}}(w):=\#\{i\in[\![{l}]\!]:\mathbf{t}_{i}=\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}\ \text{or}\ \mathbf{s}_{i+1}=\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}\}
.

From (
F.15
) and (
F.25
), we observe that the above notations are defined such that for every
l\in\mathbb{N}
, the following equality holds:

\Big(P^{U^{0}}_{j\mu}\Big)^{l}\big(G(Y^{s})-\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}\big)_{{\bf{u}}{\bf{v}}}=(-1)^{l}l!\sum_{w\in\mathcal{W}_{l}:\mathfrak{n}_{0}(w)=l}A_{{\bf{u}},{\bf{v}},j,\mu}(w).

A similar equation holds if we replace
0
with
1
. By denoting
\alpha_{j}:=\|\widetilde{U}\mathbf{e}_{j}\|_{2}
, we have that

P^{\widetilde{U}}_{j\mu}\big(G(Y^{s})-\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}\big)_{{\bf{u}}{\bf{v}}}=-\alpha_{j}\sum_{w\in\{\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}\}}A_{{\bf{u}},{\bf{v}},j,\mu}(w).

Using the above two identities, we can derive that

\displaystyle\Big(P^{U^{0}}_{j\mu}\Big)^{k_{1}}\Big(P^{U^{1}}_{j\mu}\Big)^{k_{2}}P^{\widetilde{U}}_{j\mu}F^{{\bf{v}},\,\mathsf{q}}(Y^{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l_{1},\ldots,l_{\mathsf{q}}\geq 0:\\
l_{1}+\cdots+l_{\mathsf{q}}=k_{1}+k_{2}+1\end{subarray}}\sum_{w_{1}\in\mathcal{W}_{l_{1}},\ldots,w_{\mathsf{q}}\in\mathcal{W}_{l_{\mathsf{q}}}}^{*}c(w_{1},\ldots,w_{\mathsf{q}})\alpha_{j}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\mathsf{t}=1}^{\mathsf{q}/2}\left[A_{{\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},j,\mu}(w_{\mathsf{t}})\overline{A_{{\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},j,\mu}(w_{\mathsf{t}+\mathsf{q}/2})}\right].

(F.30)

Here,
c(w_{1},\ldots,w_{\mathsf{q}})
represents certain deterministic coefficients of order
\OO(1)
, and
\sum_{w_{1}\in\mathcal{W}_{l_{1}},\ldots,w_{\mathsf{q}}\in\mathcal{W}_{l_{\mathsf{q}}}}^{*}
denotes the summation over a sequence of words
w_{1},\ldots,w_{\mathsf{q}}
satisfying the following conditions:

\sum_{\mathsf{t}=1}^{\mathsf{q}}\mathfrak{n}_{0}(w_{\mathsf{t}})=k_{1},\quad\sum_{\mathsf{t}=1}^{\mathsf{q}}\mathfrak{n}_{1}(w_{\mathsf{t}})=k_{2},\quad\sum_{\mathsf{t}=1}^{\mathsf{q}}\widetilde{\mathfrak{n}}(w_{\mathsf{t}})=1.

(F.31)

Without loss of generality, suppose there are
\mathsf{p}
non-zero length words in (
F.30
) for some
1\leq\mathsf{p}\leq\mathsf{q}\wedge(k_{1}+k_{2}+1)
, and these words are
w_{1},\ldots,w_{\mathsf{p}}
. Then, we can identify and separate the words of length 0 from the product in (
F.30
), denoted as
w_{0}
. Now, to show (
F.27
), it suffices to prove that for any
1\leq\mathsf{p}\leq\mathsf{q}\wedge(k_{1}+k_{2}+1)
and a sequence of words satisfying (
F.31
), the following estimate holds:

\displaystyle n^{-(k_{1}+k_{2}+1)/2}\sum_{j,\mu}\alpha_{j}\mathbb{E}\left|A_{{\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},j,\mu}(w_{0})^{\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{p}}\prod_{r=1}^{\mathsf{p}}A_{{\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},j,\mu}(w_{r})\right|\lesssim(n^{24\delta}\Psi)^{\mathsf{q}}+|\mathbb{E}F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}(Y^{s})|.

(F.32)

The proof of (
F.32
) follows a similar approach to that of (6.32) in
[
68
]
.
As we assume
\mathbb{E}\mathsf{X}^{3}_{i\mu}=0
according to (ii) of
Assumption

2.3
, we only need to consider the case when
\ell\geqslant 4
. (It is in this particular case that the vanishing third-moment condition is relevant and utilized.)
For
j\in\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mu\in\mathcal{I}_{2}
, we define

\mathcal{R}_{j}:=\big|G_{{\bf{v}}{\bf{u}}^{0}_{j}}\big|+\big|G^{s}_{{\bf{v}}{\bf{u}}^{1}_{j}}\big|+\big|G_{{\bf{v}}_{\alpha}\widetilde{\bf{u}}_{j}}\big|,\quad\mathcal{R}_{\mu}:=\big|G_{{\bf{v}}\mu}\big|.

Similar to Lemma 6.16 in
[
68
]
, we can establish the following rough bound using property (
\mathbf{A}_{d-1}
):

|A_{{\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},i,\mu}(w)|\prec n^{2\delta({\bm{l}}(w)+1)},

Additionally, we can get the following bounds: for
{\bm{l}}(w)\geq 2
,

|A_{{\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},i,\mu}(w)|\prec(\mathcal{R}_{i}^{2}+\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{2})n^{2\delta({\bm{l}}(w)-1)},

and for
{\bm{l}}(w)=1
,

|A_{{\bf{v}},{\bf{v}},i,\mu}(w)|\prec\mathcal{R}_{i}\mathcal{R}_{\mu}.

With these estimates, we can follow the arguments presented between (6.39) and (6.42) in
[
68
]
to complete the proof of (
F.32
). (It is worth noting that for this proof, the condition (
4.35
), which implies
\alpha_{j}\leq Cn^{-\mathsf{c}^{\prime}}
, is
not
necessary.)
Since the argument is almost the same, we omit the details here. This concludes the proof of
Lemma

F.4
.
∎

With the above results, we proceed to complete the proof of Lemma
4.12
by using the following lemma.

Lemma F.6
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
4.12
, the following estimate holds for every
s\in[0,1]
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\theta\left(\mathcal{U}_{i_{1}}(\mathsf{Q}_{t}^{s}),\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{i_{L}}(\mathsf{Q}_{t}^{s})\right)=\mathbb{E}\theta\left(\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{i_{1}}(\mathsf{Q}_{t}^{s}),\ldots,\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{i_{L}}(\mathsf{Q}_{t}^{s})\right)+\OO(n^{-\nu}),

for a constant
\nu>0
, where
\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{k}(\mathsf{Q}_{t}^{s})
,
k\in[\![{\mathsf{K}-r}]\!],
are defined as

\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{k}(\mathsf{Q}_{t}^{s}):=\frac{p}{\pi}\int_{I_{k}}\left[\im x_{k}^{s}(E)\right]\mathfrak{q}_{k}\left(y^{s}_{k}(E)\right)\mathrm{d}E

Here, the functions
y^{s}_{k}(E)
,
k\in[\![{\mathsf{K}-r}]\!],
are defined as

\displaystyle y^{s}_{i_{k}}(E)

\displaystyle\;\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\;\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{i}\sigma\mathfrak{f}_{i_{k}}^{\prime\prime}(e)\chi(\sigma)\tr\mathcal{G}_{2,t}^{s}(e+\mathrm{i}\sigma)\,\bm{\mathrm{1}}\bigl({\lvert\sigma\rvert\geqslant\tilde{\eta}_{k}n^{-C{\varepsilon}}}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}e\,\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle\qquad\;+\;\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bigl({\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{f}_{i_{k}}(e)\chi^{\prime}(\sigma)-\sigma\mathfrak{f}_{i_{k}}^{\prime}(e)\chi^{\prime}(\sigma)}\bigr)\tr\mathcal{G}_{2,t}^{s}(e+\mathrm{i}\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}e\,\mathrm{d}\sigma\,,

(F.33)

where
\widetilde{\eta}_{k}
is defined in (
4.30
),
C>0
is an absolute constant, and
\chi
is a smooth cutoff function with support in
[-1,1]
, satisfying
\chi(\sigma)=1
for
|\sigma|\leq 1/2
, and having bounded derivatives up to arbitrarily high order.

Proof.

The proof follows the same lines of the arguments presented between (5.9) and (5.11) in
[
44
]
, utilizing Lemma
F.4
and the Helffer-Sjöstrand formula (see
[
8
, Proposition 1.13.4]
). Further details are omitted.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.12
.

For
s\in[0,1]
, we introduce the function

F(Y^{s})\equiv F(\mathsf{Q}^{s}_{t}):=\theta\left(\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{i_{1}}(\mathsf{Q}_{t}^{s}),\ldots,\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{i_{L}}(\mathsf{Q}_{t}^{s})\right).

(F.34)

According to Lemma
F.6
, it suffices to show that

\mathbb{E}F(Y^{1})-\mathbb{E}F(Y^{0})=\OO(n^{-\nu})

for a constant
\nu>0
. Similar to (
F.12
), we have

\mathbb{E}F(Y^{1})-\mathbb{E}F(Y^{0})=\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s\sum_{j,\mu}\Bigl[{\mathbb{E}F\Bigl({Y^{s,Z^{1}_{(j\mu)}}_{(j\mu)}}\Bigr)-\mathbb{E}F\Bigl({Y^{s,Z^{0}_{(j\mu)}}_{(j\mu)}}\Bigr)}\Bigr].

Hence, it remains to prove

\sum_{j,\mu}\Bigl[{\mathbb{E}F\Bigl({Y^{s,Z^{1}_{(j\mu)}}_{(j\mu)}}\Bigr)-\mathbb{E}F\Bigl({Y^{s,Z^{0}_{(j\mu)}}_{(j\mu)}}\Bigr)}\Bigr]=\OO(n^{-\nu}),\quad s\in[0,1].

(F.35)

The proof of this estimate is similar to the above proof of
Lemma

F.4
. In fact, the proof here is slightly easier since we have the local law (
F.5
) at hand, and there is no need to use an induction argument based on the two scale-dependent properties
(
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{d}
)
and
(
\bm{\mathrm{C}}_{d}
)
. Furthermore, by employing the argument presented in the proof of
[
35
, Lemma 15.5]
, we can deduce the following estimate for
G(Y^{s})
from
Lemma

F.4
when the value of
\eta
is less than
n^{-1}
: for any constant
\sigma>0
and deterministic unit vectors
{\bf{u}},{\bf{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p+n}
,

\sup_{\eta\geq n^{-1-\sigma}}\sup_{\lambda_{-}-c\leq E\leq\lambda_{+}+c}\big|(G_{t}^{s}-\widetilde{\Pi}_{t})_{{\bf{u}}{\bf{v}}}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)\big|\prec n^{\sigma}.

(F.36)

Notice that as
F^{{\bf{v}},\mathsf{q}}
in (
F.11
),
F
is also a function of the resolvent
G(Y^{s})
. Similar to (
F.17
), we abbreviate

f_{(j\mu)}(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=F\left(Y^{s,\lambda_{0}U^{0}{\bf e}_{j\mu}+\lambda_{1}U^{1}{\bf e}_{j\mu}}_{(j\mu)}\right)\,,

(F.37)

To analyze this further, we perform Taylor expansions of
f_{(j\mu)}(\mathsf{X}_{j\mu},0)
and
f_{(j\mu)}(0,\mathsf{X}_{j\mu})
around
f_{(j\mu)}(0,0)
. Then, we compare the two Taylor expansions and estimate their differences using the resolvent expansion (
F.15
), the derivatives as in (
F.25
), and the local laws (
F.5
) and (
F.36
). Following the argument below (
F.17
) (or the argument in
[
35
, Section 16]
), we can derive an estimate similar to (
F.32
):

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}(\mathsf{X}_{j\mu},0)-\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}(0,\mathsf{X}_{j\mu})\right|\lesssim n^{-2}\sum_{j,\mu}\alpha_{j}\mathcal{E}_{j\mu}+n^{-1}.

(F.38)

Here,
\mathcal{E}_{j\mu}
are positive variables of order
\OO(n^{C{\varepsilon}})
, where
C>0
is an absolute constant that does no depend on
{\varepsilon}
. In the derivation, we used the vanishing third-moment condition, and the factor
n^{-2}
arises from the fourth or higher-order moments of
\mathsf{X}_{j\mu}
. The factor
\alpha_{j}
has the same origin as that in the LHS of (
F.32
). We omit the details here. Since
\alpha_{j}\leq Cn^{-\mathsf{c}^{\prime}}
by the condition (
4.35
), we deduce from (
F.38
) that

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}(\mathsf{X}_{j\mu},0)-\mathbb{E}f_{(j\mu)}(0,\mathsf{X}_{j\mu})\right|\lesssim n^{-\mathsf{c}^{\prime}+C{\varepsilon}}.

This concludes the proof by choosing
{\varepsilon}
sufficiently small, depending on
\mathsf{c}^{\prime}
and
C
.
∎
